Analytic and stochastic description of Brownian motions on star graphs
Adam Bobrowski, Andrey Pilipenko

Abstract
We provide a detailed description of all possible Feller processes on infinite star graphs with finite number of edges, processes that while away from the graph’s center behave like a one-dimensional Brownian motion. The description can be seen as a continuation of the seminal paper [ 38 ] by Itô and McKean (devoted to Brownian motions on the half-line), recast from the perspective of the theory of multi-armed bandits.



Version of August 10, 2026

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
47D06,47D07, 60J60, 60J50, 60J35

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Boundary conditions of Feller and Wentzell

Analytic description of boundary conditions for diffusions is owed to the independent work of W. Feller
[
26
,
28
]
and A.D. Wentzell (sometimes also spelled Ventzel’)
[
64
]
. In the case of processes with values in the positive half-line the gist of their findings is as follows.

Let
[0,\infty]
be the compactified positive half-line, and let
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
be the space of continuous functions on
this half-line;
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
can be equivalently characterized as the space of continuous functions
f\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that a finite limit
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)
exists. Suppose that
\mathfrak{G}
is a Feller generator in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
such that its domain is contained in the set of functions that are differentiable at
x=0
. Then
there are non-negative constants
\alpha,\beta,
and
\gamma
, and a (possibly infinite) positive Borel measure
\mathpzc m
on
(0,\infty]
such that

\int_{(0,\infty]}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
and

\alpha\mathfrak{G}f(0)-\beta f^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(0)=\int[f(x)-f(0)]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}),

where
\alpha+\beta>0
or
\mathpzc m(0,\infty]=\infty
.

The case of the one-dimensional Laplace operator
defined by

\Delta f=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\Delta)\coloneqq\mathfrak{C}^{2}[0,\infty]

is by far the most interesting and important.
Here,
\mathfrak{C}^{2}[0,\infty]
is the subspace of
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
composed of twice continuously differentiable
f\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}

(at
x=0
we consider right-hand derivatives; alternatively,
f^{\prime}(0)
and
f^{\prime\prime}(0)
are
defined as
\lim_{x\to 0+}f^{\prime}(x)
and
\lim_{x\to 0+}f^{\prime\prime}(x)
, respectively)
such that
f^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
, so that in particular the limit
\lim_{x\to\infty}f^{\prime\prime}(x)
exists and is finite. In this case, Feller and Wentzell’s work can be summarized by stating that if a Feller generator
\mathfrak{G}
is a restriction of
\Delta
, then there are non-negative constants
\alpha,\beta,
and
\gamma
, and a (possibly infinite) positive Borel measure
\mathpzc m
on
(0,\infty)
such that

\int_{0}^{\infty}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
and

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0)-\beta f^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(0)=\int[f(x)-f(0)]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G});

(1.1)

again, at least one of conditions
\alpha+\beta>0
and
\mathpzc m(0,\infty]=\infty
holds. In fact, since the point at infinity is added merely for convenience, so that we can work in a compact space, and a Brownian motion cannot reach this point in a finite time anyway, we can exclude the possibility of
\mathpzc m
having a mass there and can thus assume that
\mathpzc m
is a Borel measure on
(0,\infty)
. Conversely, any restriction of
\Delta
to the domain of the form visible in (
1.1
) (with
\mathpzc m
having no mass at
\infty
) is a Feller generator (see e.g.
[
11
]
).

A number of remarks are here in order. First of all, it has been amply clear from the very beginning that, whereas the operator
\Delta
describes the behavior of the related process
inside
the half-line, condition (
1.1
)
determines its behavior at the boundary, that is, at
x=0
. Secondly, as stressed by Itô and McKean
[
39
]
*p. 187, before Feller and Wentzell, it had not been noticed that conditions involving
\mathfrak{G}f(0)
were possible. Feller himself, alluding to this new term, acknowledges that his inspirations were in part coming from genetics and writes in
[
26
]
*p. 474: “Various probability considerations and a long outstanding problem in genetics made it increasingly clear that in addition to the classical types of boundary conditions there exist some of a new type. Physically speaking, there exist diffusion processes where a particle can be absorbed, and stay for a finite time, after which it penetrates slowly back into the interior.”

1.2.
Stochastic interpretation

As already mentioned, the boundary condition (
1.1
) characterizes behavior of the related process at the boundary
x=0
. Several examples, studied throughout the literature, can be singled out here.

1.

Brownian motion
stopped
at
0
(or:
absorbed
at
0
, see e.g.
[
44
]
*p. 41 and p. 61). In other words, if
B=\{B(t),t\geq 0\}
is a standard Brownian motion starting at
B(0)=x\geq 0
, and
\sigma\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon B(t)=0\}
is the moment when
B
reaches
0
for the first time, then
B^{\textnormal{stop}}(t)\coloneqq B(t\wedge\sigma),t\geq 0
. This process is characterized by the boundary condition

f^{\prime\prime}(0)=0

corresponding to
\beta=\gamma=0
,
\mathpzc m=0
and
\alpha>0
in (
1.1
).

2.

The
minimal
Brownian motion. This is the process which is undefined after it reaches
0
for the first time:
B^{\textnormal{min}}(t)=B(t)
whenever
t<\sigma
and undefined for
t\geq\sigma
. This corresponds to the boundary condition

f(0)=0

a particular case of (
1.1
) with
\alpha=\beta=0
,
\mathpzc m=0
and
\gamma>0
. We stress that, strictly speaking, this is not a process in
[0,\infty]
but rather in
(0,\infty]
, as
0
does not belong to the state-space. This is reflected in the fact that the boundary condition presented above does not describe a dense subset of
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
, and thus
\Delta
with this domain is not a Feller generator in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
. However, it is a Feller generator in
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]
, the subspace of
f\in\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
such that
f(0)=0
.

3.

Brownian motion
killed
at
0
(see e.g.
[
44
]
*p. 71). In this process the Brownian traveller is killed upon reaching
0
for the first time and put at a graveyard, where it stays forever. It will be convenient to think that the graveyard is
\infty
; then
B^{\textnormal{kill}}(t)=B(t)
for
t<\sigma
and
B^{\textnormal{kill}}(t)=\infty
for
t\geq\sigma
. This is an honest process, but, somewhat similarly as in Point 2.,
0
is not in its state-space, for the process leaves this point immediately to jump to
\infty
. This forces us to identify
0
and
\infty
, and so the corresponding semigroup acts in the subspace of
f\in\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
such that
f(0)=f(\infty)
. However, other authors, including Blumenthal
[
11
]
*p. 56, use the adjective
killed
to describe the not honest process that is undefined after hitting
0
for the first time, and thus do not distinguish between the minimal and the killed process. We will follow their example.

4.

Reflected
, or
reflecting
, Brownian motion. This, by definition, is the process
\{|B(t)|,t\geq 0\}
. It is characterized by the boundary condition (
1.1
) with
\alpha=\gamma=0,\mathpzc m=0
and
\beta>0
, that is,

f^{\prime}(0)=0.

5.

Brownian motion with
slowly reflecting boundary

[
57
]
*p. 421, known also as
sticky
Brownian motion
[
50
]
*p. 127 (in
[
24
]
it is called
sticky reflecting
) can be seen as a process lying between the reflected and stopped Brownian motions. Its boundary condition has the following form:

\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(0)=\beta f^{\prime}(0);

for
\alpha=0
and non-zero
\beta
this reduces to the reflected Brownian motion; for
\beta=0
and non-zero
\alpha
this becomes the stopped Brownian motion. Intuitively, this Brownian motion is partly reflected and partly stopped at
0
.

6.

The
elastic
Brownian motion (see e.g.
[
39
]
*p. 45), in turn, lies between the reflected and the minimal Brownian motions, and is characterized by the following boundary condition:

\beta f^{\prime}(0)=\gamma f(0).

At the extreme case
\beta=0
this is the minimal Brownian motion. For
\beta>0
it is best described in terms of the Brownian motion’s local time at
0
(see also the next section). To wit, when the local time exceeds an independent, exponentially distributed random variable with parameter
\frac{\gamma}{\beta}
, the process is left undefined, but until this happens it is indistinguishable from the reflected Brownian motion.

7.

Elementary return
Brownian motion. In this process, discussed perhaps for the first time in
[
27
]
*p. 3, a particle reaching
x=0
stays there for an exponential time with parameter
\alpha
and then with probability
\delta\in[0,1]
jumps back to a random point of
(0,\infty)
distributed according to a Borel probability measure
\mu
on
(0,\infty)
; with probability
1-\delta
the process is no longer defined. The boundary condition in this case reads

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0)+(1-\delta)f(0)=\delta\int[f-f(0)]\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

As a side remark, we note here an important, if obvious, feature of exemplary processes generated by the Laplace operator
\Delta
with boundary conditions (
1.1
): all of them are shown above to be in a more or less direct way constructed from the standard Brownian motion (this thought will be made even more precise in the next section). It is engaging that a similar effect can be observed from the operator theory perspective. To explain this, recall first that the standard Brownian motion semigroup, say,
T
, is given by

T(t)f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}f(B(t)),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0

(1.2)

where
\mathsf{E}_{x}
denotes expectation conditional on
B
starting at
x
, and
f
is, say, a member of the space
\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
, where
[-\infty,\infty]
is a two-point compactification of the real line. This semigroup turns out to have a number of invariant subspaces that are isomorphic to
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
. This allows considering
images in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
of restrictions of
T
to these subspaces, and some of them turn out to describe the processes characterized by the boundary condition (
1.1
); in particular, all processes corresponding to (
1.1
) with
\mathpzc m=0
are of this form. See
[
16
,
19
]
for details; see also Section
2.2
.

1.3.
Stochastic construction

Points 1. through 6. of Section
1.2
make it intuitively clear that coefficients
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
of (
1.1
) measure the intensities with which, in the related process, a particle touching
x=0
is
stopped, reflected and/or removed, respectively. Moreover, point 7. suggests that the measure
\mathpzc m
tells of the way in which the particles jump from the boundary to the interior. It is relations between these parameters that dictate in detail the behavior of the process.

However, such a description, though pleasing, is far from being
satisfactory; there is a need for a more intrinsic, more intricate and more thorough, perhaps pathwise, picture of the processes involved. Such a picture was provided in the beautiful and insightful paper
[
38
]
by Itô and McKean in 1963. To paint this picture here again, however, we first need to discuss two important notions: that of a symmetric Lévy local time for a Brownian motion (see Section
1.3.1
)
and that of a subordinator (see Section
1.3.2
). The theorem of Itô and McKean will be presented in Section
1.3.3
.

1.3.1.
Lévy local time and Lévy theorem

We recall that the symmetric Lévy local time
L_{0}
of a Brownian motion
B
, a stochastic process with values in
[0,\infty)
in itself, is defined in one of a number of equivalent ways (see e.g.
[
39
]
*pp. 42–45,
[
41
]
*pp. 201–217 and Ch. 6,
[
49
]
or
[
53
]
*pp. 31–39) as the following almost sure limit

L_{0}(t)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{|B(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

(1.3)

and it is clear from this definition that
L_{0}
is at the same time the local time of
|B|
. A surprising yet fundamental theorem of Lévy states that the distributions of
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{2}
-valued processes
(|B(t)|,L_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t),L(t))_{t\geq 0}
coincide:

(|B(t)|,L_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}\overset{d}{=}(B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t),L(t))_{t\geq 0},

(1.4)

where (cf. Section
2.3
)

L(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}(-B(s)\vee 0)\quad\text{and}\quad B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)\coloneqq B(t)+L(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

(1.5)

In particular,
B^{\textnormal{ref}}
is a (more manageable) version of
|B|
, and
L
is the local time for
B^{\textnormal{ref}}
. Furthermore,
each path of
B^{\textnormal{ref}}
is the solution to the Skorokhod problem discussed in Section
2.3
.

1.3.2.
Subordinators

Subordinators are Lévy processes that start from
0
and have nondecreasing paths in
[0,\infty)

[
7
]
. As noted in
[
7
]
*p. 71 ‘The terminology comes from the fact that when one time-changes a Markov process
M
by an independent subordinator
U
, the resulting process
M\circ U
is again Markov’, see
[
29
]
*Sec. X.7 for details.

These special properties of subordinators are reflected in the fact that their characteristic exponents (the exponents in the Lévy–Khintchine formula) have simpler form. First of all, the Gaussian coefficient of a subordinator is
0
, its drift coefficients is nonnegative, and its Lévy measure is more regular close to
0
. The last statement means that, as in (
1.1
),
\int_{0}^{\infty}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
.
(As we shall see soon this affinity to (
1.1
) is not accidental at all.) Importantly, to describe subordinators one can work with Laplace transform instead of the Fourier transform: a subordinator
U=\{U(t),t\geq 0\}
is characterized by

\mathsf{E}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda U(t)}=\mathrm{e}^{-t\Phi(\lambda)},\qquad\lambda\geq 0,t\geq 0

(1.6)

where
\Phi
, termed the
Laplace exponent
or
cumulant
, is of the form

\Phi(\lambda)=\beta+\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda x})\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad\lambda\geq 0,

with nonnegative drift coefficient
\beta
and the Lévy measure
\mathpzc m
described earlier.

1.3.3.
The theorem of Itô and McKean

Let
|B|
be a reflected Brownian motion and let
L_{0}
be its symmetric local time and
0
. Moreover, let
U
be a subordinator, independent of
|B|
, satisfying at least one of the conditions

\beta>0\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathpzc m(0,\infty)=0.

Such a subordinator has strictly increasing paths and thus it is possible to define these paths’ generalized inverses
U^{-1}
, as presented in Section
2.3
. As the following result of
[
38
]
shows, the process
U\circ U^{-1}\circ L_{0}
is of fundamental importance in describing jumps of the Brownian motion related to (
1.1
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(U\circ U^{-1}\circ L_{0}-L_{0}+|B|)\circ A^{-1}(t),&\text{ if }t<\sigma_{\infty},\\
\text{undefined},&\text{ if }t\geq\sigma_{\infty},\end{cases}

(1.7)

where

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq t+\alpha\theta(t),\quad t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\coloneqq U^{-1}\circ L_{0},

and the distribution of
\sigma_{\infty}
is specified by

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}(\sigma_{\infty}>t|X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma\theta(t)},\qquad t\geq 0.

(1.8)

Then
X=\{X(t),t\geq 0\}
is a Markov process, and its generator is the Laplace operator
\Delta
restricted to the domain where
the boundary condition (
1.1
) is satisfied.

1.4.
The goal of the paper

\mathfrak{0}

Figure 1.
Infinite star graph
K_{1,{\mathpzc k}}
with
{\mathpzc k}=9
edges and graph center
\mathfrak{0}
.

The goal of our paper is to provide both
analytic
and
stochastic
description of all possible Brownian motions on star graphs, a description that would be analogous to that we owe to Feller, Wentzell, Itô and McKean, as detailed in Sections
1.1
–
1.3
. In our use here, the adjective
analytic
refers to description of generators and/or resolvents of Feller semigroups
associated with the processes of interest, and the
stochastic
— to description of their distributions and paths. Put otherwise: the paper presents a blend of analytic and stochastic reasonings that give a thorough picture of Laplace-operator generated processes on star graphs. The main idea is that these processes can to much extend be described by similar means as those generated by the Laplace operator on the half-line.

The analytic approach
can be summarized in the following steps.

1.

In the case of
\alpha+\beta>0
,
\gamma=0
and
\mathpzc m=0
, the boundary condition (
1.1
) corresponds to a mixture of reflected and stopped Brownian motions, that is, to the slowly reflecting Brownian motion a.k.a. sticky Brownian motion. A counterpart of this boundary condition for processes on star graph
corresponds to the classical Walsh’s spider process and its version with sticky graph’s center (see Section
4
). In both scenarios, the resolvent can be calculated by elementary means.

2.

Introducing
\gamma>0
makes the process of Point 1. not honest: it is no longer defined when its local time at
x=0
exceeds an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma
. Up to that random time, the process does not differ from the one described in Point 1 (see Section
5
). As in the previous case, the related resolvent can be calculated by elementary means.

3.

If, in the set-up of Point 1.,
\mathpzc m
is no longer zero but finite, the related process, weather on the half-line or on the graph, can be seen as a continuation of that of Point 2., and
behaves as follows. When the local time at
x=0
exceeds an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\delta\coloneqq\|\mathpzc m\|
the process restarts at a random point distributed according to the probability measure
\delta^{-1}\mathpzc m
. If, additionally,
\gamma>0
, the process restarts with probability
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
and is left undefined with probability
\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+\delta}
(see Section
6
). Its resolvent can be expressed in terms of
\mathpzc m
and the resolvent of the process from Point 2. with
\gamma
replaced by
\delta
.

4.

The resolvent and the semigroup related to (
1.1
) with
\alpha+\beta>0
and infinite
\mathpzc m
, can be obtained as a limit of resolvents and semigroups of the type of Point 3. as follows. We let
\delta(\varepsilon)\coloneqq\mathpzc m(\varepsilon,\infty)
and
\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}
to be
\mathpzc m
restricted to
(\varepsilon,\infty)
and normalized by
\delta(\varepsilon)
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the resolvents and semigroups corresponding to such
\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}
and
\delta_{\varepsilon}
converge to the resolvent corresponding to the original
\mathpzc m
. The same is true for the counterparts of these objects on the graphs. See Section
7
. Notably, in this approximation,
\delta(\varepsilon)
, denoting intensity of jumps, increases to infinity, and the probability measures
\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}
converge weakly to the Dirac measure at
0
.

5.

The resolvent and the semigroup related to (
1.1
) with
\alpha+\beta=0
and infinite
\mathpzc m
, can be approximated similarly as in Point 4., but here the intensity of jumps grows to infinity yet faster. Again, the same is true for the counterparts of these objects on the graphs.

The stochastic approach
proceeds along an analogous, if unique, line. It also starts with simpler processes corresponding to the case where
\mathpzc m
is zero or at least finite, and is later able to describe those more complex by means of approximation. However, in our estimation, it reaches deeper than the analytic one, as it provides
a more detailed picture of the processes of interest. For example, the analytic approach, as detailed in Points 4. and 5. above, suggest that in the case of truly infinite
\mathpzc m
, jumps from the boundary may be infinitely small and occur infinitely often in finite intervals. The analytic approach not only confirms and elucidates this intuition, but provides explicit formulae for these processes.

An obstacle to overcome in the stochastic analysis is the fact that, unfortunately, limits involving formulae akin to (
1.7
) are not easy to handle. This is because the local time featured in it is a discontinuous functional of the paths. Therefore, rather than (
1.7
) we will use counterparts of
its alternative version. It reads

X(t)=\begin{cases}(B+U\circ U^{-1}\circ L)\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),&\text{ if }U^{-1}\circ{L}\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)<\zeta,\\
\text{undefined},&\text{ if }U^{-1}\circ{L}\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)\geq\zeta,\end{cases}

(1.9)

where

(a)

\zeta
is an exponentially distributed random variable with parameter
\gamma
, independent of the already introduced
B
and
U
and
L
is the running maximum of
-B
(see (
1.5
)),

(b)

A_{\alpha}(t)\coloneqq t+\alpha L(t),t\geq 0
is strictly increasing and continuous, and

(c)

A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is its inverse.

Using (
1.4
), one can see that the processes defined in (
1.7
) and (
1.9
) have the same distribution. The advantage of the latter is that
it represents
X
as a function of
B,U,L
and
\zeta
, the processes and the random variable that are easier to handle. In particular, instead of involved
L_{0}
, we can work with the running maximum
L
, that depends on paths in a continuous way. Moreover, a somewhat blurred picture of (
1.8
) is now replaced by the role played by
\zeta
.

We hasten to add that (
1.9
) was known to Itô and McKean, see
[
38
, §12]
.
However, they tended to focus on (
1.7
) instead because this formula allowed them to use excursion theory to study the processes of interest and calculate their resolvents. Our final remark here is that
L_{0}^{X}
featured in (
1.9
) and defined by
L_{0}^{X}(t)\coloneqq U^{-1}\circ{L}\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)
is a local time of
X
at 0 — see
[
11
, p. 69]
, cf.
[
38
, §14]
.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

The main body of the paper, that is, Sections
3
–
8
, are preceded by preliminaries in Section
2
, where we recall information on Feller semigroups, cosine families, and Skorokhod reflection problems.
In Section
3
the general Feller–Wentzel boundary conditions for the Laplace operator on star graph are discussed (see relation (
3.2
)).
The fundamental Section
4
is devoted to Walsh’s spider process. Section
5
in turn describes more general processes with continuous paths, that is, the processes that can be slowed down at the graph center and/or killed when their local time at the graph center reaches the level of an independent exponentially distributed random variable — they are characterized by the fact that the measure
\mathpzc m
in (
3.2
) is zero.
Next, in Section
6
, we commence the analysis of processes with discontinuous paths: after ‘a sufficient number of reflections’ such processes can restart in the interior of the state-space by a jump — in the related boundary condition the measure
\mathpzc m
is nonzero but finite. Sections
7
and
8
are devoted to the case where these jumps are infinitesimally small but occur infinitely often in finite intervals; this is the case of infinite
\mathpzc m
.
The most important results of the paper are summarized in Section
11
. This synopsis is preceded by Sections
9
and
10
where in particular the
\lambda
-potential of the local time is calculated, and an explicit formula for the time-change processes is given.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Feller semigroups

Let
S
be a compact metric space, and
\mathfrak{C}(S)
be the space of continuous functions on
S
. A Feller semigroup (see e.g.
[
5
,
15
,
40
]
)
is a strongly continuous family of positive contraction operators
\left(T(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathfrak{C}(S)
such that
T(0)=I_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}}
(the identity operator) and

T(t)T(s)=T(t+s)\qquad s,t\geq 0.

Because of the Riesz representation theorem, for each
x\in S
and
t\geq 0
there is a Borel measure
\textsf{m}_{x,t}
on
S
such that
\textsf{m}_{x,t}(S)\leq 1
and

T(t)f(x)=\int_{S}f\,\mathrm{d}\textsf{m}_{x,t},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$};

(2.1)

if
\textsf{m}_{x,t}(S)=1
for all
x
and
t
, the process is said to be conservative or honest.

It is well-known that with each Feller semigroup one can associate a Markov process
\left(X(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
with càdlàg paths
[
25
,
40
,
53
,
58
]
(i.e., paths that are right-continuous and possess left limits) in such a way that

T(t)f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}f(X(t))\mathsf{1}_{\{t<\tau\}},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},t\geq 0,x\in S

(2.2)

where
\mathsf{E}_{x}
denotes expectation conditional on
X(0)=x
and
\tau
is the lifetime of the process, that is,
\tau=\tau(\omega)
is the random time up to which the path
X(t,\omega)
is defined. Comparing Eqs. (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) we see that
\textsf{m}_{x,t}(\Gamma),
for a Borel set
\Gamma\subset S
should be interpreted as the probability that
\left(X(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
starting at
x
at time
0
will be in
\Gamma
at a time
t\geq 0
. The measures
\textsf{m}_{x,t}
, together with the Markov property (which translates into Chapman–Kolmogorov equations), determine finite-dimensional distributions of
\left(X(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
. If the distribution of
X(0)
is specified, these finite-dimensional distributions, in turn, when combined with the requirement that the paths are càdlàg,
determine the distribution of
\left(X(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
in the Skorokhod space
D([0,\infty),S)
.

This statement on uniqueness of the process, however, comes with a warning. The fact that for a certain process
\left(Y(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
and a Feller semigroup we have

T(t)f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}f(Y(t))\mathsf{1}_{\{t<\tau\}},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},t\geq 0,x\in S

neither implies that
\left(Y(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
coincides with
\left(X(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
nor that
\left(Y(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
has Markov property.

To elaborate on this succinct and a bit ambiguous statement, let
S\coloneqq[-\infty,\infty]
be the two-point compactification of the real line, and
Y
with values in
S
be defined as follows. Let
\xi
be a standard normal variable, and let
Y(t)\coloneqq x+\sqrt{t}\xi,t\geq 0
provided that we start from
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
Y(t)=\pm\infty,t\geq 0
provided that we start at
\pm\infty
. This process is manifestly not Markov because to determine its future behavior it does not suffice to know its present state, but rather its entire past. Nevertheless, with a good amount of good will, one can think of

\mathsf{E}_{x}f(Y(t))\coloneqq\mathsf{E}f(x+\sqrt{t}\xi)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathsf{E}_{\pm\infty}f(Y(t))=f(\pm\infty),t\geq 0,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
, to see that
\mathsf{E}_{x}f(Y(t))
coincides with
\mathsf{E}f(x+B(t))
, where
\left(B(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is the standard one-dimensional Brownian motion. Our point is that, despite this fact,
\left(Y(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
and
\left(B(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
are clearly different.

To proceed, Feller semigroups are conveniently described by their generators. The generator
\mathfrak{G}
of a Feller semigroup is defined by

\mathfrak{G}f=\lim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}(T(t)f-f)

on the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
composed of
f
such that the above strong limit exists; that is, the right-hand side converges to
\mathfrak{G}f
uniformly on
S
. It is well-known that the generator
\mathfrak{G}
characterizes the semigroup uniquely; in particular, different semigroups have different generators. Moreover, an operator
\mathfrak{G}
is the generator of a Feller semigroup (shortly: a Feller generator) in
\mathfrak{C}(S)
iff the following three conditions are met

1.

\mathfrak{G}
is densely defined,

2.

\mathfrak{G}
satisfies the positive-maximum principle,

3.

\mathfrak{G}
satisfies the range condition: for any
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
and
\lambda>0
there is an
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
such that
\lambda f-\mathfrak{G}f=g.

The semigroup generated by
\mathfrak{G}
will in what follows be denoted
\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\mathfrak{G}}\right)_{t\geq 0}.

In defining Feller semigroups it is customary to require also that the related process is honest or conservative (which comes down to the requirement that
\mathsf{1}_{S}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
and
\mathfrak{G}\mathsf{1}_{S}=0
) but in this paper we will allow the process to be not honest. It is easy to see that the process is honest iff
T(t)\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S}
for all
t\geq 0
and this holds iff
\tau
of equation (
2.1
), that is, the lifetime of the process, is a.s. equal to
\infty
. A common way of making a process honest comes down to adjoining an additional, isolated point, called graveyard, coffin state or cemetery, and commanding the process to stay there for all
t\geq\tau
. We note that this is done at the cost of changing the original Banach space to the space of continuous functions on the enlarged state-space.

An alternative description of a Feller semigroup is provided via Feller resolvents. A family
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
of non-negative operators in
\mathfrak{C}(S)
is said to be a Feller resolvent if the following conditions are met:

1.

the Hilbert equation holds:

(\lambda-\nu)R_{\nu}R_{\lambda}=R_{\nu}-R_{\lambda},\qquad\lambda,\nu>0,

(2.3)

2.

for each
f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}f=f,

3.

\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}\leq\mathsf{1}_{S}
for all
\lambda>0
.

It may be argued (see the already cited references) that for each Feller resolvent there is a Feller generator
\mathfrak{G}
such that
R_{\lambda}=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}\right)^{-1}
, and thus also the related Feller semigroup. Moreover, the related process is conservative iff
\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S}
for all
\lambda>0
.

A word about terminology: a family
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
of bounded operators is said to be a pseudoresolvent, if it satisfies the Hilbert equation. This family is said to be regular, if additionally condition 2. given above holds.

We note also the following crucial relation between the generator, the resolvent and the semigroup:

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}\right)^{-1}f(x)=R_{\lambda}f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}g(X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad x\in S,\lambda>0,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

2.2.
Cosine families

A strongly continuous family
\{C(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
of operators in a Banach space
\mathsf{F}
is said to be a cosine family iff
C(0)
is the identity operator and

2C(t)\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}=C(s+t)+C(t-s),\qquad t,s\in\mathbb{R}.

The generator of such a family is defined by

\mathfrak{G}f=\lim_{t\to 0}2t^{-2}{(C(t)f-f)}

for all
f\in\mathsf{F}
such the limit on the right-hand side exists.
For example, in
\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
, the space of continuous functions on the two-point compactification of the real line, there is the
basic cosine family
given by

C(t)f(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}[f(x+ct)+f(x-ct)],\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},t\in\mathbb{R},

(2.4)

where, for simplicity of the formula,
c\coloneqq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}
.
Its generator is the operator
f\mapsto\mathfrak{G}f=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
with domain composed of twice continuously differentiable functions on
\mathbb{R}
such that
f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
.

Each cosine family generator is automatically the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup (but not vice versa). The semigroup such operator generates is given by the
Weierstrass formula
(see e.g.
[
1
]
*p. 219)

T(0)f=f\quad\text{and}\quad T(t)f={\textstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi t}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s^{2}}{4t}}\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}f\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t>0,f\in\mathsf{F}.

(2.5)

This formula expresses the fact that the cosine family is then, in a sense, a more fundamental object than the semigroup, and properties of the semigroup can be hidden in those of the cosine family.

Let us take a closer look at three important, simple examples. First of all,
we recall that the semigroup of
unrestricted
Brownian motion (
1.2
) is generated by the particular case of
\mathfrak{G}
considered above with
c=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}
, and so the semigroup has the form (
2.5
). Next, we note
the two subspaces of
\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
formed by even and odd functions are invariant under
\{C(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
. To this end, it suffices to note that

(C(t)f)^{o}=C(t)f^{o}\quad\text{and}\quad(C(t)f)^{e}=C(t)f^{e},\quad f\in\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty],t\in\mathbb{R},

(2.6)

where
f^{e}
and
f^{o}
denote the even and the odd parts of
f
, respectively, that is,

f^{e}(x)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}(f(x)+f(-x))\quad\text{and}\quad f^{o}(x)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}(f(x)-f(-x)),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Also, the subspace of even functions is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
with isomorphism
\mathcal{I}_{e}
mapping a function in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
to its even extension in
\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
. It follows that

C_{\mathsf{ref}}(t)\coloneqq\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{e}C(t)\mathcal{I}_{e},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}

defines a strongly continuous cosine family
\{C_{\mathsf{ref}}(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
. Since the even extension of a member
f
of
\mathfrak{C}^{2}[0,\infty]
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}^{2}[-\infty,\infty]
iff
f^{\prime}(0)=0
, the domain of the generator,
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}
, of this family is composed precisely of
f\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}[0,\infty]
with this property, and we have
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}f=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
. Hence, also the semigroup of reflected Brownian motion (see Sections
1.2
or
4.1
) is of the form (
2.5
). For future reference we note that

C_{\mathsf{ref}}(t)f(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(f(x+ct)+f(|x-ct|)\right),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},x\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.7)

A similar analysis of the subspace of odd functions reveals that

C_{\mathsf{min}}(t)f(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(f(x+ct)+\sgn(x-ct)\cdot f(|x-ct|)\right),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},x\in\mathbb{R}

(2.8)

defines a strongly continuous cosine family in
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]
, and that its generator coincides with that of the semigroup describing minimal Brownian motion.

As we shall see in the main body of the article, also a couple of semigroups describing Brownian motions on star graphs are given by the Weierstrass formula.

2.3.
Skorokhod’s problems, generalized inverses

Let
\omega\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a càdlàg function such that
\omega(0)\geq 0
. A pair
(\omega^{\textnormal{ref}},\ell)
of càdlàg functions
\omega^{\textnormal{ref}},\ell\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is called a solution to the Skorokhod reflection problem for
\omega
if

•

\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}
is nonnegative,

•

\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}=\omega+\ell
, whereas

•

\ell
is nondecreasing with
\ell(0)=0
, and increases only when
\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}
is at
0
, that is,

\int_{[0,\,\infty)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)>0\}}{\rm d}\ell(t)=0.

(2.9)

It is well known (see e.g.
[
41
]
*p. 210, where, however, only continuous
\omega
s are considered; the case of càdlàg functions is discussed in
[
2
]
*p. 250,
[
22
]
and
[
36
]
) that a unique pair that solves the Skorokhod’s reflection problem is given by the formulae

\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)\coloneqq\omega(t)+\ell(t)\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(t)\coloneqq\sup_{s\in[0,t]}(-\omega(s)\vee 0)\qquad t\geq 0.

(2.10)

The
\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}
is termed Skorokhod’s reflection of
\omega
at 0.

In the generalized Skorokhod reflection problem, besides
\omega
described above, we are also given a strictly increasing càdlàg function
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\psi(0)=0
, and search for a pair of functions
\omega^{\textnormal{ref}},\ell\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties:

•

\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}
is nonnegative,

•

\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}=\omega+\psi\circ\ell,
whereas

•

\ell
is a non-decreasing continuous function with
\ell(0)=0
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\omega(t)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell(t)=0.

Such a pair
(\omega^{\textnormal{ref}},\ell)
is said to be a solution to the generalized Skorokhod problem with noise
\omega
and reflection governed by
\psi
.

As expounded in
[
55
]
(see also
[
36
]
) the analysis of this problem involves generalized inverses. To recall, if
\psi
is an increasing càdlàg function such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\psi(t)=+\infty
, then its generalized inverse is defined by

\psi^{-1}(t)\coloneqq\inf\{s\geq 0\colon\psi(s)>t\},\qquad t\geq 0.

Figure
2
shows an example of a function
\psi
, its generalized inverse
\psi^{-1}
, and composition
\psi\circ\psi^{-1}
.

\psi
\psi^{-1}
\psi\circ\psi^{-1}

Figure 2.
Generalized inverses. If a
u\geq 0
is a point of continuity of
\psi
and
\psi(u)=t
then
\psi^{-1}(t)=u
, and so
\psi\circ\psi^{-1}(t)=t
. If, however, at a
u\geq 0
there is a jump of
\psi
, that is,
\psi(u-)<\psi(u)
(by default
\psi(0-)=0
), then
\psi^{-1}(t)=u
for all
t\in[\psi(u-),\psi(u)]
, and so, for such
t
, we have
\psi\circ\psi^{-1}(t)=\psi(u)
.

The theorem alluded to above states that there exists a unique solution to the generalized Skorokhod’s problem provided that (a)
\omega
does not have negative jumps, and (b)
\psi
is strictly increasing with
\psi(0)=0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\psi(t)=+\infty
.
The solution is given explicitly:

\displaystyle\omega^{\textnormal{ref}}

\displaystyle=\omega+\psi\circ\psi^{-1}\circ k\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\ell=\psi^{-1}\circ k

where

\displaystyle k(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{s\in[0,\,t]}(-\omega(s)\vee 0),\qquad t\geq 0.

3.
Laplace operators on a star graph as generators

For a given natural number
{\mathpzc k}\geq 2
, let
S
be the union of
{\mathpzc k}
copies of the compactified right half-line
[0,\infty]
, with all left ends identified, and let
\mathfrak{C}(S)
be the space of continuous functions on
S
. In other words,
S
is the star graph
K_{1,{\mathpzc k}}
depicted at Figure
1
, and continuous functions on
S
can be identified with continuous functions
f
on

\widetilde{S}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\left(\{i\}\times[0,\infty]\right),\qquad\text{where }{\mathpzc K}\coloneqq\{1,\dots,{\mathpzc k}\},

(3.1)

that satisfy
f(i,0)=f(j,0),i,j\in{\mathpzc K}
; their common value at
0
will be denoted
f(\mathfrak{0})
, and the graph’s center will be denoted
\mathfrak{0}
.
Alternatively, members of
\mathfrak{C}(S)
can be viewed as sequences
(f_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
of elements of
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
, where
f_{i}
is defined by
f_{i}(x)=f(i,x),x\geq 0
, such that
f_{i}(0)=f_{j}(0),i,j\in{\mathpzc K}
.

3.1.
Boundary conditions for the Laplace operator in
\mathfrak{C}(S)

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
be the subspace of
\mathfrak{C}(S)
composed of
f
such that each
f_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
defined above belongs to
\mathfrak{C}^{2}[0,\infty]
(see Introduction)
and we have
f^{\prime\prime}_{i}(0)=f^{\prime\prime}_{j}(0)
for all
i,j\in{\mathpzc K}
. For such
f
, we define

\Delta f=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}

where
f^{\prime\prime}(i,x)=f_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x),i\in{\mathpzc K},x\geq 0
.

Following the arguments of Feller
[
26
,
28
]
(see also
[
39
, p. 186]
,
[
52
, pp. 39–40]
or our Appendix
12.1
, where they are repeated with necessary changes), one can show that if a restriction, say,
\mathfrak{G}
, of
\Delta
is a Feller generator, then there are non-negative constants
\alpha,\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and
\gamma
, and a (possibly infinite) positive Borel measure
\mathpzc m
on
S_{\mathfrak{0}}\coloneqq S\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}
such that
\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
, at least one of the conditions
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
and
\mathpzc m(S)=\infty
holds, and

{\tfrac{\alpha}{2}}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}).

(3.2)

We stress that, as in the case of (
1.1
), from the outset we exclude the, theoretically possible but uninteresting, possibility of
\mathpzc m
having a mass at any of the points
(i,\infty),i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover, condition
\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
means that the function
\{i\}\times[0,\infty)\ni(i,x)\mapsto 1\wedge x
is integrable with respect to
\mathpzc m|_{\{i\}\times[0,\infty)}
for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

It is one of the main goals of our paper to show that the converse is also true: the restriction of
\Delta
to the domain where (
3.2
) is satisfied, is a Feller generator. Moreover, we would like to construct the related process using natural building blocks. The case where
\mathpzc m
is zero has been thoroughly described in
[
46
]
–
[
47
]
, and it turns out that this is precisely the case in which the paths of the process are continuous; these processes are discussed again in some detail in Sections
4
–
5
. In Sections
6
–
8
the measure
\mathpzc m
is shown to be responsible for jumps from the boundary. Roughly, in Section
6
, devoted to the case of finite
\mathpzc m
, jumps occur between regular excursions from zero and are comparatively rare. In Sections
7
and
8
, where infinite
\mathpzc m
is discussed, they become very frequent and infinitesimally small at the same time; in the extreme case of
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
they turn out to be the only means of exiting from the boundary.

We end this section with the following remark. For convenience, throughout the paper we work with the compactified graph, that is, we adjoin special points
(i,\infty),i\in{\mathpzc K}
to the locally compact space
\bigcup_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\left(\{i\}\times[0,\infty)\right)
— see (
3.1
). These points are isolated in the sense that in the processes featured in the paper none of them can be reached in finite time from ‘regular’ points of the state-space. Indeed, reaching them is clearly impossible by means of an underlying Brownian motion, and by assuming that
\mathpzc m
has no mass at
\bigcup_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\{(i,\infty)\}
we exclude the possibility of reaching them by a jump. These points are furthermore traps: when started at any of them the processes considered in this paper will stay there for ever. This is a natural consequence of the definition of
\Delta
: for
f\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
, each of the limits
\lim_{x\to\infty}f^{\prime\prime}(i,x),i\in{\mathpzc K}
exists and equals zero, and so
\Delta f(i,\infty)=0
.

3.2.
Operators
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
as generators

Since the resolvents and semigroups corresponding to the case where
\mathpzc m=0
will be used as building blocks of the more complex resolvents and semigroups involving nonzero
\mathpzc m
, we complete this section by proving a generation result pertaining to the former objects.

Let non-negative parameters
\alpha,\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\gamma
be such that

\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0,

(3.3)

and let
F:\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be the functional given by

Ff={\alpha}\Delta f(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f_{i}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0}).

(3.4)

Then, the operator

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}

(3.5)

defined as the restriction of
\Delta
to

\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})\coloneqq C^{2}(S)\cap\ker F

is a Feller generator. Indeed, it is obviously densely defined and an easy argument shows that it satisfies the positive maximum principle — comp. e.g.
[
17
]
*p. 17, or see our Appendix
12.2
. Furthermore, the solution
f
to the resolvent equation
\lambda f-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}f=g
can be given explicitly for any
\lambda>0
and
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
:

f_{i}(x)=C_{i}(g)\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{2\lambda}x}+D_{i}(g)\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}-\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{x}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}(x-y)g_{i}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.6)

where

\displaystyle C_{i}

\displaystyle=C_{i}(g)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}y}g_{i}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

(3.7)

D_{i}=D_{i}(g)\coloneqq E-C_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
and

E=E(g)\coloneqq\frac{2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)+\alpha g(\mathfrak{0})}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}+\gamma}

(3.8)

is the common value of
f_{i}(0),i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

To prove the last statement we search for solutions of the resolvent equation in the form (
3.6
) where
C_{i}
s and
D_{i}
s are constants to be found, and the third term is a particular solution of the related ODE. The requirement that
f_{i}
is to belong to
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
then forces
C_{i}
to be defined as above (see
[
15
]
*Section 8.2.18 or
[
17
]
*p. 18 for details, if necessary). Since
f_{i}(0)
cannot depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and
F(f_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
is to be zero, we obtain a system of linear equations on
E
and
D_{i}
s, which yields (
3.8
). Uniqueness of the solution to the resolvent equation is guaranteed by the fact that
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
satisfies the positive-maximum principle.

For future reference we note that (
3.6
) can be written as

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+E(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}

(3.9)

where

\displaystyle(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}|x-y|}-\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}(x+y)})g_{i}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2C_{i}(g)\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{x}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}(x-y)g_{i}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

(3.10)

is the resolvent of the minimal Brownian motion on
S
, and

(\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0})_{i}(x)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x},\qquad x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(3.11)

The fact that in the case of
\gamma>0
the process is not honest is expressed in
\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S}\not=\mathsf{1}_{S}
.
The function
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\coloneqq\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S}
is the Laplace transform of the lifetime
\tau
of the process (see e.g.
[
18
]
*Proposition 3.5), and a short calculation reveals that

(\mathpzc l_{\lambda})_{i}(x)=\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}{\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}}\beta_{i}+\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x},\qquad x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(3.12)

3.3.
Lumping edges together

To record an interesting property of the resolvent of
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
, given by (
3.9
), for a given
\mathpzc n\leq{\mathpzc k}
, let
\mathfrak{C}(Sn)
be the space of continuous functions on the star graph with
\mathpzc n
edges, to be distinguished from the space
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathpzc k})
of continuous functions on the star graph with
{\mathpzc k}
edges. Also, let
\Psi
map
{\mathpzc K}
onto
\mathpzc N\coloneqq\{1,\dots,\mathpzc n\}
, and
\mathfrak{C}_{\Psi}(S_{\mathpzc k})
be the related subspace of
g=(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathpzc k})
such that
g_{i}=g_{j}
as long as
\Psi(i)=\Psi(j)
. This subspace is clearly isometrically isomorphic to
\mathfrak{C}(Sn)
, the isomorphism, say,
\mathcal{I}:\mathfrak{C}(Sn)\to\mathfrak{C}_{\Psi}(S_{\mathpzc k})
, being given by the requirement that the
i
th coordinate of
\mathcal{I}g
equals

g_{\Psi(i)},g\in\mathfrak{C}(Sn),i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover, the operators
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)^{-1},\lambda>0
leave
\mathfrak{C}_{\Psi}(S_{\mathpzc k})
invariant (because so do
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
and
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}_{\Psi}(S_{\mathpzc k})
)
and we have

\mathcal{I}^{-1}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{I}=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma}\right)^{-1},\qquad\lambda>0,

(3.13)

where
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma}
is the generator in
\mathfrak{C}(Sn)
with
\widetilde{\beta}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
given by
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}\beta_{i},j\in\mathpzc N
.
Formula (
3.13
) is an early indication of the fact that by lumping edges of the underlying graph, from the process generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
one obtains the process generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma}
(see the warning in Section
2.1
).
We will be coming back repeatedly to this subject throughout these notes.

4.
Walsh’s process (the case of
\alpha=\gamma=0
and
\mathpzc m=0
)

Let
\beta=(\beta_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
be a vector
with positive coordinates
such that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
; the second, unlike the first, of these assumptions is made without loss of generality because multiplying
F
of (
3.4
) by a positive constant does not change
F
’s kernel.

Somewhat informally, the Walsh’s motion, a.k.a., Walsh’s spider process or Walsh’s spider, is a continuous time-homogeneous strong Markov process on
S
that behaves like a standard one-dimensional Brownian motion on each ray

\mathpzc r_{i}\coloneqq\{i\}\times[0,\infty],\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

until hitting the graph center; upon hitting the center
Brownian excursions ‘select’ ray
\mathpzc r_{j}
with probability
\beta_{j},j\in{\mathpzc K}
. In what follows the process will be denoted
W_{\beta}
.

4.1.
Semigroups, resolvents, and cosine families

As revealed in
[
4
]
, Walsh’s spider is a Feller process on
S
and its semigroup can be written explicitly in terms of the minimal and reflected semigroups.

To elaborate, we recall first that the (one-dimensional) minimal Brownian motion semigroup
\left(T_{\mathsf{min}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a strongly continuous semigroup of operators in
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]
, the subspace of
f\in\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
such that
f(0)=0
. The semigroup is given by
T_{\mathsf{min}}(0)f=f
and

[T_{\mathsf{min}}(t)f](x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\varphi_{t}(x-y)-\varphi_{t}(x+y)\right)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

for
t>0,x\geq 0
and
f\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]
;
here and throughout the paper,

\varphi_{t}(z)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2t}},\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},t>0.

The domain of its generator
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}
is composed of
f\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]

that are twice continuously differentiable with
f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}(0,\infty]
. For such
f
we have

\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}f=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
.

We recall also that the reflecting Brownian motion semigroup in
\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
is given by
T_{\mathsf{ref}}(0)f=f
and

T_{\text{ref}}(t)f(x)=\mathsf{E}\,f(|x+B(t)|)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\varphi_{t}(x-y)+\varphi_{t}(x+y)\right)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

for
x\geq 0,t>0
and
f\in\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
.
The domain of the generator
\mathfrak{G}_{\text{ref}}
of
\left(T_{\text{ref}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is composed of
f
that are twice continuously differentiable with
f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty]
, and satisfy
f^{\prime}(0)=0
; for such
f
we have
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{ref}}f=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
.

In terms of the semigroups described above, the Walsh’s process semigroup
\left(T_{W_{\beta}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
has the following form (see
[
4
]
, eq. (2.2)):

(T_{W_{\beta}}(t)f)_{i}=T_{\mathsf{min}}(t)(f_{i}-\overline{f})+T_{\mathsf{ref}}(t)\overline{f},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

(4.1)

where

\overline{f}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{j}f_{j}.

There are three immediate and important consequences of (
4.1
). First of all, the generator
is the restriction of
\Delta
to
f\in\mathcal{D}(\Delta)
such that, as a special case of (
3.2
),

(\overline{f})^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)=0;

in other words, the generator coincides with
\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}
of Section
3
.
Indeed, on one hand, for
f
described above,
f_{i}-\overline{f}
belongs to the domain of
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}
for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and
\overline{f}
belongs to the domain of
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{ref}}
. Therefore,

\lim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}(T_{W_{\beta}}(t)f-f)_{i}=\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{min}}(f_{i}-\overline{f})+\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{ref}}\overline{f}=\tfrac{1}{2}f_{i}^{\prime\prime},

showing that the generator of
\left(T_{W_{\beta}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
extends
\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}
. On the other hand,
we know from Section
3.2
that given a
\lambda>0
and a
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
there is precisely one
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0})
such that
\lambda f-\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}f=g
. A standard argument shows thus that the searched for generator cannot be a proper extension of
\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}
(see e.g.
[
40
]
*p. 377 or
[
58
]
*p. 242). A different derivation of the boundary condition for the Walsh’s process can be found in
[
46
]
.

Secondly, (
4.1
) implies that
W_{\beta}
has the following transition density function:

p_{t}((i,x),(j,y))=\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(x-y)+(2\beta_{i}-1)\varphi_{t}(x+y),&\ i=j,\\
2\beta_{j}\varphi_{t}(x+y),&\ i\neq j,\end{cases}

(4.2)

where
t>0
and
x,y\geq 0
. These formulae, in turn, show that if the initial distribution of Walsh’s process is absolutely continuous with respect to the natural Lebesgue measure on
S
, then so is its distribution at any
t\geq 0
. One can thus think of the semigroup of Markov operators in
L^{1}(S)
that describes this dynamics (for the notion of Markov operators see e.g.
[
48
]
or
[
61
]
). As shown in
[
14
]
(comp.
[
12
]
) the domain of the generator of this semigroup is characterized by the ‘dual’ boundary conditions

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\phi^{\prime}_{i}(0)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{j}\phi_{i}(0)=\beta_{i}\phi_{j}(0),\quad i,j\in{\mathpzc K}.

As the third consequence of (
4.1
), the resolvent
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}\right)^{-1},\lambda>0
, which is the Laplace transform of the Walsh’s process semigroup, has the following representation: for
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
,

\displaystyle(\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}\right)^{-1}g)_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}(x)+2\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}\varphi_{t}(x+y)\overline{g}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}(x)+2\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)(\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0})_{i}(x),

where
R_{\lambda}^{0}
, introduced in (
3.10
), is the resolvent of the minimal Brownian motion on
S
,
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
is the Laplace transform of its life-time (see (
3.11
)), and
C_{i}(g)
s are defined in (
3.7
). The above relation,
not surprisingly, is a special case of (
3.9
).

As shown recently in
[
56
]
, the fundamental formula (
4.1
) is but a reflection of a deeper result. To see this, let
\mathsf{F}=(\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty])^{\mathpzc k}
be the Cartesian product of
{\mathpzc k}
copies of the space
\mathfrak{C}[-\infty,\infty]
of Section
2.2
, and let
\{\mathcal{C}(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be the
Cartesian product basic cosine family
in
\mathsf{F}
defined by means of the basic cosine family of (
2.4
) as follows:

\mathcal{C}(t)(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}=(C(t)h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Using (
2.6
) it is then easy to see that the subspace

\mathsf{F}_{\beta}\coloneqq\{(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\in\mathsf{F}\colon h_{i}^{e}=h_{j}^{e},i,j\in{\mathpzc K}\text{ and }(\overline{h})^{o}=0\}

(4.3)

is invariant under
\{\mathcal{C}(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
; here, as before,
\overline{h}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}h_{i}
, and
h^{e}
and
h^{o}
denote the even and the odd parts of
h
, respectively.

Moreover,
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
is isomorphic to
\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}\subset(\mathfrak{C}[0,\infty])^{\mathpzc k}
. For, given
(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\in\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
, we obtain an element
(f_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
by defining
f_{i}(x)=h_{i}(x),x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
; note that, by definition of
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
,
h_{i}(0)=h_{j}(0)
and so
f_{i}(0)=f_{j}(0),i,j\in{\mathpzc K}
. Vice versa, given
(f_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
we define
(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{\beta}(f_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
by

h_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}f_{i}(x),&x\geq 0,\\
-f_{i}(-x)+2\overline{f}(-x),&x<0,\end{cases}

to check that
h_{i}^{e}(x)=\overline{f}(x),x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}h_{i}^{o}=0
, which implies that
(h_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
belongs to
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{R}
are inverses of one another, it follows that
\{\mathcal{C}_{\beta}(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
defined by

\mathcal{C}_{\beta}(t)=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{C}(t)\mathcal{E}_{\beta},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}

is a strongly continuous cosine family in
\mathfrak{C}(S)
. Now, a straightforward calculation shows that

(\mathcal{C}_{\beta}(t)f)_{i}=C_{\mathsf{min}}(t)(f_{i}-\overline{f})+C_{\mathsf{ref}}(t)\overline{f},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},t\in\mathbb{R},i\in{\mathpzc K},

(4.4)

where
C_{\mathsf{min}}
and
C_{\mathsf{ref}}
, defined in Section
2.2
, are cosine families related to the minimal and reflected Brownian motions. Because of (
4.1
) and the
Weierstrass formula, the generator of
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
coincides with the generator of
T_{W_{\beta}}
, that is, with
\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0}
.

To summarize: (
4.1
) mirrors (
4.4
), and the latter formula is a direct consequence of the fact that
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
of (
4.3
) is invariant under the Cartesian product basic cosine family.

4.2.
Stochastic description

Having described its semigroup, we proceed with stochastic characterization of Walsh’s process itself. In this section, starting with a formal definition of the process, we present three characterization theorems. The first two of these come from the early paper
[
4
]
, whereas the third has been obtained recently in
[
6
]
.

4.2.1.
Two theorems of Barlow, Pitman and Yor

To begin with, it is clear from the intuitive description given in Section
4.1
, or either of formulae (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), that the distance of Walsh’s spider from the graph’s center is a reflected Brownian motion. In the Walsh’s original construction, in turn, the spider process is obtained as a function of reflected Brownian motion started at
0
as follows. Let a sequence
\left(\epsilon_{i}\right)_{i\geq 0}
of i.i.d. random variables be given such that
\mathsf{P}(\epsilon_{i}=j)=\beta_{j},i\geq 0,j\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover, let us enumerate excursions of a given, independent of
\left(\epsilon_{i}\right)_{i\geq 0}
, reflected Brownian motion
B^{\textnormal{ref}}
in an arbitrary measurable way, and let
n(t)=n(t,\omega)
be the number of the excursion at time
t
in this enumeration (if
B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)=0
, then
n(t)
is defined arbitrarily, say
n(t)=1
). Then

W_{\beta}(t)\coloneqq(\epsilon_{n(t)},B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t))\in S,\qquad t\geq 0

(4.5)

is a Walsh’s spider process with parameter
\beta
. This formula says that we place each excursion of a reflected Brownian motion on the
i
th ray with probability
\beta_{i}
independently of all other excursions and of the reflected Brownian motion.

One can also think of
W_{\beta}
as a
{\mathpzc k}
dimensional process. To this end, using (
4.5
), for
i\in{\mathpzc K}

we define
X_{i}(t)\coloneqq B^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)
whenever
n(t)=i
, and
X_{i}(t)=0
otherwise. Then we can identify
W_{\beta}
with the vector
(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
; we note that all possible values of this random vector lie in

S_{\mathsf{can}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}\colon v=x\mathfrak{e}_{i}\text{ for some }x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}\}

where
(\mathfrak{e}_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}
, and ‘can’ stands for ‘canonical’.

In this spirit, Barlow, Pitman and Yor
[
4
]
have characterized Walsh’s process as follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
B
be a one-dimensional standard Brownian motion, and
\sigma(B)\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon B(t)=0\}
be the first time
B
hits zero. Also, let

X=(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}

be a strong Markov process with values in
S_{\mathsf{can}}
, and let the first time it hits
\mathfrak{0}
be denoted
\sigma(X)
. Then
X
is Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta
iff

(a)

for each
x\geq 0
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and a Borel subset
\mathcal{B}
of the space of continuous paths

\mathsf{P}\big\{(X_{i}(t)1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{t\leq\sigma(X)\}})_{t\geq 0}\in\mathcal{B}\big|X(0)=x\mathfrak{e}_{i}\big\}\\
=\mathsf{P}\big\{(B(t)1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{t\leq\sigma(B)\}})_{t\geq 0}\in\mathcal{B}\big|B(0)=x\big\},

(b)

for each
t>0
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and a Borel set
\mathpzc B\subset(0,\infty)

\mathsf{P}\{X_{i}(t)\in\mathpzc B|X(0)=\mathfrak{0}\}=\beta_{i}\mathsf{P}\left\{|B(t)|\in\mathpzc B\big|B(0)=0\right\}.

Condition (a) in the theorem says that up to the time it hits
\mathfrak{0}
,
W_{\beta}
started at an
i
th ray is undistinguishable from the standard Brownian motion on this ray, and (b) says that
W_{\beta}
started at the origin is at a time
t>0
on the
i
th ray with probability
\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Next, we turn to a martingale characterization of Walsh’s spider process. We begin by stating that, since the distance of Walsh’s process from the origin is a reflected Brownian motion,
the local time of the former process at
0
can be defined as the almost sure limit

L(t)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0.

(4.6)

We remark in passing that both Walsh’s process and reflected Brownian motion have zero sojourn at
0
with probability
1
. Hence the integral above could have been replaced by
\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{|W_{\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s
. For reasons that will become clear later, it is, however, convenient to exclude
0
from the integration (see, for example, the calculation leading to (
5.19
)).

The following martingale characterization of Walsh’s process was first given in
[
4
]
, see also
[
20
]
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
X=(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
and
L
be two continuous processes, the first with values in

\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}
, the second with values in
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\mathcal{F}^{X}
be the filtration generated by
X
. Then
X
is a Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta
, and
L
is its local time at
0
defined by (
4.6
) iff

(1)

X_{i}(t)\geq 0,1\leq i\leq{\mathpzc K}
, and
X_{i}(t)X_{j}(t)=0,i,j\in{\mathpzc K},i\not=j
for
t\geq 0
;

(2)

L
is a.s. nondecreasing
\mathcal{F}^{X}
-adapted process with
L(0)=0
and

\int_{[0,\,\infty)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s)\neq\mathfrak{0}\}}\,{\rm d}L(s)=0\quad\text{a.s.};

(3)

the processes
M_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
defined by

M_{i}(t)\coloneqq X_{i}(t)-\beta_{i}L(t),\quad t\geq 0

(4.7)

are continuous square integrable martingales with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{X}
with predictable quadratic variations

\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0;

(4.8)

(4)

\int_{0}^{\infty}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s)=\mathfrak{0}\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=0

a.s.

Let us mention two immediate corollaries to Theorem
4.2
. First, let

W_{\beta}
be Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta
, and let

\Psi
map
{\mathpzc K}
onto
\mathpzc N\coloneqq\{1,\dots,\mathpzc n\}
, where
\mathpzc n\leq{\mathpzc k}
is a natural number.
The process
(X_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
defined by

X_{j}(t)=\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\beta})_{i}(t),\qquad j\in\mathpzc N,t\geq 0

where
(W_{\beta})_{i}
is the
i
th coordinate of
W_{\beta}
, is then Walsh’s process with the new parameter
\widetilde{\beta}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
obtained as follows
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}\beta_{i},j\in\mathpzc N
. Moreover, the local times of
X
and
W_{\beta}
at
\mathfrak{0}
coincide. Thus, in the specific instance of
\alpha=\gamma=0
, this establishes the fact suggested already by formula (
3.13
): by lumping edges of the graph in which Walsh’s process has its values, we obtain another Walsh’s process, on a ‘smaller’ graph.

In particular,

X(t)\coloneqq{\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}}(W_{\beta})_{i}(t),\qquad t\geq 0,

(4.9)

is a reflected Brownian motion.
Notably, this process can be represented as

X(t)=B(t)+L(t),\qquad t\geq 0,

where
B\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}M_{i}
(the martingales
M_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
are defined in point (3) above)
is a Brownian motion.

4.2.2.
A theorem of Bayraktar et al.

Our next and final goal in this section is a representation of Walsh’s process that emerged from the analysis of so called
multi-armed bandits
, see
[
51
,
42
,
3
,
6
]
.
By way of introduction, we recall that any square integrable continuous martingale
M
is a time-changed Brownian motion: there exists a Brownian motion
B
such that
M=B\circ\langle M\rangle
, where
\langle M\rangle
is the quadratic variation of
M
(see e.g.
[
35
]
*Sec. 2.7, Thm. 7.2 for this statement in its more general form). In this context (
4.8
) says that for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, there exists a Brownian motion
B_{i}

such that

X_{i}(t)=B_{i}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)+\beta_{i}L(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

The last expression in turn reveals that the paths of the process
X_{i}
are solutions to the Skorokhod reflection problems with noise given by the paths of
B_{i}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(t),t\geq 0
. Since solutions to these problems are determined uniquely and are given by (
2.10
), we see that

X_{i}(t)=B_{i}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)+\max_{s\in[0,t]}\left(-B_{i}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)\vee 0\right),\qquad t\geq 0.

As an immediate consequence,

X_{i}=B_{i}^{\textnormal{ref}}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle,\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},

(4.10)

where, as before,
B_{i}^{\textnormal{ref}}(t)\coloneqq B_{i}(t)+L_{i}(t),t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
L_{i}(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}

(-B_{i}(s)\vee 0),t\geq 0,
is the Skorokhod reflection of
B_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
. The Brownian motions
B_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
are independent due to Knight’s theorem, see
[
57
, Theorem 1.9, Chapter V]
.

Moreover, (
4.7
) reveals that each
X_{i}
is a semimartingale, as the sum of the martingale
M_{i}
and the nondecreasing process
\beta_{i}L
. Hence, the Tanaka formula (
[
41
]
*p. 220 or
[
59
]
*p. 96) applies to give

\displaystyle X_{i}(t)-X_{i}(0)

\displaystyle=|X_{i}(t)|-|X_{i}(0)|

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}(M_{i}(s)+\beta_{i}L(s))+L_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s)+L_{i}(t)

(4.11)

where in the first step we have used the fact that
X_{i}
is nonnegative and in the last — the fact that
L
increases only when
X_{i}=0
;
L_{i}
is the symmetric local time of
X_{i}
at
0
. Also,
\mathsf{E}\,(\int_{0}^{t}1_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s)-\int_{0}^{t}1\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s))^{2}=\mathsf{E}\,(\int_{0}^{t}1_{\{X_{i}(s)\leq 0\}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s))^{2}
and this, by Ito isometry,
equals

\mathsf{E}\,\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)\leq 0\}}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M_{i}(t)\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)\leq 0\}}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=0,\qquad t\geq 0.

with the previous-to-last equality following by (
4.8
).
Hence, almost surely,
\int_{0}^{t}1_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s)=\int_{0}^{t}1\,\mathrm{d}M_{i}(s)=M_{i}(t)-M_{i}(0)
and so (
4.11
) renders
X_{i}(t)-X_{i}(0)=M_{i}(t)-M_{i}(0)+L_{i}(t)
. Since
X_{i}(0)=M_{i}(0)
, (
4.7
) now proves that
\beta_{i}L
and
L_{i}
coincide and we can thus write

\displaystyle\beta_{i}L(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|X_{i}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|B_{i}^{\textnormal{ref}}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|B_{i}^{\textnormal{ref}}(s))|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=L_{i}\circ\langle M_{i}\rangle(t)=L_{i}\left(\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(z)>0\}}{\rm d}z\right),\qquad t\geq 0.

It follows that, almost surely, for all
i,j\in{\mathpzc K}
,

L(t)=\frac{L_{i}(\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(z)>0\}}{\rm d}z)}{\beta_{i}}=\frac{L_{j}(\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{j}(z)>0\}}{\rm d}z)}{\beta_{j}},\qquad t\geq 0.

(4.12)

The so-obtained formula suggests that a copy of Walsh’s process can be constructed as a concatenation of several independent Brownian motions balanced locally via ties similar to these visible above. The following theorem establishes that this is indeed the case; the time
T_{i}(t),t\geq 0
featured in it is a counterpart of
\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,t\geq 0
, considered above, and should be interpreted as the time the process spends at the
i
th ray of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
. As already mentioned, the original idea of the theorem comes from the theory of multi-armed bandits, see
[
51
,
42
,
3
,
6
]
; the statement below is a version of a more general result
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\beta=(\beta_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
be a vector of positive numbers such that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
. Given
a standard
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional Wiener process
B=(B_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
with
B(0)\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
, we define

L_{i}(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}(-B_{i}(s)\vee 0)\quad\text{ and }\quad B^{\textnormal{ref}}_{i}(t)\coloneqq B_{i}(t)+L_{i}(t),\quad i\in{\mathpzc K},t\geq 0.

Then there is a unique collection of continuous, non-negative and non-decreasing processes
T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
such that almost surely

\frac{L_{i}(T_{i}(t))}{\beta_{i}}=\frac{L_{j}(T_{j}(t))}{\beta_{j}},\qquad i,j\in{\mathpzc K},t\geq 0,

(4.13)

and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
.
Moreover, the process

X\coloneqq(B^{\textnormal{ref}}_{i}\circ T_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}

(4.14)

is then Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta.

A couple of remarks are here in order. First of all,
only one coordinate of the Walsh’s process
X
defined in (
4.14
) can be non-zero,
X
has no sojourn at
\mathfrak{0}
, and the processes
T_{i}
from Theorem
4.3
satisfy
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
. Therefore, almost surely,

T_{i}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,

so that indeed
T_{i}(t)
is the time spent at the
i
th ray up to time
t
. Also, the theorem shows that the local time
L(t)
defined in Theorem
4.2
is tied to the local times of Theorem
4.3

by

L(t)=\frac{L_{i}(T_{i}(t))}{\beta_{i}},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},t\geq 0,

which agrees with (
4.12
); moreover,

\displaystyle L(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}{L_{i}(T_{i}(t))},\qquad t\geq 0.

(4.15)

Finally, formula (
4.14
) is richer in meaning than (
4.10
); whereas in the latter existence of a Brownian motion that underlies
X_{i}
is claimed for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the former asserts moreover that these Brownian motions are independent.

Most importantly, the process
X
of (
4.14
)
can be considered as a multi-parameter time change of the multidimensional reflected Brownian motion
(B^{\textnormal{ref}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
. In fact, it was proved in
[
6
, Lemma 3.3, Proposition 3.2]
that for
multi-parameter stopping times
, that is, a class of processes that includes
(T_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
, an analogue of the
the strong Markov property holds.

A similar argument shows that for any
t\geq 0
the processes

\{B_{i}(T_{i}(t)+s)-B_{i}(T_{i}(t)),s\geq 0\},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

are mutually independent Brownian motions that are also independent of the processes describing the past:

\{B_{i}(T_{i}(t)\wedge s),s\geq 0\},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

Further, if a non-negative exponential random variable
\zeta
is independent of
B,
then
the processes

\{B_{i}(T_{i}(\sigma)+s)-B_{i}(T_{i}(\sigma)),s\geq 0\},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

(4.16)

where
\sigma\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon L(t)=\zeta\},
are mutually independent Brownian motions that are also independent of the processes describing the past

\{B_{i}(T_{i}(\sigma)\wedge s),s\geq 0\},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

We complete this section by remarking that
filtration generated by Walsh’s process is a delicate subject. Theorem
4.3

shows that Walsh’s process is a function of Brownian motions. However, the filtration generated by the Walsh process on a graph with more than three rays differs from the Brownian filtration
[
63
]
.

4.3.
The degenerate case

So far, in our stochastic description, we have focused on the case where
\beta_{i}>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
. Since multiplying
F
of (
3.4
) by a positive constant does not change
F
’s kernel, the second of these conditions is just a normalization that additionally provides a natural interpretation of
\beta_{i}
s, but this is not the case with the first of them. Hence, there is the need to clarify what happens when some, but not all,
\beta_{i}
vanish.

Let us thus consider the case where
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i
in a proper subset
\mathpzc L
of
{\mathpzc K}
; without loss of generality we can assume that
\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc L}\beta_{j}=1
(in other words, by definition,
W_{\beta}\coloneqq W_{\beta^{\prime}}
where
\beta^{\prime}
is obtained from
\beta
by dividing its coordinates by
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc L}\beta_{i}
). If started from an
i
th edge with
i\in\mathpzc L
, the degenerate
W_{\beta}
behaves like a one-dimensional Brownian motion until the time it reaches the origin; at this instant it chooses, with probability
\beta_{j}
, to continue its motion on a
j
th edge with
j\not\in\mathpzc L
. From that time on it never returns to the edge where it initially started, neither does it go to any of the other edges with indices
i\in\mathpzc L
, but rather behaves like the Walsh’s spider on the subset of the original graph formed by edges with indices
j\in{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc L
. In other words, points of edges with indices
i\in\mathpzc L
are transient for the process, and the process is essentially the Walsh’s spider on a smaller star graph with
\#({\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc L)
edges.

Interestingly, the transition densities formula (
4.2
) remains valid also in the degenerate case. In particular, for
i\in\mathpzc L
, the first line of the formula says that from the perspective of the
i
th ray the process is that of minimal Brownian motion: once it reaches
\mathfrak{0}
it is never seen again. At the same time, as expressed in the second line, the
i
th ray is invisible, that is, unreachable, from the other edges.

5.
The full case of
\mathpzc m=0
; processes with continuous paths.

Having described the process of Walsh, which, to recall, corresponds to the case of
\alpha=\gamma=0
in (
3.4
), we now proceed to the general case of nonzero
\alpha
and/or
\gamma
. In other words, we will now study the processes related to the boundary condition

{\tfrac{\alpha}{2}}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=0\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}).

(5.1)

We start with the case where all betas are positive and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
.

We have already developed the intuition that increasing
\alpha
forces the related process to linger at
\mathfrak{0}
longer, and nonzero
\gamma
tells of the possibility for the process to be killed, that is, to be undefined after a certain random time spent at
\mathfrak{0}
. The construction presented below confirms this intuition and gives deeper insight into what really happens. This construction, notably, can be carried out in a rather general context, and so, in Sections
5.1
–
5.4

we present this general picture, and specialize to the case of Walsh’s process later in Section
5.5
, where we provide a description of paths of the process related to (
5.1
).

5.1.
Slowing down a Feller process at a point

Let
X
be a Feller process with values in a compact metric space
S
; we assume its trajectories are càdlàg. Moreover, let
a\in S
be a point such that for
\sigma
defined as the first time
X
hits
a
(
\sigma\coloneqq\inf\{t>0\colon X(t)=a\}
) we have

(a)

a
is regular for itself, that is,
\mathsf{E}_{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\sigma}=1,\lambda>0
,
and

(b)

S\ni x\mapsto\mathsf{\mathpzc l_{\lambda}}(x)\coloneqq\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\sigma}\in[0,1]
is continuous for all
\lambda>0
.

It is well-known
[
11
]
*pp. 84–93 that even under less stringent assumptions (condition (b) is introduced in
[
11
]
much later, on p. 140) there is a local time, say,
L
, of
X
at
a
, that is, a continuous additive functional (CAF) that may grow only when
X
visits
a
.
We recall that in fact there are many such local times, but that all of them are equivalent up to a multiplicative constant. For the present purposes it is irrelevant which local time at
a
is used.

To proceed, using
L
and given
\alpha>0
, we define another CAF by

A_{\alpha}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}t+\alpha L(t),&t\in[0,\tau),\\
\tau+\alpha L(\tau-),&t\in[\tau,\infty),\end{cases}

(5.2)

where
\tau
is the life-time of
X
(see also the remark at the end of this section).
Then, each trajectory of
A_{\alpha}
is strictly increasing and continuous on
[0,\tau)
, and we have
A_{\alpha}(0)=0
. It is moreover well-known that for each
t\geq 0
, the
[0,\infty]
-valued random variable
A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)
is a stopping time with respect to the filtration generated by
X
. It follows (see
[
23
, Theorem 10.10, Chapter 10.5]
or
[
30
, Chapter II.6]
) that
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
given by

\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}X\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),&A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)<\infty,\\
\text{undefined},&A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)=\infty,\end{cases}

(5.3)

is a time-homogeneous Markov process. As we shall see in Section
5.3
,
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
has the Feller property.

For now, we will argue that
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
‘slows down’ when at
a
and only at
a
, and spends positive time at this point. First of all, whenever
X
is away from
a
,
L
does not increase. Thus
A_{\alpha}
increases linearly with slope
1
and so does
A_{\alpha}^{-1}
. This means that while away from
a
,
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
behaves precisely as
X
. However, when at
a
,
A_{\alpha}
grows faster than linearly with slope
1
and so
A_{\alpha}^{-1}
grows slower than linearly with slope
1
, that is, slower than the regular time, as claimed. This analysis shows that excursions of
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
from
a
coincide with excursions of
X
; it is only the parts of the trajectories where
X
is at
0
that are ‘stretched out’.

Turning to the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
spends a positive time at
a
, we write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(A_{\alpha}^{-1}(s))=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(u)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\alpha}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(u)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}(u+\alpha L(u))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(u)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}u+\alpha L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),

(5.4)

and note that the second term is positive for all
t\geq\sigma
. This establishes the claim.

We prove also that

\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}L\coloneqq L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}

is a continuous additive functional for
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
. To this end, since the process
\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,t\geq 0
is obviously a continuous additive functional, it suffices to show that for a constant, say,
c
,

L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)=c\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

(5.5)

almost surely with respect to any initial distribution. To prove this formula, in turn, we suppose first that
X
has zero sojourn time at
a
, that is, by definition, that
\int_{0}^{\infty}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=0
, almost surely as above. In this case, the first term in the last line of (
5.4
) is
0
, and then, almost surely,

\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\alpha L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

(5.6)

This means that (
5.5
) holds with
c\coloneqq\alpha^{-1}
. Also, if
X
