does not have zero sojourn at
a
, then for some
t>0
the probability of the event
\{\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s>0\}
is not zero. As a result,
\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s)=a\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,t\geq 0
is a nontrivial local time, and so, by the uniqueness result mentioned already above, all other local times
are constant multiples of this one. In particular,
L(t)=c_{1}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{X(u)=a}\,\mathrm{d}u,t\geq 0
for a certain
c_{1}>0
. But then,
the first term in the last line of (
5.4
) is
c_{1}^{-1}L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)
; this shows that (
5.5
) holds with
c\coloneqq(c_{1}^{-1}+\alpha)^{-1}
, completing the proof.

Before completing this section, we come back to formula (
5.2
) to comment on its second line and its influence on the definition of the local time
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}L
. Namely, given a Markov process that is not honest, it is tempting to agree that its CAF is undefined whenever the process is undefined. However, such an agreement leads to technical difficulties in interpreting the CAF’s functional equation, and complicates further considerations. As it turns out (compare e.g.
[
10
]
*p. 149), it is thus more convenient to define the CAF, say,
K
, beyond the lifetime
\tau
of the process by

K(t):=K(\tau-),\qquad t\geq\tau,

(5.7)

so that, for
t\geq\tau
,
K(t)
can be thought of as the CAF accumulated during the lifetime.
Definition (
5.2
) is in line with this more convenient approach. Throughout the paper, we will keep defining CAFs, and local times in particular, beyond the lifetimes of their processes as in (
5.7
); see the next section for example.

5.2.
Killing a Feller process at a point

A Feller process can also be killed. To elaborate, let again
X
be a process satisfying assumptions (a)–(b) of Section
5.1
and
L
be its local time at
a
. Given an independent exponentially distributed random variable
\zeta
with parameter
\gamma
, we let
\tau\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon L(t)=\zeta\}
. Then the process

\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}X(t),&t<\tau,\\
\text{undefined},&t\geq\tau\end{cases}

(5.8)

will be referred to as
killed
at
\tau
. It is well known that
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
is a strong Markov process. Moreover, it turns out that

\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}L(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}L(t),&t<\tau,\\
L(\tau-),&t\geq\tau\end{cases}

is a local time of
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
at
a
.

It is interesting that the transformations
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
in a sense commute. Whereas, in general, processes
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
and
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
obviously differ, they turn out to be the same if extra care is taken in their construction. Suppose namely that an exponential random variable
\zeta
is fixed and
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
is defined to be equal to
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
up to (but not including)
the time when
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}L=\zeta
, and undefined
from that time on. Also, let
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
be the process
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
slowed down with the help of the local time
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}L
defined using the same variable
\zeta
. Then, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X=\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
.

5.3.
A criterion for Feller property

Our next aim is to prove that the strong Markov processes
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
and
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
inherit Fellerian nature of the original process. To this end, in this section, we introduce an auxiliary criterion for Feller property, that is, Proposition
5.1
further down.

Let
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
be the resolvent of a process
X
satisfying conditions (a) and (b) of Section
5.1
, that is, let

R_{\lambda}f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}f(X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad x\in S,\lambda>0,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

For simplicity of exposition and without losing any essential examples, we assume furthermore that
a
is an accumulation point of
S
and the lifetime of
X
is no smaller than
\sigma
, so that
X
is defined at least to the time when it first reaches
a
. Also, let the
minimal
process
X_{\mathsf{min}}
be defined as being identical to
X
up to (but not including) time
\sigma
and undefined from
\sigma
on. The strong Markov property of
X
allows then showing that the resolvent
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
of
X_{\mathsf{min}}
is related to the resolvent of
X
as follows

R_{\lambda}f(x)=R_{\lambda}^{0}f(x)+R_{\lambda}f(a)\,\mathpzc l_{\lambda}(x),\qquad x\not=a,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

(5.9)

Indeed, since
X_{\mathsf{min}}
and
X
are identical before
\sigma
, and
X
is strong Markov, we can write

\displaystyle R_{\lambda}f(x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}f(X_{\mathsf{min}}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\sigma}\mathsf{E}_{X(\sigma)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}f(X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

for
x\not=a
and
f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
. This gives (
5.9
) because
X_{\mathsf{min}}
is undefined after
\sigma
and
X(\sigma)=a
. Moreover, formula (
5.9
) can be extended by continuity to
x=a
, but then assumption (a) on the original process forces
R_{\lambda}^{0}f(a)=0,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
; keeping this in mind we have

R_{\lambda}f=R_{\lambda}^{0}f+R_{\lambda}f(a)\,\mathpzc l_{\lambda},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},\lambda>0.

(5.10)

It should be stressed here that
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
is not a Feller resolvent in the sense introduced in Section
2.1
: for
f
with
f(a)\not=0
we cannot have
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}f=f
because
\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}f(a)=0
for all
\lambda>0
. Nevertheless, by (
5.10
), for
f\in\mathfrak{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}}\coloneqq\{f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}\colon f(a)=0\}
,

\|\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}f-f\|\leq\|\lambda R_{\lambda}f-f\|+\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\||\lambda R_{\lambda}f(a)-f(a)|

and so
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}f=f
because
\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\|=1
and
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is a Feller resolvent. Moreover,
R_{\lambda}^{0}
maps
\mathfrak{C}(S)
into
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)
. As a result, there is a strongly continuous semigroup in
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)
that describes the minimal process. We note in passing that (
5.10
) implies

\mathpzc l_{\lambda}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}.

(5.11)

Indeed, there is at least one honest process that is identical to
X_{\mathsf{min}}
before
\sigma
(for example, the process that stays at
a
from
\sigma
on), and for such a process we have
\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S}
; multiplying both sides of (
5.10
) with
f=\mathsf{1}_{S}
by
\lambda
yields (
5.11
).

It is an equally important point, however, that the procedure described above can be reversed: if
\widetilde{X}
is a strong Markov process that coincides with the minimal process up to
\sigma
, then the resolvent
\widetilde{R}_{\lambda},\lambda>0
of
\widetilde{X}
is related to
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
as in (
5.12
) (and the reader may want to compare this result with (
3.9
)):

\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}f=R_{\lambda}^{0}f+\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}f(a)\,\mathpzc l_{\lambda},\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},\lambda>0.

(5.12)

This allows deducing Fellerian nature of such an
\widetilde{X}
as follows.

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{X}
is a strong Markov process that is identical to
X_{\mathsf{min}}
for
t<\sigma
. Then
\widetilde{X}
is a Feller process provided that

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}f(a)=f(a),\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

(5.13)

Proof.

By assumption (b) of Section
5.1
, the right-hand side of (
5.12
) is a continuous function whenever
f
is, that is, each
\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}
maps
\mathfrak{C}(S)
into itself. Moreover, since
\widetilde{X}
is a Markov process, these operators satisfy the Hilbert equation. They are also clearly nonnegative and condition 3. of the definition of Feller resolvent is fulfilled.

We are thus left with showing that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}f=f,f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
. Starting with the case where
f=\mathsf{1}_{S}
we note that condition (
5.12
), when combined with (
5.11
), renders

\|\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}-\mathsf{1}_{S}\|=(1-\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}(a))\|\ell_{\lambda}\|=1-\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}(a)
and this converges to
0
as
\lambda\to\infty
, by assumption (
5.13
). Therefore, it suffices to show that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}f=f,f\in\mathfrak{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}}
. However, for such
f
,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}f=f
, and so the desired result is a consequence of
(
5.12
) and (
5.13
); note again that
\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\|=1
. ∎

In practice, to show (
5.13
) it suffices to check that

\lim_{t\to 0+}f(\widetilde{X}(t))=f(a),\qquad f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}

(5.14)

almost surely conditional
on
\widetilde{X}(0)=a
. Indeed, if this conditions is satisfied, we also have
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}f(\widetilde{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t=f(a)
(almost surely as above) and then (
5.13
) follows by the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem. In particular, (
5.14
) is fulfilled if the lifetime of the process starting at
a
is positive with probability
1
.

5.4.
Fellerian nature of slowed down and killed processes

Proposition
5.1
allows proving that the slowed down process
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
of (
5.3
) has Feller property. For, as we have already stated,
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
is strong Markov. Moreover, for
t<\sigma
it is identical to
X_{\mathsf{min}}
and so (
5.12
) is in force. Finally, since
X
has càdlàg paths, so does
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}X
and thus condition (
5.14
) is satisfied.

The same proposition can be used to show that also the killed process
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
has Fellerian nature. Indeed, first of all,
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
is strong Markov and identical to
X_{\mathsf{min}}
for
t<\sigma
; hence (
5.12
) is again in force. Moreover, by assumption,
\lim_{t\to 0+}f(X(t))=f(a)
for all
f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
almost surely conditional on
X(0)=a
. However,
\tau>\sigma
almost surely, because
\zeta
is almost surely positive and a positive value cannot be reached by a continuous process starting at zero, that is, by the local time of
X
at
a
. Hence, before
\tau>0
, each path of
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X
is the same as that of
X
, and this yields
\lim_{t\to 0+}f(\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}X(t))=f(a)
almost surely as above, so that (
5.14
) is satisfied.

5.5.
Slowing down and killing the Walsh’s process

Let us come back to the Walsh’s process
W_{\beta}
as defined in (
4.14
). This process can be slowed down at
\mathfrak{0}
: to this end we define

W_{\alpha,\beta}\coloneqq W_{\beta}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}

(5.15)

where
A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the inverse of
A_{\alpha}
of (
5.2
) with
L
of (
4.15
)
being the symmetric local time of
W_{\beta}
at
\mathfrak{0}
. We know that the so-defined
W_{\alpha,\beta}
is a Feller process. Indeed,
W_{\beta}
satisfies conditions (a)–(b) of Section
5.1
; we have
S_{\mathsf{can}}\ni x\mathfrak{e}_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{x\mathfrak{e}_{i}}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\sigma}=\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x},x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
(this is just the restatement of the definition of
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
introduced in (
3.11
)), where
\sigma
is the first time
W_{\beta}
reaches the graph center
\mathfrak{0}
. Hence, the argument of the first part of Section
5.4
is applicable.

This process, in turn, can be
killed with the help of an exponential random variable
\zeta
with parameter
\gamma
that is independent of the process
W_{\alpha,\beta}
. The procedure is summarized in

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}W_{\alpha,\beta}(t),&L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)<\zeta,\\
\text{undefined},&L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)\geq\zeta.\end{cases}

(5.16)

The same result can be obtained by reversing the order of slowing down and killing, as follows. First we introduce

W_{0,\beta,\gamma}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}W_{\beta}(t),&L(t)<\zeta,\\
\text{undefined},&L(t)\geq\zeta,\end{cases}

(5.17)

where, again,
W_{\beta}
is the Walsh’s process of (
4.14
),
L
is the local time of (
4.6
), and
\zeta
, independent of
W_{\beta}
, is an exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma
. Then, we slow down
W_{0,\beta,\gamma}
at
0
:

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)\coloneqq W_{0,\beta,\gamma}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

(5.18)

It is easy to check, as in the general case, that both procedures lead to the same process. Moreover, since
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is a local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta}
, formula (
5.16
) is a particular case of (
5.8
). Thus, we conclude that

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
has Fellerian nature.

We end this section by noting that
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta}
, that is, almost surely,

L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\alpha,\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0.

(5.19)

Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\alpha,\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\beta}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\alpha}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}(s+\alpha L(s))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\beta}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,

with the last equality following by the fact that
L
may increase only if
W_{\beta}
is at
\mathfrak{0}
. This shows (
5.19
) by (
4.6
).

5.6.
The generator of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
; an approach via Dynkin’s operator

We have established that the formula

T_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)f(v)=\mathsf{E}_{v}f(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)),\qquad v\in S_{\mathsf{can}},f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}),t\geq 0

defines a Feller semigroup
\left(T_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
(see Section
4.2.1
for the definition of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
); the related process is that of Walsh but slowed down at
\mathfrak{0}
with parameter
\alpha
and killed with intensity
\gamma
. Our goal will be reached once we show that
this semigroup is generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
of (
3.5
).

To this end, we will use the notion of
characteristic operator of Dynkin
. To recall, under quite general mild conditions the characteristic operator extends the generator of a Markov process
(see
[
23
]
* §3 of Chapter 5), and coincides with the generator of a Feller process (see
[
58
]
*p. 256). Thus, denoting by
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
the generator of
\left(T_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, we have

\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}f(v)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mathsf{E}_{v}f(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon}))-f(v)}{\mathsf{E}_{v}\tau_{\varepsilon}},\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}),v\in S_{\mathsf{can}},

(5.20)

where
\tau_{\varepsilon}
is the first time
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
exits the open ball centered at
v
with radius
\varepsilon
.
We will argue that
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
extends
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
.

For, if
v
lies away from the center and is thus of the form
v=x\mathfrak{e}_{i}
for some
x>0
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,
\tau_{\varepsilon}
is the time needed for a standard Brownian motion to exit from the interval
(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)
(as long as
\varepsilon<x
). Indeed, because Brownian paths are continuous, the process cannot touch the graph’s center before
\tau_{\varepsilon}
, and thus before that time it behaves like a one-dimensional Brownian motion on the
i
th edge; in particular, before this time it is neither slowed down nor killed. Hence,
\mathsf{E}_{v}\tau_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{2}
(this can be deduced e.g. from
[
41
]
*Problem 8.14, p. 100; other ways of reaching this conclusion are listed in
[
45
]
*p. 10). Moreover, using continuity of Brownian paths again, we see that
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})
is either
(x-\varepsilon)\mathfrak{e}_{i}
or
(x+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{e}_{i}
, and by symmetry both cases happen with the same probability. Thus, the right-hand side of (
5.20
) reduces to
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\frac{1}{2}f((x+\varepsilon)\mathfrak{e}_{i})+\frac{1}{2}f((x-\varepsilon)\mathfrak{e}_{i})-f(x\mathfrak{e}_{i})}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\Delta)
this equals
\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(v)
.

To treat the case where
v
is the graph’s center, we need to gather some helpful information.

(i)

First of all, Lemma 3.4 in
[
45
]
says that, as long as
\gamma=0
,

\tau_{\varepsilon}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}+\alpha L\circ\sigma_{\varepsilon}=A_{\alpha}(\sigma_{\varepsilon})

where
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is the time needed for a reflecting Brownian motion started at
0
to reach
\varepsilon
for the first time.

(ii)

Using
[
41
]
*Problem 8.14, p. 100 alluded to above we see that
\mathsf{E}_{0}\sigma_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{2}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{E}_{0}L\circ\sigma_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
. Indeed, the second part of (
1.5
) holds for all
t
for almost all paths and can thus be also used with
t
replaced by
\tau_{\varepsilon}
to yield
\varepsilon=B(\tau_{\varepsilon})+L(\tau_{\varepsilon})
. Since Brownian motion is a martingale, a reference to Doob’s Optional Sampling Theorem reveals that
\mathsf{E}_{0}B(\tau_{\varepsilon})=0
, completing the proof of the claim.

(iii)

By (i),
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(\tau_{\varepsilon})=L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})
, and so
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{0}}(L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(\tau_{\varepsilon})\leq\zeta)=\mathsf{P}_{0}(L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})\leq\zeta)
— see
(
5.16
). Since
\zeta
is exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma
, the latter probability equals

\phantom{===}\int_{0}^{\infty}\gamma\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma t}\mathsf{P}_{0}(L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})\leq t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma t}\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{P}_{0}(L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})\leq t)=\mathsf{E}_{0}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})}.

The Laplace transform of
L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})
, in turn, can be obtained as a special case of formula 2.3.3 on p. 356 in
[
21
]
(this is formula 2.3.3 on p. 362 in the 2015 corrected printing of this monograph); to this end, put
z=\varepsilon
,
x=r=0
and take the limit as
\alpha\to 0
, to obtain

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{0}}(L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(\tau_{\varepsilon})\leq\zeta)=\mathsf{E}_{0}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})}=\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon\gamma};

the same formula for the Laplace transform of
L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})
can be found on p. 429 of
[
41
]
; a semigroup-theoretic proof of the fact that
L(\sigma_{\varepsilon})
is exponentially distributed with parameter
\varepsilon
is given in
[
13
]
.

With this information under our belt, we can calculate
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}f(\mathfrak{0})
. Since, conditional on
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(\tau_{\varepsilon})\leq\zeta
, the process
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
started at
\mathfrak{0}
is at time
\tau_{\varepsilon}
at
\varepsilon\mathfrak{e}_{i}
with probability
\beta_{i}
,

\mathsf{E}_{\mathfrak{0}}f(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon}))=\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{0}}(L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(\tau_{\varepsilon})\leq\zeta)\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f(\varepsilon\mathfrak{e}_{i})

Thus, by (ii) and (iii), in the case of
v=\mathfrak{0},
the right-hand side of (
5.20
) reduces to
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon\gamma}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f(\varepsilon\mathfrak{e}_{i})-f(\mathfrak{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}+\alpha\varepsilon}
. If
\alpha>0
, this limit, for
f
that have the first derivative at
\mathfrak{0}
along each edge, equals
\alpha^{-1}(\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f_{i}^{\prime}(0)-\gamma f(\mathfrak{0}))
.
Now, for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})
this coincides with
\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})
, establishing that
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
extends
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
. Similarly, as can be checked by de l’Hospital’s rule, in the case of
\alpha=0
the above limit is equal to
\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})+\gamma(\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0))
for
f\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
, and reduces to
\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})
for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})
.

However,
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
, being a Feller generator, satisfies the positive-maximum principle, and thus
cannot be a proper extension of a generator (see e.g.
[
40
]
*p. 377 or
[
58
]
*p. 242). Since
\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
extends the generator
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
,

\mathfrak{H}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma},

as desired.

5.7.
The generator of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
; an approach via resolvent

The fact that
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
can also be established
by looking at its resolvent, but then we need to use the results of
[
38
,
45
]
in a more fundamental way than before.

To elaborate on this succinct statement, let us begin by noting that the process of Walsh can be seen as a concatenation of
{\mathpzc k}
reflected Brownian motions on the edges having common local time at the graph’s center — this is the intrinsic meaning of Theorem
4.3
. In
W_{\alpha,\beta}
of (
5.15
) each of these reflected Brownian motions is slowed down at
0
, and then in
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
of (
5.16
) — killed with intensity
\gamma
. It is one of the main findings of
[
38
,
45
]
that the first procedure transforms each of those reflected Brownian motions to the process generated by the one dimensional Laplace operator (see Section
1.1
) with the boundary condition
\frac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0)=f^{\prime}(0)
, whereas the second — to the process generated by the same operator with the boundary condition
\frac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0)-f^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(0)=0
. The resolvent of the latter operator, let us call it
\mathfrak{A}
, is well known and given by (see e.g.
[
17
]
*pp. 17-18)

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{A}\right)^{-1}f(x)=C(f)\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{2\lambda}x}+D(f)\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}-\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{x}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}(x-y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

for all
x\geq 0
and
f\in C[0,\infty]
, where
C(f)
and
D(f)
are constants defined as follows:

\displaystyle C(f)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}y}f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad D(f)=\frac{(\sqrt{2\lambda}-\alpha\lambda-\gamma)C+\alpha f(0)}{\alpha\lambda+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma}.

In particular,

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{A}\right)^{-1}f(0)=C(f)+D(f)=\frac{2\sqrt{2\lambda}C(f)+\alpha f(0)}{\alpha\lambda+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma}.

Coming back to
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
: using the strong Markov property as in (
5.10
), we obtain the following formula for its resolvent

R_{\lambda}f=R_{\lambda}^{0}f+R_{\lambda}f(\mathfrak{0})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})

where
R_{\lambda}^{0}
and
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
are the resolvent of the minimal process and the Laplace transform of the time needed to reach the graph center, respectively (see (
3.10
)).
Moreover, since
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
started at
\mathfrak{0}
is at the
i
th edge with probability
\beta_{i}
and while at this edge behaves like the process generated by
\mathfrak{A}
described above, we have

R_{\lambda}f(\mathfrak{0})=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{A}\right)^{-1}f_{i}(0)=\frac{2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(f)+\alpha f(\mathfrak{0})}{\alpha\lambda+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma}

(5.21)

with
C_{i}(f)
defined in (
3.7
). It is now easy to see that
R_{\lambda}f(\mathfrak{0})
is a particular case of
E(f)
introduced in (
3.8
) (recall that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
), and thus conclude that the resolvent of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
coincides with the resolvent of the generator
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
of (
3.5
), as was expected.

5.8.
The degenerate case of some or all
\beta_{i}=0

Throughout Section
5
, we have assumed that
\beta_{i}>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and, without loss of generality, have normalized them to have
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
. Now is the time to clarify what happens when some or all
\beta_{i}
s vanish.

Let us first consider the case where
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i
in a proper subset
\mathpzc L
of
{\mathpzc K}
. Then
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is a function of the degenerate process
W_{\beta}
of Section
4.3
: by definition,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is
W_{\beta}
slowed down at
\mathfrak{0}
as in (
5.15
) and killed as in (
5.16
) — the local time of the degenerate Walsh’s process coincides with the local time of the regular Walsh’s process on a smaller graph with
\#({\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc L)
edges.

The second case is that of
\mathpzc L={\mathpzc K}
. Here, we must have
\alpha>0
for otherwise (
3.3
) is violated. To describe this scenario, we begin with the process which at each edge behaves like a Brownian motion but upon hitting
\mathfrak{0}
for the first time stays at this point for ever. It is easy to check that the generator of this process is
\mathfrak{G}_{1,0,0}=\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,0,0}
. Then, we modify the process by requiring that it is killed when its local time at
\mathfrak{0}
(which is the same as the regular time) exceeds an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma
. Again, a simple analysis of the related Dynkin operator shows that the modified process is generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{1,0,\gamma}=\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,0,\alpha\gamma}
.

5.9.
Resolvents, semigroups and cosine families

In the preceding sections we have established that the process related to the boundary condition (
3.2
) with
\mathpzc m=0
is Walsh’s process slowed down at the graph center and killed when the local time at the graph center exceeds an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma
. This fact can also be seen, but perhaps less vividly, directly from (
3.12
).

First of all, if
\gamma
is zero,
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}
is a zero function, as a reflection of the fact that the process is then honest. Moreover,
\lambda\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}
is the Laplace transform of the time needed for a Brownian motion starting at
x
to reach
0
(see
[
39
]
* Eq. 5) p. 26,
[
41
]
*Eq. (8.6) p. 96 or the original
[
49
]
*pp. 221-223).
It can be argued, moreover, that

\lambda\mapsto\frac{\gamma}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}+\gamma},

(5.22)

is completely monotone, and thus is the Laplace transform of the distribution of an
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-valued random variable. Therefore,

\mathpzc l_{\lambda}(x)
describes the distribution of the sum of two nonnegative random variables: one is the time to reach
0
from
x
, the other — the time spent at the boundary before the process is no longer defined. In particular, for
\alpha=1
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
, (
5.22
) reduces to
\lambda\mapsto\frac{\gamma}{\lambda+\gamma}
, which we recognize as the Laplace transform of the exponential distribution with parameter
\gamma
(see Section
5.8
, above).
Likewise, for
\alpha=0
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
, (
5.22
) reads
\lambda\mapsto\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma}
which is the Laplace transform of the time needed for the Lévy local time to exceed an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma
, see
[
39
]
*p. 45, Eq. 1. For
\alpha>0
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
, the set of times the process spends at the boundary has positive Lebesgue measure, but contains no intervals, see
[
50
]
*p. 128.

An additional insight into the process can also be gained from the analysis of the related cosine families and semigroups. Notably, see
[
56
]
, there is an elegant subspace of
\mathsf{F}
that plays a similar role for the cosine family related to
W_{\alpha,\beta,0}
as
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
of (
4.3
) plays for
W_{\beta}
. As a result, there are counterparts of (
4.4
) and (
4.1
): to obtain a formula for the cosine family, or the semigroup, related to
W_{\alpha,\beta,0}
, it suffices to replace
C_{\mathsf{ref}}
by
C_{\mathsf{sticky}}
and
T_{\mathsf{ref}}
by
T_{\mathsf{sticky}}
in the formulae just mentioned (see also the already cited
[
4
]
). The same is true in the case of
W_{0,\beta,\gamma}
with
\gamma>0
, but the general case seems to be still unknown.

We note also the paper
[
32
]
, where existence of cosine families related to
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
was established by a method of decomposition of resolvents inspired by Example 3.59 in
[
50
]
.

5.10.
Lumping edges together

In Section
4.2.1
we have seen, as a corollary to Theorem
4.2
,
that by lumping edges of a star graph together, we transform Walsh’s process to another Walsh’s process, and the two processes have the same symmetric local time at
\mathfrak{0}
. Here, we extend this result to the case where additionally slowing down at
\mathfrak{0}
and killing are allowed.

To this end, let

W_{\beta}
be Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta
, and let, as in Section
4.2.1
, for some natural number
\mathpzc n\leq{\mathpzc k}
,

\Psi
map
{\mathpzc K}
onto
\mathpzc N\coloneqq\{1,\dots,\mathpzc n\}
.
We know that the process
(X_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
defined by

X_{j}(t)=\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\beta})_{i}(t),\qquad j\in\mathpzc N,t\geq 0

where
(W_{\beta})_{i}
is the
i
th coordinate of
W_{\beta}
,
is then Walsh’s process also, but with the modified parameter
\widetilde{\beta}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
, where
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}\beta_{i},j\in\mathpzc N
. Moreover, the symmetric local times of
W_{\beta}
and
W_{\widetilde{\beta}}=X
at
\mathfrak{0}
coincide.

We claim that, for any
\alpha>0
, the process
(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
defined by

Y_{j}(t)=\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\alpha,\beta})_{i}(t),\qquad j\in\mathpzc N,t\geq 0

is
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}}
. Indeed,
W_{\alpha,\beta}
is
W_{\beta}
slowed down at
\mathfrak{0}
, as revealed in (
5.15
). Thus, for
j\in\mathpzc N
and
t\geq 0
,

Y_{j}(t)=\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\alpha})_{i}(A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t))=X_{j}(A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t))=(W_{\widetilde{\beta}})_{j}(A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)).

Since
L
featured in (
5.2
) (and defined in (
4.6
)) coincides simultaneously with the symmetric local time of
W_{\widetilde{\beta}}
, this relation shows that
Z=W_{\widetilde{\beta}}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}=W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}},
as claimed.

Similarly, for any
\gamma>0
, the process
(Z_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
defined by

Z_{j}(t)=\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})_{i}(t),\qquad j\in\mathpzc N,t\geq 0

is
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma}
. Indeed, as explained in (
5.16
),
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is identical to
W_{\alpha,\beta}
before the time, say,
\tau
, when
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
reaches the level of an independent random variable
\zeta
that is exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma
, and is undefined later. Hence, for
i\in\mathpzc N
,
Z_{j}(t)
is either undefined (for
t\geq\tau
) or equals
\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}(W_{\alpha,\beta})_{i}(t)=W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}}(t)
(for
t<\tau
). Since
L
is also the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}}
, this means that
Z
is
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma}
, as announced above.

Notably, even though above we have tacitly assumed that all
\beta_{i}
are positive and sum up to
1
, it can be argued that the principle of lumping the edges together applies also to the degenerate case of Section
5.8
; we leave the details to the readers (see also Sections
6.8
and
7.4
, further down).

6.
The case of non-zero but finite
\mathpzc m

In this section, we turn to the case where
\mathpzc m
in (
3.2
) is non-zero but finite, and so the condition in question can be rewritten as

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=\delta\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x),

where
\mu\coloneqq\frac{\mathpzc m}{\mathpzc m(S_{\mathfrak{0}})}
is a probability measure and
\delta\coloneqq\mathpzc m(S_{\mathfrak{0}})
.
Equivalently,

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+(\gamma+\delta)f(\mathfrak{0})=\delta\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

(6.1)

showing in particular that a non-zero
\mathpzc m
automatically contributes a term to the coefficient of
f(\mathfrak{0})
. As a result, there are two natural interpretations of
\gamma
.

(a)

In the first of these, the process related to (
6.1
) is seen as a modification of the process related to

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\delta f(\mathfrak{0})=\delta\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu;

(6.2)

when the local time at
0
of the latter exceeds an independent exponential time with parameter
\gamma
, the former is killed, that is, no longer defined.

(b)

In the second interpretation, the process related to (
6.1
) is seen as a continuation of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}
: at a random time when the latter ceases to be defined, the former is ‘resurrected’ with probability
\frac{\delta}{\delta+\gamma}
and starts all over again, forgetting the past, at a random point with distribution
\mu
.

Figure 3.
A Brownian motion on the nonnegative half-line with jumps from the boundary where
x=0
. When the local time at
x=0
exceeds an independent random variable, the process starts all over again at a random point.

Of course, interpretation (b) applies also to the process related to (
6.2
): whenever the process generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\delta}
ceases to be defined, the process related to (
6.2
) is resurrected and starts all over again at a random point. Figure
3
illustrates this procedure in the analogous situation of Brownian motion on the positive half-line. In the case of star graph the process can not only restart at any of the edges but also each excursion from zero can lead to a different edge; otherwise, the analogy is complete.

Details of the stochastic construction are given below.
As in the previous section, we start with a rather general situation to specialize to the case of processes on star graphs later.

6.1.
Concatenating infinitely many copies of one Feller process

Let the space
S
, the process
X
, the point
a
and the local time
L
be as in Section
5.1
. Guided by the interpretation (b) presented above, given a probability measure
\mu
on
S
and real parameters
\gamma\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, we construct a Feller process
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
which initially is identical to
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
(see Section
5.2
), but when
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
ceases to be defined
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
can be continued: with probability
p\coloneqq\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
it starts anew at a random point distributed according to the measure
\mu
, and with probability
1-p
is killed.

Our construction uses the following building blocks — the construction of the type presented below has apparently been devised by Ikeda, Nagasawa and Watanable in
[
34
]
; see also the recent
[
fitzsi
]
.

(i)

Processes
X_{n},n\geq 1
that are independent and have the same transition probabilities as
X
, and
L_{n},n\geq 1
that are their local times at
a
. However, whereas the initial distribution of
X_{1}
is chosen arbitrarily, all the initial distributions of
X_{n},n\geq 2
are the same and identical to
\mu
.

(ii)

Random variables
\zeta_{n},n\geq 1
that are mutually independent, and independent of
X_{n}
s, with common exponential distribution of parameter
\gamma+\delta
.

(iii)

Bernoulli random variables
\epsilon_{n},n\geq 2
that are mutually independent, and independent of
X_{n}
s and
\zeta_{n}
s, such that
\mathsf{P}(\epsilon_{n}=1)=p
and
\mathsf{P}(\epsilon_{n}=0)=1-p
.

In terms of these, we first define
\vartriangle_{n}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon L_{n}(t)=\zeta_{n}\},n\geq 1
as the moments when the local times at
a
reach exponential levels, and then, if
X_{n}
s are honest, let

\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X(t)\coloneqq X_{n+1}(t-\tau_{n}),\quad t\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}),\qquad n=0,\dots,n_{0}-1,

(6.3)

where

\tau_{0}\coloneqq 0,\tau_{n}\coloneqq{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle_{k},n\geq 1
and
n_{0}\geq 1
is the smallest index such that
\epsilon_{n}=0
. In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
is left undefined from
\tau_{n_{0}}
onwards. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}L
given by

\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}L(t)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{k}(\vartriangle_{k})+L_{n+1}(t-\tau_{n}),\,t\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}),\,n=0,\dots,n_{0}-1,

(6.4)

together with the convention (
5.7
) that allows extending the definition beyond
\tau_{n_{0}}
,
is a local time of
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
at
a
.
Also, if additionally
L_{n}(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|X_{n}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,
for
t\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, then, clearly,
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}L(t)

=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,
for
t\geq 0
.

If
X_{n}
s are not honest, these formulae need to be modified as follows: we agree that if there is an
n<n_{0}
and an
s\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1})
such that
X_{n}(s)
is undefined, then so is
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
from that time on. However,
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}L
is continued beyond this time as in (
5.7
).

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
is strong Markov, because so is
\mathcal{K}_{\delta+\gamma}X
. It is also honest whenever
X_{n}
s are honest and
\gamma=0
. We claim, moreover, that it possesses the Feller property. To prove this, we note first of all that
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
extends the minimal process (the process that is undefined from the moment
\sigma
when
X
reaches
a
for the first time), and thus Proposition
5.1
can be used.
Furthermore, in Section
5.4
we have established that
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
is a Feller process, and in particular has càdlàg paths. It follows that, almost surely conditional on
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
starting at
a
,
\lim_{t\to 0+}f(\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X(t))=f(a)
; hence also
\lim_{t\to 0+}f(\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X(t))=f(a)
because
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
is identical to
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
for all the times smaller than
\tau\coloneqq\tau_{1}
, and
\tau>0
. Thus, (
5.14
) is satisfied, completing the proof.

For a future use we note that the resolvent of
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
, denoted below
\widetilde{R}_{\lambda},\lambda>0
, can be informatively expressed in terms of the resolvent
R_{\lambda}^{\tau},\lambda>0
of
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma+\delta}X
. For, using strong Markov property of
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
as in Section
5.3
(this time at
\tau
, not at
\sigma
), we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g(x)

\displaystyle=R_{\lambda}^{\tau}g(x)+\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\tau}\mathsf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X(\tau)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}g(\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle=R_{\lambda}^{\tau}g(x)+p\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}(x)\int_{S}\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu

with
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}(x)\coloneqq\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\tau}
, because at
\tau
the process
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
starts all over again with probability
p
and its starting distribution is
\mu
. Next, we integrate both sides of the so-obtained relation with respect to
\mu
to see that
\int_{S}\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\frac{\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{\tau}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{1-p\int_{S}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
; the denominator here is never zero, even if
p=1
, because
\tau>0
and so, for any
x\in S
,
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}(x)\leq\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}(a)<1
. Since
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}^{\tau}\mathsf{1}_{S}
, this yields

\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g=R_{\lambda}^{\tau}g+\frac{\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{\tau}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{1-p+p\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{\tau}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{\tau},\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

(6.5)

6.2.
Two alternative constructions

Alternatively, we can start from processes of point (i) and two sequences, say,
\left(\upzeta_{1,n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
and
\left(\upzeta_{2,n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of random variables (jointly independent and independent of the processes), the first of these being exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma
, the second — exponentially distributed with parameter
\delta
. Then
\zeta_{n}\coloneqq\upzeta_{1,n}\wedge\upzeta_{2,n},n\geq 1
are exponential with parameter
\gamma+\delta
, as in point (ii). Moreover,
\epsilon_{n}\coloneqq[\upzeta_{2,n}\leq\upzeta_{1,n}],n\geq 1
(where
[\cdot]
is the Iverson bracket) have Bernoulli distribution with parameter
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
and are independent of
\zeta_{n}
s, as in point (iii). Hence, the rest of the construction proceeds as before.

We may also construct
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
in two steps, by building
\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X
first and then killing it with the help of transformation
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
— see interpretation (a) at the beginning of Section
6
. Notably, the first step does not require the Bernoulli variables
\epsilon_{n}
s, for the process
\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X
is always restarted; similarly, in the alternative construction, we need merely the sequence
\left(\upzeta_{2,n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
, because
\gamma=0
corresponds to
\upzeta_{1,n}
s being infinite. Moreover, the second step requires just one additional exponentially distributed random variable.

The situation resembles the following experiment. Let
\tau_{n},n\geq 1
be independent exponential random variables with common parameter
\gamma>0
, and let
p\in(0,1)
be given. We wait for the exponential time
\tau_{1}
to perform a random experiment which with probability
p
results in a success. If we fail, we wait for the additional exponential time
\tau_{2}
to perform the random experiment for the second time, etc.: if we fail
n
times, we wait for the time
\tau_{n+1}
to have our next chance. Then, the time to the first success turns out to be exponential with parameter
p\gamma
.

To prove that

\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X

(6.6)

we suppose the latter process was constructed with the help of sequences
\left(\upzeta_{1,n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
and
\left(\upzeta_{2,n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
and that for the construction of the former, besides the same copies of
X
, the second of these sequences is used. Also, let
\zeta
be an independent random variable of exponential distribution with parameter
\gamma
, and assume initially that processes
X_{n},n\geq 1
are honest.

Initially, up to the first jump or killing,
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X
coincides with
X_{1}
, and the time of jump or killing is the moment when the local time of
X_{1}
reaches the level of
\zeta\wedge\upzeta_{2,1}
; if
\zeta>\upzeta_{2,1}
, we see a jump, otherwise the process is killed. The same is true of
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
except that we wait to the moment when the local time of
X_{1}
reaches the level of
\upzeta_{1,1}\wedge\upzeta_{2,1}
; if
\upzeta_{1,1}>\upzeta_{2,1}
, we see a jump, otherwise the process is killed. Since
\zeta
and
\upzeta_{1,1}
have the same distribution, so do
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X
and
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
up to the first jump or killing. Suppose next that, for some natural
n
, both processes have the same distribution up to the time of
n
th jump or killing. We can also assume that neither of the processes has been killed as yet, because otherwise there is nothing to prove. This means, on the one hand, that
\upzeta_{1,k}\geq\upzeta_{2,k},k=1,\dots,n
and, on the other, that
\zeta>\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}L(\tau_{n})
,
where
\tau_{n}
is the moment when the local time of
X_{n}
reaches the level of
\upzeta_{2,n}\leq\upzeta_{1,n}
. By the memoryless property of exponential distribution, conditional on the event just described,
\widetilde{\zeta}\coloneqq\zeta-\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}L(\tau_{n})
is still exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma
. Moreover, the next jump or killing in
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}X
will occur at the moment when the local time reaches the level of
\widetilde{\zeta}\wedge\upzeta_{2,n+1}
; if
\widetilde{\zeta}>\upzeta_{2,n+1}
we will see a jump, otherwise the process will be killed. Since a similar description applies to
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}X
, except that the role of
\zeta
is played by
\upzeta_{1,n+1}
, this shows that the processes in question have the same distribution also to the
n+1
jump or killing event, completing the proof by induction. To treat the case where
X_{n}
s need not be honest, it suffices to note that in this case additional killing events can occur between those considered above, but that their distribution is the same in both cases.

6.3.
Operations of killing, slowing down and concatenating commute

As explained in Section
5.2
, transformations
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
in a sense commute. An argument similar to that used to establish (
6.6
) shows that so do
\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
(and their composition amounts to
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}
). It can also be argued that
\mathcal{C}_{0,\delta,\mu}
commutes with
S_{\alpha}
and that the family of transformations
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}
indexed by
\gamma
and
\delta
with fixed
\mu
has the following semigroup property:

\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}\mathcal{C}_{\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\mu}X\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{C}_{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime},\delta+\delta^{\prime},\mu}X.

6.4.
Definition of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}

Having covered the general scenario, we return to the case of Brownian motions on star graphs. Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
be given and assume additionally that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
.

Then

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is simply defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}W_{\alpha,\beta}
. Therefore, we have the following special case of (
6.5
):

\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}g+M(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda},\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}

(6.7)

where
\widetilde{R}_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is a temporary notation for the resolvent of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
,

M(g)\coloneqq\frac{\delta\int_{S}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\gamma+\delta\lambda\int_{S}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}

(6.8)

and

(\mathpzc l_{\lambda})_{i}(x)=\frac{\gamma+\delta}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma+\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x},\qquad x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

(6.9)

is the function introduced (
3.12
), but with
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}{\beta_{i}}
equal to
1
and
\gamma
replaced by
\gamma+\delta
.

6.5.
The generator of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}

We claim that
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is generated by the operator

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu},

defined as the restriction of the Laplace operator
\Delta
to the set of functions
f\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
such that
Ff=\delta\int_{S}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu
, where
F
was introduced in (
3.4
). To show this, it suffices to check that
\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}
of (
6.7
) coincides with
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}\right)^{-1}
. Moreover,
the solution to the resolvent equation for
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
, if it exists, is necessarily unique, because
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
satisfies the positive maximum principle (see Appendix
12.2
), and is thus dissipative. Hence,
it suffices to show that
f\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}g
solves the resolvent equation for this operator.

Now, since both
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}g
and
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}
belong to
C^{2}(S)
, so does
f
. Moreover, for the functional
F
of (
3.4
) (with
\gamma
replaced by
\gamma+\delta
)
we have
F\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}g=0
and
F\mathpzc l_{\lambda}=\gamma+\delta
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle Ff-\delta\int_{S}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=-\delta\int_{S}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu+M(g)\,(\delta+\gamma-\delta\int_{S}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\mu),

this in turn is zero, because
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma+\delta}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S}
. Hence,
f
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu})
. Finally, since
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}
is an eigenvector of
\Delta
corresponding to
\lambda>0
, we check that
\lambda f-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}f=g
, completing the proof.

6.6.
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
as a function of Brownian motion and subordinators

Next, we want to see that to construct a
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is suffices to have just one
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional Brownian motion, as opposed to its infinitely many independent copies used in Sections
6.1
–
6.2
. This will lead to a very handy formula expressing
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
in terms of this Brownian motion and certain subordinators.
In this section we assume that
\beta_{i}>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and, for convenience, normalize our parameters so that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
; we will get rid of this additional assumption in Section
6.7
.

6.6.1.
The construction

We start with the case where
\alpha=\gamma=0
, that is, with the process
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
, where
\delta>0
and
\mu
is a measure on
S_{\mathsf{can}}
(see Section
4.2.1
). This process behaves like
W_{\beta}
up to the moment when its local time at
\mathfrak{0}
reaches the level of an independent, exponential random variable with parameter
\delta>0
, and then starts all over again at a random point with distribution
\mu
. Let

(
\upalpha
)

B
be a
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional Wiener process starting at a random point of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
,

(
\upbeta
)

\left(\zeta_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of independent, exponentially distributed random variables with parameter
\delta>0
that are independent of
B
also, and

(
\upgamma
)

(\xi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of independent random variables with common distribution
\mu
that are independent of
B
and
\left(\zeta_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
too.

As a preparation for the main construction, using these building blocks, we produce a sequence
\left(B_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of Wiener processes such that
B_{1}=B
and
B_{n}(0)=\xi_{n-1},n\geq 2
. To proceed by induction we let
B_{1}\coloneqq B
, and, assuming that
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
are already constructed, define
\vartriangle_{n}\coloneqq\min\{t\geq 0\colon L_{n}(t)=\zeta_{n}\}
, where
L_{n}
is the local time of
B_{n}
at
\mathfrak{0}
. Theorem
4.3
tells us then that there is a unique collection of processes
T_{n,i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
such that a counterpart of (
4.13
) holds. Moreover, as noted at the end of Section
4.2.2
, we can take

B_{n+1}\coloneqq(B_{n+1,i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
where

B_{n+1,i}(t)\coloneqq\xi_{n,i}+B_{n,i}(T_{n,i}(\vartriangle_{n})+t)-B_{n,i}(T_{n,i}(\vartriangle_{n})),\qquad t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}

B_{n,i}
s are coordinates of
B_{n}
and
\xi_{n,i}
s are coordinates of
\xi_{n}
, as a Wiener process starting at
\xi_{n}
and independent of
B_{k}(t),t\in[0,\Delta_{k}],1\leq k\leq n.

This completes the construction of
\left(B_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
. We note that the processes
(B_{n}{(t\wedge\vartriangle_{n}))}_{t\geq 0}
,
n\geq 1
are independent.

With this sequence under our belt, we proceed as follows. For
n\geq 1
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, let

L_{n,i}(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}(-B_{n,i}(s)\vee 0)\quad\text{and}\quad B^{\textnormal{ref}}_{n,i}(t)\coloneqq B_{n,i}(t)+L_{n,i}(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

Then, by Theorem
4.3
, each

X_{n}\coloneqq(B^{\textnormal{ref}}_{n,i}\circ T_{n,i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}},\qquad n\geq 1,

(6.10)

is a Walsh’s process with parameter
\beta
, which for
n\geq 2
starts at
\xi_{n-1}
. Therefore,
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}

can be constructed by means of the following formula, being a particular case of (
6.3
):

W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}(t)\coloneqq X_{n+1}(t-\tau_{n}),\quad t\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}),\qquad n\geq 0,

where

\tau_{0}\coloneqq 0
and
\tau_{n}\coloneqq{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle_{k},n\geq 1
. Moreover,

L(t)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{k}(\vartriangle_{k})+L_{n+1}(t-\tau_{n}),\qquad t\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}),n\geq 0,

(6.11)

is its local time, and since
L_{n}(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|X_{n}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,
for
t\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, we have

L(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0.

(6.12)

Finally, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,

T_{i}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}T_{k,i}(\vartriangle_{i})+T_{n+1,i}(t-\tau_{n}),\qquad t\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}),n\geq 0,

(6.13)

can be interpreted as the time spent by
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
at the
i
th edge of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
up to
t\geq 0
. An induction argument shows that
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathpzc k}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
.

6.6.2.
Representation via subordinators

Let, here and in what follows, by abuse of notations,
B_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
denote the
i
th coordinate of the original
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional process
B
; previously,
B_{n},n\geq 1
denoted the
n
th copy of
B
, but we do not need this notation any longer. Also, let
(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}
be the
i
th coordinate of
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
.
Since, for each
n\geq 1
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,
L_{n,i}=\beta_{i}L_{n}
, an induction argument can be used to show that

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}(t)=B_{i}(T_{i}(t))+\beta_{i}L(t)+\sum_{\{k\geq 1\colon\tau_{k}\leq t\}}\xi_{k,i},\qquad t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}.

This relation, in turn, can be rewritten as

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}(t)=B_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)+\beta_{i}L(t)+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N\circ L(t)}}\xi_{k,i},\qquad t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}.

where
N
is the Poisson process constructed by means of
\left(\zeta_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
, that is,
N(t)
is the largest integer
n\geq 1
such that
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\zeta_{k}\leq t
; if there is no integer with this property,
N(t)=0
. Indeed, since
L_{{j}}(\vartriangle_{{j}})=\zeta_{{j}},{{j}}\geq 1
, we have
L(\tau_{k})=\sum_{{{j}}=1}^{k}L_{{j}}(\vartriangle_{{j}})=\sum_{{{j}}=1}^{k}\zeta_{{j}},k\geq 1
and thus,
L
being nondecreasing, condition
\tau_{k}\leq t
implies
\sum_{{{j}}=1}^{k}\zeta_{{j}}\leq L(t)
. Moreover, since
\tau_{k}
is the smallest of
t
such that
L(t)=\sum_{{{j}}=1}^{k}\zeta_{{j}}
, the converse is also true, and so the conditions in question are equivalent. But this means that

\tau_{k}\leq t
iff
k\leq N\circ L(t)
, establishing the relation.

We further see that

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=B_{i}\circ T_{i}+U_{i}\circ L\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},

where
U_{i}
is the subordinator given by

U_{i}(t)\coloneqq\beta_{i}t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}}\xi_{k,i},\qquad t\geq 0.

(6.14)

This relation reveals that (for each path) the pair
((W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i},L)
is a solution to the generalized Skorokhod problem for
B_{i}\circ T_{i}
with noise
U_{i}
. Indeed,
(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}
is nonnegative and, clearly,
\int_{0}^{\infty}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{B_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}L(t)=0
. Since
B_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not have jumps, and
U_{i}
is strictly increasing with
\lim_{t\to\infty}U_{i}(t)=\infty
, the solution is unique and thus we have (see Section
2.3
)
L(t)=U_{i}^{-1}\circ K_{i}(t),t\geq 0
where
K_{i}(t)\coloneqq\sup_{s\in[0,t]}(-B_{i}\circ T_{i}(s)\vee 0)=\sup_{u\in[0,T_{i}(t)]}(-B_{i}(u)\vee 0)
.
Introducing

\mathcal{L}_{i}(t)\coloneqq\sup_{s\in[0,t]}(-B_{i}(s)\vee 0),\qquad t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}

(6.15)

(to be distinguished from
L_{{j}}
featured in e.g. (
6.11
)), we see finally that
K_{i}=\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
and

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(6.16)

Our discussion is summarized in the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

There is a family
T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
of nondecreasing nonnegative processes with continuous paths such that

1.

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathpzc k}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
,

2.

W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
is represented as in (
6.16
) for
U_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}
defined in (
6.14
) and (
6.15
), respectively, and

3.

U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Moreover, conditions 1. and 3. determine
T_{i}
s uniquely (a.s.), and the common value of
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
coincides with the local time
L
of
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
— see (
6.11
) and (
6.12
).

The existence of the family described above has already been established; all we need to prove is the uniqueness. To this end, we present the following lemma that seems to be of some interest in itself.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\ell_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
be nondecreasing functions
\ell_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that do not have common levels of constancy (this, by definition, means that there are no indexes
i\not=j
and numbers
0\leq a<b
and
0\leq c<d
such that
\ell_{i}(a)=\ell_{i}(b)=\ell_{j}(c)=\ell_{j}(d)
). Then
there is at most one collection
t_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
of nondecreasing functions
t_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(s)=s,s\geq 0
and
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Proof.

Striving for a contradiction, suppose that there are two different collections, say,
t_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\hat{t}_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, of functions that satisfy the conditions listed in the lemma. Then, for some
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
s\geq 0
, we have
a\coloneqq t_{i}(s)\not=\hat{t}_{i}(s)=:b
, and without loss of generality we may assume that
0\leq a<b
. Moreover, since
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(s)=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\hat{t}_{i}(s)
, there is a
j\not=i
such that
d\coloneqq t_{j}(s)>\hat{t}_{j}(s)\eqqcolon c\geq 0
. Monotonicity of
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{j}
yields therefore

\ell_{i}(t_{i}(s))\leq\ell_{i}(\hat{t}_{i}(s))
and
\ell_{j}(t_{j}(s))\geq\ell_{j}(\hat{t}_{j}(s))
. Since, by assumption,
\ell_{i}(t_{i}(s))=\ell_{j}(t_{j}(s))
and
\ell_{i}(\hat{t}_{i}(s))=\ell_{j}(\hat{t}_{j}(s))
, this is possible only if all these four numbers are the same, that is, when

\ell_{i}(a)=\ell_{i}(b)=\ell_{j}(c)=\ell_{j}(d).

Now we have the required contradiction since we assumed that
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{j}
do not have common levels of constancy.
∎

Proof of uniqueness in Theorem
6.1
.

By Lemma
6.2
it suffices to show that the processes
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
do not have common levels of constancy, that is, that their generalized inverses
(U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})^{-1}=\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}\circ U_{i}
do not have common points of jumps (a.s.). But, each
U_{i}
is clearly a subordinator and the well-known result says that so is
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}
(see
[
7
, Chapter IV, Theorem 8]
).
Therefore,
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}\circ U_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
are subordinators. This reduces our task to showing that they are independent, because
independent subordinators do not have common moments of jumps. Indeed, jumps of a subordinator are precisely the jumps of the corresponding Poisson point process, and the Disjointness Lemma in §2.2 of

[
43
]
says that independent Poisson point processes do not have common points of jumps.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{i}
s are independent among themselves and independent of
U_{i}
s, we are left with establishing that
U_{i}
s are independent, that is, that the compound Poisson processes, say,
V_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, featured in (
6.14
) are independent. These processes may seem to be dependent, being driven by the same Poisson process
N
, but in fact are not. This is because random variables
\xi_{k},k\geq 1
have values in
S_{\mathsf{can}}\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}
so that their coordinates
\xi_{k,i}
are nonnegative, and for each index
k
and event
\omega
there is precisely one
i
such that
\xi_{k,i}(\omega)>0
; this implies that the actual driving processes of
V_{i}
s are obtained from
N
by (independent) coloring (see
[
43
]
*p. 53). ∎

6.6.3.
Notes on Theorem
6.1

We note, first of all, that Theorem
6.1
generalizes Theorem
4.3
. Indeed, if there is no jump part in (
6.14
),
U_{i}
takes the form
U_{i}(t)=\beta_{i}t,t\geq 0
; hence,
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}(t)=t,t\geq 0
for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and consequently (
6.16
) reduces to (
4.14
). At the same time,
U_{i}^{-1}(t)=\frac{t}{\beta_{i}},

t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and so the requirement that
U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
simply means that balance conditions (
4.13
) are satisfied. We leave it to the reader to check that Theorem
6.3
generalizes the formula for
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
introduced in Section
5.5
.

Secondly, as a direct consequence of Theorem
6.1
and commutation results of Section
6.3
, we obtain the following statement.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
be the process of (
6.16
). Then the distributions of
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
and
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
coincide, provided that in the construction of the former process one uses the common value of
U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
as the local time of
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
.

Our final remark here concerns the symmetric local time
L
of
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
. Theorem
6.1
includes, as its integral part, the representation

L(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}(s)|<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

and the fact that
L
coincides with each and every process
U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i},

i\in{\mathpzc K}
.
The following corollary to the theorem provides an alternative description of
L
issuing from these two statements.

Corollary 6.4
.

For each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,

L(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\beta_{i}\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}(s)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0

Proof.

We start by noting that in the case of
{\mathpzc k}=1
, the family
T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
of time-changes reduces to just one process
T_{1}
and this process is deterministic:
T_{1}(t)=t,t\geq 0
. Moreover, the vector
\beta
becomes a scalar and is necessarily equal to
1
. Finally,
W_{0,1,0,\delta\mu}
has only one coordinate, and (
6.16
) becomes

W_{0,1,0,\delta\mu}=B+U\circ U^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L},

(6.17)

where (a)
B
is a Brownian motion and
\mathcal{L}
is its running minimum
defined by
\mathcal{L}(t)\coloneqq-\min_{s\in[0,t]}(B(s)\wedge 0),t\geq 0
, and (b)
U
is an independent subordinator of the form

U(t)\coloneqq t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}}\xi_{k},\qquad t\geq 0.

where, as in (
6.14
),
N
is a Poisson process with parameter
\delta
and
\xi_{k},k\geq 1
are independent random variables with distribution
\mu
.
W_{0,1,0,\delta\mu}
is evidently a Brownian motion on the right half-line with jumps from
0
.

Coming back to the general case of
{\mathpzc k}\geq 1
, let
i
be fixed, and let
V(t)\coloneqq U_{i}(\beta_{i}^{-1}t),t\geq 0
, so that, by (
6.14
),

V(t)=t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}}\xi_{k,i},\qquad t\geq 0,

but this time
N
is a Poisson process with parameter
\delta\beta_{i}^{-1}
. Then

X\coloneqq B_{i}+V\circ V^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i},

being of the form (
6.17
),
is a Brownian motion on the right half-line with jumps from
0
, as described above. More precisely,
X=W_{0,1,0,\beta_{i}^{-1}\delta\mu_{i}}
where
\mu_{i}
is the restriction of
\mu
to the
i
th ray. Theorem
6.1
says therefore that the local time
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\beta_{i}\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X(s)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,t\geq 0
of
X
exists and equals
V^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}=\beta_{i}U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}
:

\beta_{i}U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X(s)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0.

Also, since
V\circ V^{-1}=U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}
, (
6.16
) says that
(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=X\circ T_{i}
, and thus our task reduces to showing that, on the one hand,

\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X\circ T_{i}(s)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{T_{i}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X_{i}(u)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}u,\qquad t\geq 0

(6.18)

because, to recall,
L=U_{i}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
.

To prove this relation, we note first that, by Lemma
8.5
(a), for all
j\in{\mathpzc K}
,
T_{j}(t)\geq\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{j}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,t\geq 0
; indeed, the lemma says that
T_{j}
grows linearly with slope
1
whenever
(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{j}
is positive (the lemma is presented in Section
8
, but its proof does not use any of the results obtained in the meantime; in fact, the lemma is purely deterministic). On the other hand,
\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{j}(t)=t
and
\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{j}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{|W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}(s)|>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=t,t\geq 0
. Hence,

T_{j}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s
, for all indexes
j\in{\mathpzc K}
and in particular for
j=i
:

T_{i}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X\circ T_{i}(s)>0\}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0.

Also, since
1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{A}=(1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{A})^{2}
for any indicator function
1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{A}
, the left-hand side of (
6.18
) equals

\int_{0}^{t}(1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X\circ T_{i}(s)<\varepsilon\}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s
and this, by the relation just established is
\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X\circ T_{i}(s)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}T_{i}(s)
. This completes the proof of (
6.18
) because the last expression can be rewritten, by the change of variables, as

\int_{0}^{T_{i}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<X_{i}(u)<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}u
.
∎

6.7.
Extensions

Throughout Section
6.6
we have assumed that (a) all betas are positive, and (b) they add up to
1
(in Section
6.4
only (b) is used). Again, (b) is made without loss of generality, for convenience and to have a natural interpretation of the betas. Indeed,
the boundary condition (
3.2
) with
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
remains the same if
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
\delta
are multiplied by a positive constant. Hence, if (a) holds we can let
\overline{\beta}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
and

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}\coloneqq W_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}\mu},

(6.19)

where
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}
are obtained by dividing
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
\delta
by
\overline{\beta}
.

Notably, as a result of this definition, Section
6.4
now applies also to the case where all betas are positive but
\overline{\beta}
need not be
1
. Theorems
6.1
and
6.3
similarly remain true without any essential changes even if (b) is violated. Elaborating on this succinct statement, suppose (a) is satisfied but
\overline{\beta}\not=1
. Then, by definition, as in (
6.16
) and (
6.14
),

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=(B_{i}+U_{i}^{\sharp}\circ(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

where
U_{i}^{\sharp}(t)\coloneqq(\beta_{i}/\overline{\beta})t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N^{\sharp}(t)}}\xi_{k,i},t\geq 0

and
N^{\sharp}
is a Poisson process with rate
\delta/\overline{\beta}
. However, the formula
N(t)=N^{\sharp}(\overline{\beta}t),t\geq 0
defines a Poisson process with rate
\delta
, and introducing
U_{i}(t)=\beta_{i}t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}}\xi_{k,i},t\geq 0
, we see that
U_{i}^{\sharp}(t)=U_{i}(t/\overline{\beta}),t\geq 0
. Consequently,
(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}=\overline{\beta}U_{i}^{-1}
and
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}=U_{i}^{\sharp}\circ(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover, because of the first of these relations, the requirement that
(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i
is equivalent to the requirement that
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i
, and thus, together with other conditions listed in Theorem
6.1
, determines the same
T_{i}
s. As a result,

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K};

(6.20)

this means that, even if
\overline{\beta}\not=1
, formula (
6.16
) is still in force, and neither
\beta_{i}
nor the rate of the Poisson process need to be modified by dividing them by
\overline{\beta}
— this is what we mean by saying that Theorem
6.1
remains essentially the same.

However, it should be noted that, by the relation
(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}=\overline{\beta}U_{i}^{-1}
established above, the new local time
L\coloneqq U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not coincide with the old
L^{\sharp}\coloneqq(U_{i}^{\sharp})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
. Rather, we have,

L^{\sharp}=\overline{\beta}L.

(6.21)

But this state of affairs has positive consequences. First of all, to obtain
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
from
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
of (
6.20
) we should, by definition, slow it down at
\mathfrak{0}
by means of the inverse of
A_{\alpha}^{\sharp}
defined by
A_{\alpha}^{\sharp}(t)\coloneqq t+(\alpha/\overline{\beta})L^{\sharp}(t),t\geq 0
. Since, by (
6.21
),
A_{\alpha}^{\sharp}
coincides with
A_{\alpha}
defined by
A_{\alpha}(t)=t+\alpha L(t),t\geq 0
the description of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
given in Theorem
6.3
applies also to the case where
\overline{\beta}\not=1
. Finally, to obtain
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
, we should kill
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
at the instant when
L^{\sharp}
reaches the level of a random variable
\zeta
that is exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma/\overline{\beta}
. Since this is the instant when
L
reaches the level of
\zeta/\overline{\beta}
and
\zeta/\overline{\beta}
is exponentially distributed with parameter
\gamma
, we conclude that also the description of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
given in Theorem
6.3
applies to the case where
\overline{\beta}
is not necessarily
1
.

To summarize our discussion: as long as all
\beta_{i}
s are positive,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
can be characterized as follows. Let
\left(\xi_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of independent
S_{\mathsf{can}}
-valued random variables with common distribution
\mu
and
N
be an independent Poisson process with intensity
\delta
. Also, let

U_{i}(t)=\beta_{i}t+{\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}}\xi_{k,i},\qquad t\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(6.22)

Then there is a
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional time change process
(T_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
uniquely determined by the requirements that
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathpzc k}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
and
U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
(
\mathcal{L}_{i}
s are defined in (
6.15
)). Moreover, the symmetric local time
L
of
W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
(see (
6.12
)) coincides with
U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
and

W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}
has the following form:

(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu})_{i}=B_{i}\circ T_{i}+U_{i}\circ L=(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(6.23)

Finally, if
\zeta
is an independent exponentially distributed random variable with parameter
\gamma
, then

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}(t)=\begin{cases}W_{0,\beta,0,\delta\mu}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t),&U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)<\zeta,\\
\text{undefined},&U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)\geq\zeta,\end{cases}

(6.24)

where
A_{\alpha}(t)=t+\alpha L(t),t\geq 0
, and the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is given by

K(t)=\begin{cases}U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t),&U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)<\zeta,\\
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(\zeta),&U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)\geq\zeta.\end{cases}

(6.25)

6.8.
Lumping edges together

Let, as in Sections
4.2.1
and
5.10
, for some natural number
\mathpzc n\leq{\mathpzc k}
,

\Psi
map
{\mathpzc K}
onto
\mathpzc N\coloneqq\{1,\dots,\mathpzc n\}
. Also, let, by a slight abuse of notation,
\Psi
denote simultaneously the map
S_{\mathpzc k}\ni(i,x)\mapsto(\Psi(i),x)\in S_{\mathpzc n}
, and the associated transformation
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})\to D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc n})
given by
(\Psi\omega)(t)=\Psi(\omega(t)),t\geq 0,\omega\in D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})
.
We claim that

\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}=W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\delta\widetilde{\mu}},

(6.26)

where

\widetilde{\beta}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
with
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}\beta_{i},j\in\mathpzc N
, and
\widetilde{\mu}
is the image of the measure
\mu
via
\Psi
. To clarify, (
6.26
) means that the distributions of
\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
and
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\delta\widetilde{\mu}}
, as vectors with values in
D([0,\infty),S)
, are the same.

To see this, we recall first of all that, by (
6.19
), without loss of generality, it can be assumed that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
. Then, see Section
6.4
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}W_{\alpha,\beta}
. This means that (a) before the time
\tau
when
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
reaches the level of an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma+\delta
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is identical to
W_{\alpha,\beta}
, and (b) at
\tau
, with probability
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
starts all over again, forgetting its past, at a random point of
S_{\mathpzc k}
, its distribution being
\mu
. Accordingly, (a) as established in Section
5.10
, before
\tau
,
\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is identical to
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}}
, and (b) at
\tau
, with probability
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
,
\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
starts all over again, forgetting its past, at a random point of
Sn
, its distribution being
\widetilde{\mu}
. Since
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is a common symmetric local time for
W_{\alpha,\beta}
and
W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta}}
, this establishes the claim.

6.9.
Degenerate cases

As in Section
5.8
we need to describe the process also in the degenerate case where some or all betas vanish. Basically, the related process is a concatenation of processes described in Section
5.8
. Again, if started at the edge with
i\in\mathpzc L
, the process of Section
5.8
rather quickly leaves this part of the state-space via origin, never to return. However, as long as
\mu
has a nonzero mass at this edge, the process related to
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
can come back here by a jump when the local time of the process (which from the time of reaching origin behaves like a possibly slowed down Walsh’s process on a subgraph) at the origin reaches an exponential level. Edges with
\beta_{i}=0
and of zero measure
\mu
, form the transient part of the state-space.

If all betas vanish,
\alpha
is necessarily positive and, as we know from Section
5.8
, the process generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,0,\gamma}=\mathfrak{G}_{1,0,\gamma/\alpha}
ceases to be defined when it first reaches
0
to be stopped there, and then stays there for an exponential time. However, that of

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,0,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is resurrected with probability
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
and starts all over again at a random point of the graph, distributed according to
\mu
. If it so happens that some edges are of zero measure
\mu
, they again form the transient part of the state-space, as the process can never return there. We will write more extensively on such cases in our analysis of infinite
\mathpzc m
.

We leave it to the readers to check that the principle of lumping edges together applies also to these degenerate cases — in Section
7.4
we will give an independent proof of this fact.

7.
The case of infinite
\mathpzc m
, analytic part

We come to the case where
\mathpzc m(S)=\infty
; as throughout the paper, we assume that
\int(1\wedge x)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)
is finite. Given such a measure, nonnegative real parameters
\alpha
and
\gamma
, and a nonnegative vector
\beta=(\beta_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
, we consider the restriction, say,

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m},

of
\Delta
to the domain of
f\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
such that

\tfrac{\alpha}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f_{i}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x);

(7.1)

note that for
f\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
, the function
S\ni x\mapsto f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})
is
\mathpzc m
is integrable. We stress that, in contrast to the previous sections,
\beta_{i}
need not be positive; in fact, in the extreme case we may even have
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
. Our main theorem in this section, Theorem
7.2
, states that
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is a Feller generator; the related process will be constructed using natural building blocks in Section
8
.

The idea of the proof is to approximate the searched-for semigroup generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
by the semigroups described in Section
6
. This approximation involves probability measures
\mu_{\varepsilon}
constructed from
\mathpzc m
as follows. Since, by assumption,
\int(1\wedge x)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)
is finite, we have
\delta(\varepsilon)\coloneqq\mathpzc m(\Gamma_{\varepsilon})<\infty
for any
\varepsilon>0
, where
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\subset S
is the set of points in
S
that lie at a distance
\geq\varepsilon
from the graph’s center. Therefore, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the formula

\mu_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)=\frac{\mathpzc m(\mathcal{\cdot}\cap\Gamma_{\varepsilon})}{\mathpzc m(\Gamma_{\varepsilon})}

defines a Borel probability measure on
S
. Notably, by assumption, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\delta(\varepsilon)

=\infty
and at the same time the measures
\mu_{\varepsilon}
converge to the Dirac delta at the graph’s center.

We also need vectors
\beta(\varepsilon)=(\beta_{i}(\varepsilon))_{i\in{\mathpzc K}},\varepsilon>0
such that

\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)>0,\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},\varepsilon>0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)=\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}.

This assumption allows thinking of the semigroups generated by

\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}},\qquad\varepsilon>0,

described in Section
6
.

7.1.
The generation theorem

In terms of the objects introduced above, our main theorem says that the semigroup generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
can be obtained as a limit, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, of the semigroups generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}
. The following lemma is a first step in this direction.

Lemma 7.1
.

As
\varepsilon\to 0
