, the resolvents
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1},\lambda>0
converge strongly to the Feller resolvent
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
given by

R_{\lambda}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+\overline{C}(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},

(7.2)

where

(a)

R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
is the resolvent of the minimal Brownian motion, and
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S},\lambda>0
is the Laplace transform of its lifetime (see (
3.11
)),

(b)

\overline{C}(g)\coloneqq\frac{\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+C(g)}{\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})}
,
C(g)\coloneqq 2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)+\alpha g(\mathfrak{0})
, and
C_{i}(g)
s were introduced in (
3.7
).

Proof.

As explained in Section
6.7
, the generator of the process characterized by the vector of parameters
(\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon})
is, by definition, the generator of the process characterized by the vector
(\overline{\beta}(\varepsilon))^{-1}(\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon})
, where
\overline{\beta}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)
.
Moreover, in (
6.7
) the resolvent of the latter generator is expressed in terms of the resolvent of the generator characterized by

(\overline{\beta}(\varepsilon))^{-1}(\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon))
, that is, of
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)}
. In the light of this formula we have namely

\displaystyle\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}g+M_{\varepsilon}(g)\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}

(7.3)

where

•

R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)}\right)^{-1}
,
\lambda>0
,

•

\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\mathsf{1}_{S}
, so that — see (
3.12
) — for
x\geq 0
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
we have
(\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon})_{i}(x)=\frac{\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}{\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}}\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)+\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}
, and

•

M_{\varepsilon}(g)\coloneqq\frac{\delta(\varepsilon)\int_{S}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}}{\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}}
.

Furthermore, by (
3.9
),

R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+E_{\varepsilon}(g)\ell_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda,\varepsilon>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},

where

E_{\varepsilon}(g)\coloneqq\frac{2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)C_{i}(g)+\alpha g(\mathfrak{0})}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)+\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)}
.

Since, clearly,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}E_{\varepsilon}(g)=0
, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g
and, in particular,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}=\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
. Thus, to establish that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda}g
, it suffices to show that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}M_{\varepsilon}(g)=\overline{C}(g),\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},

(7.4)

and this, of course, involves the analysis of
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\delta(\varepsilon)\int_{S}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}
. For the latter, we note first that, by (
3.10
), the limit
\lim_{x\to 0+}x^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}(x)
exists (and equals
2C_{i}(g)\sqrt{2\lambda}
),
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. This implies that
R_{\lambda}^{0}g
is integrable with respect to
\mathpzc m
, and therefore, by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\delta(\varepsilon)\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m=\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
. Moreover,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\delta(\varepsilon)E_{\varepsilon}(g)\int_{S}\ell_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}=C(g)\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{S}\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}=C(g),

because the measures
\mu_{\varepsilon}
converge to the Dirac delta measure at
\mathfrak{0}
. This shows that the numerator of
M_{\varepsilon}(g)
converges to
\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+C(g)
, whereas its denominator converges to
\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})
. This establishes (
7.4
), for the denominator is larger than zero:
\gamma+\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})
is nonnegative and
\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m=\int_{S}(\mathsf{1}_{S}-\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
is strictly positive because
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}<1
everywhere outside of
\mathfrak{0}
, that is, almost everywhere with respect to
\mathpzc m
.

Having established
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda}g,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
, we note next that
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
,
as a limit of Feller resolvents, is a pseudoresolvent of nonnegative operators with
\|\lambda R_{\lambda}\|\leq 1,\lambda>0
, and check that
\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}\leq\mathsf{1}_{S}
with equality for
\gamma=0
. Therefore, our task comes down to proving that

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}g=g,\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

(7.5)

To this end, we claim first that

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m=\infty.

(7.6)

Indeed, introducing
\overline{\mathpzc m}
, a Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
,
as the image of
\mathpzc m
via the map
S\ni(x,i)\mapsto x
, we see that
\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
equals
\int_{[0,\infty)}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x})\overline{\mathpzc m}(\mathrm{d}x)
, and thus converges to
\infty
as
\lambda\to\infty
, because by assumption
\mathpzc m
is infinite, and thus so is
\overline{\mathpzc m}
.

Next, we write

\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=(1-\lambda\overline{C}(\mathsf{1}_{S}))\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+C(\mathsf{1}_{S})}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
and recall that
\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}\|_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}}=1
. Since this, when combined with (
7.6
), yields
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S}
, in proving (
7.5
) we may restrict ourselves to
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}
, that is, to
g
with
g(\mathfrak{0})=0
. But
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
is the resolvent of the minimal Brownian motion semigroup, and hence we have
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}g=g
for
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}
. Because of
\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}\|_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}}=1
, this reduces our task to establishing that

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\frac{\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}=0,\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}.

(7.7)

We note furthermore that, since
\|\lambda R_{\lambda}\|\leq 1
, it suffices to prove
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}g

=g
, or, equivalently,
the formula above, for
g
in a dense subset of
\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)
, and in particular, we can choose this subset to be the common range of
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
.

However, for
g
of the form
g=R_{\nu}^{0}h,
where
h\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}
and
\nu>0
is fixed, the numerator in (
7.7
) equals

\int_{S}\nu R_{\lambda}^{0}R_{\nu}^{0}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+\int_{S}R_{\nu}^{0}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m-\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m

by the Hilbert equation.
If
h
is nonnegative, the first and the third terms here are nonincreasing functions of
\lambda
, and thus have finite limits, whereas the second does not depend on
\lambda
(and is finite). It follows that this expression has a finite limit as
\lambda\to\infty
for all
h\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{0}(S)$}
. To complete the proof, therefore, it suffices to note that, by (
7.6
), the denominator in (
7.7
) converges to
\infty
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
∎

Our next result identifies
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
as the generator of the Feller semigroup related to
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
of (
7.2
).

Theorem 7.2
.

The operator
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is a Feller generator, and its resolvent coincides with
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
. Moreover,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\mathrm{e}^{t\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}}=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}}
strongly and uniformly for
t
in compact intervals.

Proof.

Since
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is a Feller resolvent, there is an operator, say,
\mathfrak{G}
, such that
\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}\right)^{-1}=R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
. This operator’s domain is the common range of
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
. We claim that

\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}).

First of all, by (
7.2
),
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})\subset C^{2}(S)
, because both
R_{\lambda}^{0}g,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
and
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
belong
C^{2}(S)
. Hence, it suffices to check that for
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
, that is, for
f
of the form
f=R_{\lambda}g
for some
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
, condition (
7.1
) is satisfied. Let
f
be of this form. We already know (see the proof of Lemma
7.1
) that
S\ni x\mapsto R_{\lambda}^{0}g(x)-R_{\lambda}^{0}g(\mathfrak{0})=R_{\lambda}^{0}g(x)
is
\mathpzc m
integrable. Since,
\lim_{x\to 0+}(\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}-1)/x=-\sqrt{2\lambda}
the same is true of
S\ni x\mapsto\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x)-\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(\mathfrak{0})
, and we have, on the one hand,

\displaystyle\int_{S}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+\overline{C}(g)\int_{S}(\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x)-\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(\mathfrak{0}))\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m-\lambda\overline{C}(g)\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m.

(7.8)

On the other hand,
(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}^{\prime}(0)=2C_{i}(g)\sqrt{2\lambda}
and
(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)^{\prime\prime}(0)=-2g(\mathfrak{0})
, showing that, for
F
of (
3.4
),
FR_{\lambda}^{0}g=-\alpha g(\mathfrak{0})-2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)=-C(g)
. In particular, because of
\ell_{\lambda}^{0}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S},
we have
F\ell_{\lambda}^{0}=\gamma+\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})
, and, consequently,

Ff=-C(g)+\overline{C}(g)(\gamma+\lambda C(\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S})).
A look at the definition of
\overline{C}(g)
reveals now that the last expression coincides with the right-most side in (
7.8
), that is, that
f
satisfies (
7.1
), as claimed, and so the desired inclusion is established.

Next, we will argue that
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
extends
\mathfrak{G}
. Indeed, any
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
is of the form
f=R_{\lambda}g,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
and so, by definition,
\mathfrak{G}f=\mathfrak{G}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}\right)^{-1}g=\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}\right)^{-1}g-g=\lambda f-g
. At the same time, this
f
belongs, as we have just proved, to
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m})
, and a calculation, based on (
7.2
) and
\Delta\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}=\lambda\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}
, shows that
\lambda f-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}f=g
, that is,
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}f=\lambda f-g
. This implies that
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}f=\mathfrak{G}f,f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
, as heralded above.

However,
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
cannot be a proper extension of
\mathfrak{G}
, because
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
satisfies the positive-maximum principle (see Appendix
12.2
) and is thus dissipative, and so we conclude that
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}=\mathfrak{G}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is a Feller generator with resolvent
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
.
The rest therefore follows by the Trotter–Kato–Neveu theorem
[
1
,
15
,
17
,
25
,
31
,
40
,
54
]
.
∎

7.2.
Continuous dependence on parameters

As a direct corollary to Lemma
7.1
and Theorem
7.2
, we obtain the following formula for the resolvent of
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
. We have

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+\overline{C}(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}

(7.9)

where, as before,

\overline{C}(g)=\frac{\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+C(g)}{\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m+\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})}\text{ and }C(g)=2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)+\alpha g(\mathfrak{0}),

or, in the expounded form,

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+\frac{\alpha g(\mathfrak{0})+2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)+\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}.

Notably, as we will now argue, this formula is valid for all possible choices of parameters. Indeed, for
\mathpzc m=0
,
\overline{C}(g)
coincides with
E(g)
of (
3.8
) and so (
7.9
) reduces to (
3.9
). Also in the limit case of
\alpha=\beta_{i}=0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\mathpzc m=0
but
\gamma>0
, we have
\overline{C}(g)=0
and thus the right hand side in (
7.9
) reduces to the minimal Brownian motion. Finally, to prove that (
7.9
) includes also the case where
\mathpzc m
is a nonzero but finite measure, one could transform (
6.7
) to the desired form with the help of (
3.9
), but this approach turns out to be computationally demanding. It is relatively easier to note that the argument presented in the proof of Theorem
7.2
and showing that the right-hand side of (
7.2
), that is, the right-hand side of (
7.9
), is a solution to the related resolvent equation, works well also in the case of our interest. Since such a solution is necessarily unique, we conclude that, as claimed, (
6.7
) is a special case of (
7.9
) but in a disguise.

Once we note that (
7.9
) encompasses all the possible choices of
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
\mathpzc m
, it is rather easy to see that the solution to the resolvent equation depends continuously on these parameters, and thus so do the corresponding semigroups and processes. Indeed,
C(g)
depends continuously on
\alpha\geq 0
and
\beta\geq 0
and therefore
\overline{C}(g)
depends continuously on these two parameters and on
\gamma\geq 0
in the region where
\overline{C}(g)
’s denominator is nonzero. This region is composed of two subsets: in the first of these
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
, in the second
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
but
\gamma>0
and/or
\mathpzc m
is infinite (in fact, it suffices to assume that
\mathpzc m
is nonzero, but the case of finite
\mathpzc m
and
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
does not correspond to a Feller resolvent, see below). Moreover,
\overline{C}(g)
depends continuously on
\mathpzc m
in the following sense: if for certain Lévy measures
\mathpzc m
and
\mathpzc m_{n},n\geq 1
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{n}=\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},\lambda>0
, then the same is true for the corresponding
\overline{C}(g)
s,
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
.

Remark 7.3
.

It follows from Theorems 2.6 and 2.8, and the proof of Theorem 2.10 in
Chapter V of
[
11
]
that if
\gamma=\beta_{i}=0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\mathpzc m
is an infinite measure on
S_{\mathsf{can}}\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}
such that
\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}(1\wedge|x|)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty,
then (
7.9
) defines a resolvent of a Feller process whose excursion measure is equal up to a multiplicative constant to
\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}\bar{P}_{x}\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x).
Here
\bar{P}_{x}
is the distribution of a Brownian motion on
S_{\mathsf{can}}
that is stopped upon hitting
\mathfrak{0}
.

7.3.
Comparison to Rogers’ work

It is worthwhile to look at (
7.9
) in the light of the seminal work of L.C.G. Rogers
[
60
]
, devoted to his exposition of Itô excursion theory via resolvents.
In our context, the main result of this paper can be stated as follows (see Rogers’ Theorems 1 and 2): the formula

R_{\lambda}g=R_{\lambda}^{0}g+\overline{C}(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}

(7.10)

defines a Feller resolvent
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
iff there are nonnegative constants
\alpha
and
\gamma
, and a family
\mathpzc n_{\lambda},\lambda>0
of finite measures concentrated on
S\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}
such that

\mathpzc n_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}>0,\quad(\mu-\lambda)\mathpzc n_{\mu}R_{\lambda}^{0}=\mathpzc n_{\lambda}-\mathpzc n_{\mu},\qquad\lambda,\mu>0

(7.11)

and
\overline{C}(g)
is of the form
\overline{C}(g)=\frac{\mathpzc n_{\lambda}g+\alpha g(0)}{\lambda\alpha+\lambda\mathpzc n_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}+\gamma}
.
In (
7.11
), measures
\mathpzc n_{\lambda},\lambda>0
are seen as functionals on
\mathfrak{C}(S)
; as explained by Rogers, they are Laplace transforms of entrance laws for the minimal process.

In this light, (
7.9
) can be summarized by saying that in the case of Brownian motion on a star graph the family
\mathpzc n_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is necessarily of the form

\mathpzc n_{\lambda}g=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g)+\int_{S\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m,\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}.

(7.12)

This representation can also be proved directly by arguing as in Section 3 of
[
60
]
; to this end, not surprisingly, one needs to use the fact (visible from (
3.10
) and already referred to above) that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
, the limit
\lim_{x\to 0+}x^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{0}g)_{i}(x)
exists and equals
2C_{i}(g)\sqrt{2\lambda}
.

7.4.
Lumping edges together

Formula (
7.9
), besides sharing a similar form with (
3.9
), inherits also from its predecessor a property that is worth recording again here, when we deal with the more general situation. To wit, in the notations of Section
3.3
, we have

\mathcal{I}^{-1}\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{I}=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\widetilde{\mathpzc}m}\right)^{-1},\qquad\lambda>0,

(7.13)

where
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\widetilde{\mathpzc}m}
is the generator in
\mathfrak{C}(Sn)
with
\widetilde{\beta}=(\widetilde{\beta}_{j})_{j\in\mathpzc N}
given by
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\{i\colon\Psi(i)=j\}}\beta_{i},j\in\mathpzc N
, and
\widetilde{\mathpzc}m
is the image of the measure
\mathpzc m
via the map
\Psi
.

This suggests that formula (
6.26
), established for finite
\mathpzc m
, extends to infinite
\mathpzc m
as well. That is,

\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}=W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\widetilde{\mathpzc}m},

(7.14)

whatever the parameters are, and, again, this means that the distributions of both processes involved, seen as having values in
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})
, are the same.

To prove (
7.14
), we fix
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
\mathpzc m
(such that at least one of the conditions
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
and
\mathpzc m(S_{\mathpzc k})=\infty
holds), and for each integer
n
choose a positive vector
\beta_{n}
, a constant
\delta_{n}
and a probability measure
\mu_{n}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n}=\beta\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{n}\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}=\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m,g\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathpzc k}),\lambda>0.

(7.15)

As explained in Section
7.2
, the semigroups generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta_{n},\gamma,\delta_{n}\mu_{n}}
converge then to the semigroup generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
. Hence, by the Trotter–Sova–Kurtz–Mackevičius Theorem (see e.g.
[
40
]
p. 385, see also Theorem 2.5 in Chapter 4 in
[
25
]
), the
processes
W_{\alpha,\beta_{n},\gamma,\delta_{n}\mu_{n}}
converge in distribution to
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
in
D([0,\infty),S)
(provided that they start from the same point), that is, for any continuous, bounded, real-valued function
\mathsf{f}
on
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})
and
x\in S_{\mathpzc k}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{f}(W_{\alpha,\beta_{n},\gamma,\delta_{n}\mu_{n}})=\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{f}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}).

(7.16)

Similarly, since (
7.15
) forces

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{\beta_{n}}=\widetilde{\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{n}\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}{\widetilde{\mu}_{n}}=\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mathpzc}m,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$},\lambda>0,

we have also

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta_{n}},\gamma,\delta_{n}\widetilde{\mu_{n}}})=\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\widetilde{\mathpzc}m}),

for any bounded, real-valued continuous function
\mathsf{g}
on
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc n})
and
x\in S_{\mathpzc n}
.

Since (
6.26
) applies to all the approximating processes, the obvious idea to complete the proof of (
7.14
) is by approximation. To this end, we take a closer look at
\Psi
. To begin with,
S_{\mathpzc k}
is equipped with the following natural metric: the distance between
(i,x)
and
(j,y)
is
x+y
if
i\not=j
and
|x-y|
otherwise (we have used this metric in Section
5.6
without spelling out the details). It is clear that
\Psi\colon S_{\mathpzc k}\to S_{\mathpzc n}
is a non-expansive mapping with respect to this metric — to see this it suffices to note that
|x-y|\leq x+y
for all nonnegative
x
and
y
. The definition of the metric in
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})
(see formulae (12.13) and (16.4) in
[
9
]
) thus implies that
\Psi
is also non-expansive as a map from

D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc k})
to
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc n})
. It follows, that
\mathsf{g}\circ\Psi
is a real-valued, bounded, continuous function on
D([0,\infty),S_{{\mathpzc k}})
whenever
\mathsf{g}
is a real-valued, bounded, continuous function on
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathpzc n})
.

This allows writing, for any
\mathsf{g}
described above and
x\in S_{\mathpzc n}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}\circ\Psi(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}\circ\Psi(W_{\alpha,\beta_{n},\gamma,\delta_{n}\mu_{n}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(\Psi W_{\alpha,\beta_{n},\gamma,\delta_{n}\mu_{n}})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta_{n}},\gamma,\delta_{n}\widetilde{\mu_{n}}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{x}\mathsf{g}(W_{\alpha,\widetilde{\beta},\gamma,\widetilde{\mathpzc}m}).

Since
\mathsf{g}
and
x
are arbitrarily chosen, this completes the proof of (
6.26
).

7.5.
The singular case

It remains to comment on the right-hand side of (
7.9
) in the case where
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
and
\mathpzc m
is a nonzero but finite measure, so that
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
for a probability measure
\mu
and
\delta=\mathpzc m(S)>0
; moreover,
C(g)=0
and
\overline{C}(g)=\frac{\delta\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\gamma+\lambda\delta\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
. The formula

R_{\lambda}g\coloneqq R_{\lambda}^{0}g+\overline{C}(g)\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}

(7.17)

then still defines a pseudo-resolvent, that is, the operators
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
satisfy the Hilbert equation. Moreover,
\lambda R_{\lambda}
s are positive contractions and
\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}\leq\mathsf{1}_{S}
(with equality for
\gamma=0
). Nevertheless,
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is not a Feller resolvent, because condition 2. of the definition fails.

To see this we note that
the calculation presented in the proof of Theorem
7.2
carries over to the case of interest with no substantial changes, and thus shows that the range of
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is contained in the kernel of the bounded linear functional
\mathpzc F\in(\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$})^{*}
given by

\mathpzc Fg=\delta\int_{S}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu-(\gamma+\delta)g(\mathfrak{0}),\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$};

this excludes the possibility of
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}g=g
for
g
with
\mathpzc Fg\not=0
.

As expounded in the following proposition, one can still find a semigroup corresponding to
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
, but this semigroup is defined merely on
the kernel of
\mathpzc F
, that is, on a (closed) subspace of
\mathfrak{C}(S)
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
be the subspace of
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}
such that
\mathpzc Fg=0
.
There is a (necessarily unique) strongly continuous semigroup
\left(T(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
of positive contractions in
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
such that

R_{\lambda}g=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}T(t)g\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\lambda>0,g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)$}.

(7.18)

Moreover,
T(t)\mathsf{1}_{S}=1_{S},t\geq 0
iff
\gamma=0
.

Proof.

We know that the restrictions of the operators
R_{\lambda},\lambda>0
to
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
form a pseudo-resolvent in this space, and that the norm of
\lambda R_{\lambda}|_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)$}}
does not exceed
1
for
\lambda>0
. Hence, by the Hille–Yosida Theorem, it suffices to show that

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}g=g,\qquad g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)$}.

(7.19)

To this end, we fix a
\nu>0
and choose a constant
c
in such a way that
\mathsf{1}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\mathsf{1}_{S}-c\ell_{\nu}^{0}
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
, that is, we take
c\coloneqq\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+\delta-\delta\int_{S}\ell_{\nu}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
; note that
\int_{S}\ell_{\nu}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu<1
and so the denominator above is positive. Since
\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
is an exit law for
R_{\lambda}^{0},\lambda>0
, we have

(\lambda-\nu)R_{\lambda}^{0}\ell_{\nu}^{0}=\ell_{\nu}^{0}-\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0.

(7.20)

This equation (a counterpart of the Hilbert equation), together with
\ell_{\lambda}^{0}=\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}
yields

\ell_{\nu}^{0}-\lambda R_{\lambda}\ell_{\nu}^{0}=(1-\lambda C(\ell_{\nu}))\ell_{\lambda}^{0}-\nu R_{\nu}\ell_{\nu}^{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{1}_{S}-\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=(1-\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S}))\ell_{\lambda}^{0},

for all
\lambda>0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}^{\sharp}-\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=[1-\lambda C(\mathsf{1}_{S})-c(1-\lambda C(\ell_{\nu}^{0}))]\mathpzc l_{\lambda}+c\nu R_{\lambda}^{0}\ell_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma-c(\gamma+\delta-\int_{S}(\nu R_{\lambda}^{0}\ell_{\nu}^{0}+\ell_{\nu}^{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mu)}{\gamma+\delta-\delta\int_{S}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\ell_{\lambda}^{0}+c\nu R_{\lambda}^{0}\ell_{\nu}^{0},\quad\lambda>0,

where (
7.20
) was used once more. Also
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\int_{S}\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0
(because
\mu(\{\mathfrak{0}\})
is
0
), implying that the denominator in the fraction next to
\ell_{\lambda}^{0}
above converges to
\gamma+\delta>0
; at the same time, by
\|R_{\lambda}^{0}\|\leq\lambda^{-1}
, the numerator converges to
\gamma-c(\gamma+\delta-\int_{S}\ell_{\nu}^{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu)=0
. The same estimate on
\|R_{\lambda}^{0}\|
together with

\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\|_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}}=1
allows us thus to conclude that the last expression in the display converges to
0
, as
\lambda\to\infty
, that is, that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}^{\sharp}=\mathsf{1}^{\sharp}
.

The problem therefore reduces to establishing that (
7.19
) holds for
g\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)$}
such that
g(\mathfrak{0})=0
. For such
g
,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}g=g
and thus, because of (
7.17
) and
\|\mathpzc l_{\lambda}\|_{\mbox{$\mathfrak{C}(S)$}}=1
, we need to show that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\frac{\delta\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\gamma+\delta\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}=0
. Now,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=1-\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\int_{S}\ell_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=1
, and so the denominator in this fraction converges to
\gamma+\delta
. At the same time, due to
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{0}g=g
, the numerator converges to
\delta\int_{S}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu
. This integral equals zero, because
g
belongs to the kernel of
\mathpzc F
and we have
g(\mathfrak{0})=0
. This completes the proof of (
7.19
).

Finally, if
\gamma>0
there is no sense of talking about
T(t)\mathsf{1}_{S}
because
\mathsf{1}_{S}
does not belong to
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
. On the other hand, if
\gamma=0
, a straightforward calculation shows that
\overline{C}(\mathsf{1}_{S})=\lambda^{-1}
. This implies
\lambda R_{\lambda}\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S},\lambda>0
and, hence,
T(t)\mathsf{1}_{S}=\mathsf{1}_{S},t\geq 0
. ∎

We end this section by noting that the semigroup of Proposition
7.4
is related to the following ‘singular’ process.
On each edge it behaves like a Brownian motion, but at the instant when it touches
\mathfrak{0}
, is killed with probability
\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+\delta}
or, with probability
\frac{\delta}{\gamma+\delta}
, starts all over again
at a random point distributed according to
\mu
. It should be stressed here that, strictly speaking,
\mathfrak{0}
thus does not belong to the state-space of the process. Also, the reader will not find it difficult to check that the semigroup is generated by
\Delta
with domain composed of
f\in C^{2}(S)
such that both
f
and
f^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathpzc F}(S)
.

8.
The case of infinite
\mathpzc m
: stochastic analysis

Let, as in the previous section
\mathpzc m(S)=\infty
and
\int(1\wedge x)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)
be finite. As a direct consequence of Theorem
7.2
, there exists an
S
-valued Feller process, say,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
, the restriction of
\Delta
to the domain where (
3.2
) holds, provided that
\alpha,\gamma\geq 0
and
\beta\geq 0
. It is our goal in this section to find an explicit formula for
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
, seen as having values in
S_{\mathsf{can}}
, that would be analogous to that presented in Section
6
for
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
.

We are working under the standing assumption saying that, for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,

\text{ the $\mathpzc m$ measure of the $i$th ray is infinite whenever }\beta_{i}=0.

(8.1)

The case where this assumption is violated will be covered in Section
8.5
.

8.1.
A representation of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}

To find a counterpart of (
6.16
) we need, besides a
{\mathpzc k}-
dimensional Brownian motion
B
, an independent Poisson point process
\Pi
with values in
[0,\infty)\times S_{\mathsf{can}}
and mean measure
\,\mathrm{d}t\times\mathpzc m
. To recall, see
[
43
]
, this means that
\Pi
is a collection of points that are randomly scattered over
[0,\infty)\times S_{\mathsf{can}}
in such a way that their number in any measurable
A\subset[0,\infty)\times S_{\mathsf{can}}
is Poisson distributed with mean
(\mathrm{d}t\times\mathpzc m)(A)
, and their numbers in disjoint subsets are independent (and independent of
B
). It will also be convenient to think of
\Pi
as a random measure, being the sum of Dirac measures concentrated at the points of
\Pi
:

\Pi=N(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\coloneqq\sum_{(t,x)\in\Pi}\updelta_{(t,x)}.

With the help of
\Pi
, we can define an
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\mathpzc k}
-valued process

\mathcal{U}(t)\coloneqq t\beta+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}xN(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)=t\beta+\sum_{(s,x)\in\Pi,s\in[0,t]}x,\qquad t\geq 0.

(8.2)

As in the proof of uniqueness in Theorem
6.1
, one can argue that coordinates of
\mathcal{U}
, denoted in what follows by
U_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, are independent subordinators. Because of our standing assumption (
8.1
), each
U_{i}
is strictly increasing and so its generalized inverse
U_{i}^{-1}
is continuous.

Our main result in this section says that, as long as (
8.1
) is satisfied,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
can be represented in terms of
B
and
\mathcal{U}
.

Theorem 8.1
.

There is a family
T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
of nondecreasing nonnegative processes with continuous paths such that

1.

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathpzc k}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
,

2.

W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
defined by requiring that its
i
th coordinate equals

(W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m})_{i}=(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},

(8.3)

(where
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is defined in (
6.15
)) is a Feller process in
S_{\mathsf{can}}
generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
,
and

3.

U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Moreover, conditions 1. and 3. determine
T_{i}
s uniquely (a.s.), and the common value
L
of
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
is a local time of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
at
\mathfrak{0}
. Finally,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
can be represented as

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(t)=\begin{cases}W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}(A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)),&L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)<\zeta,\\
undefined,&L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)\geq\zeta,\end{cases}

(8.4)

where
A_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the inverse to
A_{\alpha}
defined by
A_{\alpha}(t)=t+\alpha L(t),t\geq 0
and
\zeta
is an exponentially distributed random variable with parameter
\gamma
that is independent of
B
and
\Pi
.

Several comments are here in order. First of all, in this theorem, as before, by a local time of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
at
\mathfrak{0}
we mean a continuous additive functional that grows only when
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
is at
\mathfrak{0}
. Also, once we establish that
L
is a local time
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
at
\mathfrak{0}
, as a direct consequence we will get that
L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}
is a local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
at
\mathfrak{0}
. Similarly,
K
defined by

K(t)=\begin{cases}L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t),&L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)<\zeta,\\
\zeta,&L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(t)\geq\zeta.\end{cases}

(8.5)

will turn out to be a local time for
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
.

As a further comment, we recall that, by Theorem
7.2
, the process
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}

is obtained as a limit of processes studied in Section
6
which have larger and larger intensities of ‘resurrections’ (equal to
\gamma+\delta(\varepsilon)
) whereas distributions of their position after these ‘resurrections’ (equal to
\mu_{\varepsilon}
) are concentrated more and more around the graph’s origin. This leads to the intuition that jumps of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
occur ‘with infinite intensity’ but are ‘infinitesimally small’. Representation (
8.3
) confirms this. Indeed, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, both
B_{i}\circ T_{i}
and
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
have continuous paths, and so the jumps of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
come only from those of
\mathcal{U}
. These, in turn are the jumps of
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}xN(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)
. Now, (
8.8
) (further down) shows that
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}xN(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)
is a sum of an absolutely convergent series, which by nature consists of infinitely many extremely small terms.

Finally, Theorem
8.1
generalizes Theorem
6.1
in a similar way as the latter generalizes Theorem
4.3
.

8.2.
Approximation in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
; four lemmas

The main idea of the proof of Theorem
8.1
is to approximate
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
by processes
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
(where
\delta(\varepsilon)
and
\mu_{\varepsilon}
are those of the beginning of Section
7
) as constructed in Theorem
6.1
. We know from Theorem
7.2
that the semigroups of processes
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converge to the semigroup of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
, and so, by the Trotter–Sova–Kurtz–Mackevičius Theorem (see e.g.
[
40
]
p. 385, see also Theorem 2.5 in Chapter 4 in
[
25
]
), the
processes
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converge in distribution to
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k})
, provided that they start from the same point. Since this in turn implies that each coordinate of
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converges in distribution to the corresponding coordinate of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
in the space
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
, to prove representation (
8.3
) it suffices to show that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the
i
th coordinate of
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converges in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
to the right-hand side of (
8.3
) almost surely. This will be done in Proposition
8.6
(see further down) which, however, needs to be preceded by
four lemmas. The proof of Theorem
8.1
will be completed in Section
8.4
.

To fix notation, we write

(W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}})_{i}=(B_{i}+U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{\varepsilon,i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

(8.6)

where
U_{\varepsilon,i}
s are subordinators defined as counterparts of (
6.14
), and

T_{\varepsilon,i}
s are nondecreasing nonnegative processes characterized in Theorem
6.1
. Our first lemma reveals that all
U_{\varepsilon,i}
s can be constructed by means of the Poisson process
\Pi
introduced above and that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the so-constructed processes converge to the coordinates of
\mathcal{U}
of (
8.2
).

Lemma 8.2
.

The role of
U_{\varepsilon,i}
can be played by

(U_{\varepsilon,i}(t))_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\coloneqq t\beta(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}xN(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x),\qquad t\geq 0,

(8.7)

where, as before,
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is the set of points of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
that lie at a distance
\geq\varepsilon
from the graph’s center. Moreover, for any
t>0
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, almost surely,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|U_{\varepsilon,i}(s)-U_{i}(s)|=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}(s)-U_{i}^{-1}(s)|=0.

Proof.

Definition (
8.7
) implies that the number of jumps of
(U_{\varepsilon,i}(t))_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
in an interval of length
\varDelta t
is Poisson distributed with parameter
\delta(\varepsilon)\varDelta t\mu_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})=\delta(\varepsilon)\varDelta t
, and that the numbers of these jumps in disjoint intervals are independent. This shows (see e.g.
[
8
]
*Ch. 23) that the moments of jumps are the moments of jumps of a Poisson process with parameter
\delta(\varepsilon)
. Since, moreover, the distribution of the size of each jump is
\mu_{\varepsilon}
, this shows the first part of the lemma (see (
6.14
)).

To establish the second, we start by noting that, for any measurable
A\subset S_{\mathsf{can}}
,
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}1_{A}(x)N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)
is
a Poisson random variable with parameter
t\mathpzc m(A)
, and so
\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}1_{A}(x)N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)=t\mathpzc m(A),t\geq 0
. A standard procedure shows thus that, for any measurable
f\colon S_{\mathsf{can}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}f(x)N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)=t\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}f(x)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),t\geq 0
. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}|x|1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{|x|<1\}}N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=t\int_{|x|<1}|x|\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq t\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}(|x|\wedge 1)\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty,

where
|x|
is the length of
x
.
Together with the fact that
\mathpzc m(\Gamma_{1})<\infty
this shows that, for
t>0
, the sum

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}xN(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{(s,x)\in\Pi,s\in[0,t]}x

(8.8)

has at most countably many terms, and thus can be thought of as a series; moreover, the series converges absolutely. But, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
s\in[0,t]
,

\displaystyle 0\leq U_{i}(s)-U_{\varepsilon,i}(s)

\displaystyle=|\beta_{i}-\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)|+\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\{|x|<\varepsilon\}}x_{i}N(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq|\beta_{i}-\beta_{i}(\varepsilon)|+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{|x|<\varepsilon\}}x_{i}N(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}x),

where
x_{i}
is the
i
th coordinate of
x
.
The last expression converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the integral being the partial sum of the absolutely convergent series of (
8.8
) that contains only the terms with norm smaller than
\varepsilon
. This shows that
U_{\varepsilon,i}\rightrightarrows U_{i}
on
[0,t],i\in{\mathpzc K}
. The rest is now a direct consequence of
[
66
, Theorem 13.6.3]
, because each
U_{i}
is strictly increasing.
∎

Our next lemma establishes convergence of
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s.

Lemma 8.3
.

As
\varepsilon\to 0
,
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s converge, almost surely, uniformly with respect to
t
in finite intervals, to nondecreasing, continuous and nonnegative
T_{i}
s uniquely characterized by the following conditions:

1.

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{i}(t)=t,t\geq 0
,

2.

U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2
,
U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}
s converge uniformly in compact subintervals to
U_{i}^{-1}
s on a set of probability
1
. In what follows we consider only events from this set.

Let
t>0
be fixed. Since
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{\varepsilon,i}(s)=s,s\in[0,t],\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s are nonnegative,
\max_{s\in[0,t]}T_{\varepsilon,i}(s)\leq t,i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover, for
s^{\prime}>s
, we have

0\leq T_{\varepsilon,i}(s^{\prime})-T_{\varepsilon,i}(s)=s^{\prime}-s-\sum_{j\not=i}[T_{\varepsilon,j}(s^{\prime})-T_{\varepsilon,j}(s)]\leq s^{\prime}-s,i\in{\mathpzc K}
, because
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s are nondecreasing. This shows that
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s are Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
1
. Hence, the Arzela–Ascoli theorem applies, showing that
T_{\varepsilon,i}
s, as restricted to
C[0,t]
, form a pre-compact set.

Hence, for any sequence
\left(\varepsilon_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of positive numbers not exceeding
1
and converging to
0
, there is a subsequence
\left(\varepsilon_{n_{k}}\right)_{k\geq 1}
such that, for each
i
,
T_{\varepsilon_{n_{k}},i}
s converge, as
k\to\infty
, uniformly in
[0,t]
, to a
T_{i}
. Clearly,
T_{i}
s are nonnegative, nondecreasing and continuous, and satisfy
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}T_{i}(s)=s,s\in[0,t]
. Moreover, since
U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}
does not depend on
i
, neither does
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}
. Therefore, one can argue as in the proof of uniqueness in Theorem
6.1
(see Lemma
6.2
in particular) that
T_{i}
s are determined uniquely. Since
\left(\varepsilon_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
was chosen arbitrarily, this completes the proof. ∎

Before presenting the third lemma we recall that our aim is to show that the right-hand sides of (
8.6
) converge, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to the right-hand side of (
8.3
) in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. The difficulty lies in the fact that neither addition nor composition is a continuous operation in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
, and both formulae involve such manipulations. Fortunately, Lemma

8.3
shows that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
B_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}
converge uniformly with respect to
t
in finite intervals, to a continuous process (equal to
B_{i}\circ T_{i}
), and in such a case Theorem 4.1 in
[
65
]
can be used to establish that our task comes down to proving that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}=U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}\qquad\text{in }D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}).

(8.9)

(The theorem reveals that sums of elements of the Skorokhod space converge to the sum of limits if the latter exist and at least one of them is continuous.) Moreover, to deal with compositions, we recall the following lemma; its part (a) comes from
[
37
, Lemma 3.3]
, and its part (b) — from
[
62
, Proposition 2.3]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
f_{\varepsilon},g_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\in[0,1]
be members of
D([0,\infty),{\mathbb{R}})

such that we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}f_{\varepsilon}=f_{0}
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}g_{\varepsilon}=g_{0}
in this Skorokhod space. Assume also that each
g_{\varepsilon}
is nonnegative and nondecreasing.

(a)

Assume that
g_{0}
is continuous and that if, for some
t\geq 0
,
g_{0}(t)
is a point of discontinuity of
f_{0}
then
g_{0}(t^{\prime})\not=g_{0}(t)
for all other
t^{\prime}\geq 0
. Then
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}f_{\varepsilon}\circ g_{\varepsilon}=f_{0}\circ g_{0}
in
D([0,\infty),{\mathbb{R}})
.

(b)

Assume that
g_{\varepsilon}
are unbounded and that
g_{0}
is strictly increasing. Then
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}g_{\varepsilon}\circ g_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=g_{0}\circ g_{0}^{-1}
in
D([0,\infty),{\mathbb{R}})
.

Lemma
8.4
will be used in conjunction with the following result.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\ell_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
be nondecreasing functions
\ell_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that do not have common levels of constancy. Furthermore, let
t_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
be nondecreasing functions
t_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(u)=u,u\geq 0
and
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

(a)

Suppose that for some
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,
a\geq 0
and
\epsilon>0
we have
\ell_{i}(a+\epsilon)=\ell_{i}(a)
, and, for some
s\geq 0
,
t_{i}(s)=a
. Then, for
u\in[s,s+\epsilon]
,

t_{i}(u)=a+u-s\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{j}(u)=t_{j}(s),\quad j\not=i,

that is, in this small interval,
t_{i}
grows linearly with slope
1
whereas the other
t_{j}
s remain constant.

(b)

Suppose that, for an
s>0
, we have
\ell_{i}(v)<\ell_{i}(t_{i}(s))
for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
v<t_{i}(s)
. Then
t_{i}(u)<t_{i}(s)
for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
u<s
.

Proof.

(a) Since
t_{j}
s are nondecreasing and
\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{j}(u)=u,u\geq 0
, we have
t_{i}(s)\leq t_{i}(u)\leq t_{i}(s)+u-s=a+u-s,u\geq s
. Thus,
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
stays equal
\ell_{i}(a)
for a short while: we have

\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}(u)=\ell_{i}(a),\qquad u\in[s,s+\epsilon],

(8.10)

because the inequality just established shows that, for
u
as above,

t_{i}(u)
lies in
[a,a+\epsilon]
and
\ell_{i}
is nondecreasing.

Next, recalling that
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}
does not depend on
j\in{\mathpzc K}
, we see that
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}(s)=\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}(s)=\ell_{i}(a),j\in{\mathpzc K}
. It follows that

\ell_{j}(v)>\ell_{j}(t_{j}(s)),\qquad v>t_{j}(s),j\not=i;

indeed,
\ell_{j}(t_{j}(s))=\ell_{i}(a)
cannot be a level of constancy of
\ell_{j},j\not=i
, being a level of constancy of
\ell_{i}
. This in turn proves the second part of our thesis, for the inequality
t_{j}(u)>t_{j}(s)
for some
j\not=i
and
u\in[s,s+\epsilon]
would imply
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}(u)>\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}(s)=\ell_{i}(a)
and so, in view of (
8.10
), contradict the fact that
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}
coincides with
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
. Finally, to see that the first part is a consequence of the second, we write

u={\textstyle\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}}t_{j}(u)=t_{i}(u)+{\textstyle\sum_{j\not=i}}t_{j}(s)=t_{i}(u)+s-t_{i}(s)=t_{i}(u)+s-a,u\in[s,s+\epsilon].

(b) Striving for a contradiction, assume that there is a
u<s
and an
i\in{\mathpzc K}
such that
t_{i}(u)=t_{i}(s)
. Since
\sum_{j\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{j}
is a strictly increasing function, there is a
j\in{\mathpzc K}
such that
t_{j}(u)<t_{j}(s)
and so
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}(u)<\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}(s)
whereas, clearly,
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}(u)=\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}(s)
. Now we have the required contradiction since by assumption
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
and
\ell_{j}\circ t_{j}
do not differ. ∎

8.3.
Approximation in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
; the main proposition

Here is the approximation theorem heralded before.

Proposition 8.6
.

For
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the right-hand sides of (
8.6
) converge almost surely, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to the right-hand side of (
8.3
) in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

As we have seen in Section
8.2
, it suffices to show (
8.9
). Let
i\in{\mathpzc K}
be fixed.

Step 1. Convergence of
U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}.

In Lemma
8.2
we have established that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}U_{\varepsilon,i}(t)=U_{i}(t)
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}(t)=U_{i}^{-1}(t)
for all
t\geq 0
almost surely, and the limit is uniform with respect to
t
in all compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
. It follows that
f_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U^{-1}_{\varepsilon,i}
converge to
f_{0}\coloneqq U_{i}\circ U^{-1}_{i}
in the sense of
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
, by Lemma
8.4
(b), because
U_{i}
is strictly increasing. At the same time,
g_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{i},\varepsilon\in[0,1]
converge trivially, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
g_{0}
. Since, moreover,
g_{0}
is continuous, to apply Lemma
8.4
(a) we need to check that, almost surely, for any point
u
where
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}
jumps, there is at most one time
t
such that
\mathcal{L}_{i}(t)=u
. But, if there are two distinct times with this property,
u
is a level of constancy of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
, and thus a point of jump of
\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{i}
. Hence, it suffices to show that, with probability
1
, the sets of times of jumps of
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}
are disjoint. However,
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}
is known to be a subordinator (see
[
7
]
*Thm. 8 p. 114) and thus the probability that it jumps at a given time is zero. Moreover,
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}
is càdlàg  and thus has at most countable number of jumps. Since
\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{i}
is independent of
U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}
, the probability that they have a common point of jump is zero also. This completes the proof.

Step 2. Convergence of
U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}.

Our strategy is to use Lemma
8.4
(a) again, this time with
f_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq U_{\varepsilon,i}\circ U^{-1}_{\varepsilon,i}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}
and
g_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq T_{\varepsilon,i},\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. Thus, by the first step and Lemma
8.3
, the task comes down to showing that, almost surely, if
u
is a point of jump of
U_{i}\circ U^{-1}_{i}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}
and, for a
t\geq 0
,
T_{i}(t)=u
, then
T_{i}(t^{\prime})\not=T_{i}(t)
for all nonnegative
t^{\prime}\not=t
.

We start our argument by recalling (see the proof of Theorem
6.1
) that processes
U_{j}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{j},j\in{\mathpzc K}
almost surely do not have common points of constancy. Hence, by disregarding a set of probability
0
, we can thus assume that the paths of
\ell_{j}\coloneqq U_{j}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{j}
and
t_{j}\coloneqq T_{j},j\in{\mathpzc K}
satisfy the main assumption of Lemma
8.5
.

For each such path, to show that the set
\{t\geq 0\colon T_{i}(t)=u\}
, which we know is nonempty, is a singleton, we let
t
be its minimum; this
t
belongs to the set because
T_{i}
is a continuous function. Moreover, since
u
is a level of constancy of
\ell_{i}
, Lemma
8.5
(a) tells us that
T_{i}(t^{\prime})>T_{i}(t)
for
t^{\prime}>t
. Moreover,
\ell_{i}(s)<\ell_{i}(t_{i}(t))
for
s<t_{i}(t)
because, by continuity,
t_{i}(t)=\min\{s\colon\ell_{i}(s)=u\}
. Finally, we have
\ell_{j}(s)<\ell_{j}(t_{j}(t))
for
s<t_{j}(t)
since
u=\ell_{j}(t_{j}(t))
is not a level of constancy of
\ell_{j},j\not=i
. Lemma
8.5
says therefore that
T_{i}(t^{\prime})<T_{i}(t)
for
t^{\prime}<t
, completing the proof. ∎

8.4.
Proof of Theorem
8.1

8.4.1.
The case of
\gamma=0
.

Theorem
7.2
says that the semigroups related to
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converge to that of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
. Thus, by the Trotter–Sova–Kurtz–Mackevičius Theorem, processes
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
converge in distribution to
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k})
and, as a consequence, in
[D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})]^{\mathpzc k}
as well.

Next, let

A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(t)\coloneqq t+\alpha L_{\varepsilon}(t)\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}(t),\quad t\geq 0,\varepsilon>0

(8.11)

(recall that
U_{\varepsilon,i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{\varepsilon,i}
does not depend on
i\in{\mathpzc K}
). By combined Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}=A_{\alpha}
(
A_{\alpha}
is defined in Theorem
8.1
) almost surely and uniformly in compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
. Hence, as a corollary to Proposition
8.6
, we see that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,
(W_{0,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}})_{i}\circ A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}^{-1}
(see (
8.6
)) converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
in
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
;
indeed, since
A_{\alpha}
is strictly increasing and continuous, so is its inverse, and thus Lemma
8.4
(a) is in force. In other words,

W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
, which, by Theorem
6.3
, coincides with

W_{0,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}\circ A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}
, converges almost surely in
[D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})]^{\mathpzc k}
to the process whose
i
th coordinate is
(B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i})\circ T_{i}\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
. Since almost sure convergence implies convergence in distribution, the process just described is identified as
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
. This completes the proof in the case of
\gamma=0
.

8.4.2.
The case of
\gamma>0
.

The difficulty with this case is technical in nature: the Trotter–Sova–Kurtz–Mackevičius Theorem is usually stated for processes that are honest, whereas
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
with
\gamma>0
is clearly undefined from a random time on. A natural way to circumvent this difficulty is to extend
S_{\mathsf{can}}
by adjoining to it an additional point (sometimes called the cemetery or coffin state, see
[
15
,
5
,
40
]
), and redefine
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
to make it honest by requiring that the new process is at the coffin state whenever the old is undefined. Below are the details.

(A) Analysis of resolvents.
We choose a point
\dagger
in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}\setminus S_{\mathsf{can}}
, let
S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger}
be the union of
S_{\mathsf{can}}
and
\{\dagger\}
, and let
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}
be the process with values in
S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger}
, given by

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}(t),&L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(t)<\zeta,\\
\dagger,&L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(t)\geq\zeta;\end{cases}

(8.12)

as before,
\zeta
is an exponentially distributed random variable with parameter
\gamma
, which is independent of
B
and
\mathcal{U}
, and
L_{\varepsilon}
and
A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}
are defined in (
8.11
). It is clear, see Theorem
6.3
, that
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}
equals
\dagger
whenever
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
is undefined, but otherwise these processes coincide (needless to say, the former process started at
\dagger
stays at this point forever).

It follows that the related semigroup, say,
T_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
, in
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
is given by
T_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(t)f(\dagger)=f(\dagger)
and

T_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(t)f(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}f(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}})+\mathsf{P}_{x}[L_{\varepsilon}\circ A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}^{-1}(t)\geq\zeta]f(\dagger),x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
for
t\geq 0
and
f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger});
this semigroup is clearly honest. There is also a handy counterpart of this equality in terms of resolvents. To wit, denoting by
R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
the Laplace transform of
T_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
, and viewing
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
as the subspace of
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
of functions that vanish at
\dagger
, we have

R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}f=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}f+\lambda^{-1}f(\dagger)\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon},\qquad f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})\subset\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})

and
\lambda R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}}=\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}},\lambda>0
. Here,

•

as in Section
7
,
\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}
is the generator of
W_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
,

•

\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
equals
1
at
\dagger
and is
0
otherwise,

•

\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}
is seen as an operator mapping the subspace
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})\subset\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
to itself,

•

\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
is the Laplace transform of the lifetime of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
, that is,
\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}=\mathsf{1}_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}-\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}
, where
\mathsf{1}_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}\in\mathfrak{(}S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
equals
1
for
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
and is
0
otherwise.

This formula, in turn, shows that the strong limit
R_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
exists and is given by

R_{\lambda}^{\dagger}f=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1}f+\lambda^{-1}f(\dagger)\ell_{\lambda},\qquad f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})\subset\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})

and
\lambda R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}}=\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}},\lambda>0
, where
\ell_{\lambda}\coloneqq\mathsf{1}_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}-\lambda\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1}\mathsf{1}_{S_{\mathsf{can}}}
is the Laplace transform of the lifetime of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
. Indeed, in Lemma
7.1
and Theorem
7.2
we have established that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda,\varepsilon}=\left(\lambda-\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\right)^{-1},\lambda>0
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that
R_{\lambda}^{\dagger},\lambda>0
is a Feller resolvent: for example, the condition
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R_{\lambda}^{\dagger}f=f,f\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
can be established by noting that
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
is a direct sum of
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
and the subspace spanned by
\mathsf{1}_{\{\dagger\}}
, where convergence is obvious. It follows thus that there is a Feller process
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}
with values in
S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger}
, that is a limit in the sense of distributions, of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}
.

(B) Analysis of paths.
In order to describe the limit process in more detail, let us first take a closer look at the time, say,
\mathpzc t
, at which
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}
reaches
\dagger
. For
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
this is the lifetime of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
, and for
x=\dagger
this is
0
. Since the Laplace transform of the lifetime of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
started at an
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
is
\lambda\mapsto\ell_{\lambda}(x)
, the Laplace transform of the time at which
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}
started at an
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger}
reaches
\dagger
is
\lambda\mapsto\ell_{\lambda}^{\dagger}(x)
, where
\ell_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
is obtained from
\ell_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
by assigning it value
1
at
\dagger
. Analogously, the Laplace transform of the time, say,
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
, at which
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
started at an
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger}
reaches
\dagger
is
\lambda\mapsto\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(x)
, where
\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
coincides with
\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}
on
S_{\mathsf{can}}
, and equals
1
at
\dagger
. Since, as we have seen in point (A),
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}=\ell_{\lambda}
in
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}})
, we have also

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\ell_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}=\ell_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
in
\mathfrak{C}(S_{\mathsf{can}}^{\dagger})
, and this means that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}=\mathpzc t
weakly.

Formula (
8.12
) can be used to provide yet more specific information on
\mathpzc t
, and, in particular, to prove that
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
converge to
\mathpzc t
almost surely. First of all, the formula reveals that

\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{t\geq 0\colon L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(t)=\zeta\}.

(8.13)

To proceed, we recall the following real analysis result. Suppose that, for some
t_{0}
, we are given nondecreasing, continuous
functions
f_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,t_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
starting at
0
that converge uniformly to a function
f
. Suppose also that, for an
a>0

there is a
\mathpzc t\in(0,t_{0})
such that
f(\mathpzc t)=a
, but
a
is not a level of constancy of
f
. Then for
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0\colon f_{\varepsilon}(t)=a\},\varepsilon>0
the limit of
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
exists and equals
\mathpzc t
. Indeed, for any
s\in(\mathpzc t,t_{0}]
, we have
f(s)>a
,
because
a>0
is not a level of constancy of
f
. Therefore, for
\varepsilon
small enough,
f_{\varepsilon}(s)>a
, and this, by the Darboux property, implies that
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}<s
. Since a similar argument shows that, for
s\in[0,\mathpzc t)
,
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}>s
as long as
\varepsilon
is small enough, the result is established. (To see that the claim is no longer true if the assumption that
a
is not a level of constancy is dropped, consider
t_{0}=2,

a=1
,
f(t)=\min(t,1),t\in[0,2]
and piecewise linear
f_{\varepsilon}
with the graph connecting points
(0,0),(1,1-\varepsilon)
and
(2,1)
.)

Armed with this information we come back to
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
of (
8.13
). By combined Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(t)=L\circ A_{\alpha}(t)

(8.14)

uniformly with respect to
t
in compact intervals. Moreover, a level of constancy of
L\circ A_{\alpha}
is a level of constancy of
L
and thus a point of jump of
\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is independent of
\zeta
and this random variable has continuous distribution, with probability one
\zeta
is not a level of constancy of
L\circ A_{\alpha}
. It follows that the real analysis result proved above applies, and we infer that
t_{\varepsilon}
converge, almost surely, to the time when
L\circ A_{\alpha}
reaches the level of
\zeta
, that is, to

\mathpzc t=\min\{t\geq 0\colon L\circ A_{\alpha}(t)=\zeta\}.

(8.15)

Finally, we will show that the paths of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}
are obtained from those of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
by requiring that they are equal to
\dagger
from
\mathpzc t
on. In this argument we will be aided by the following result, which can be thought of as an exercise in using Skorokhod’s metric. Assume that
f_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon>0
are members of
D([0,\infty),S_{\mathsf{can}})
, and that each coordinate of
f_{\varepsilon}
converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to the corresponding coordinate of
f
in the Skorokhod topology. Suppose, moreover, that
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
are positive numbers converging to a
\mathpzc t>0
and that
\mathpzc t
is a point of continuity of
f
. Then, for
\widehat{f}_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon>0
defined as

\widehat{f}_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}f_{\varepsilon}(t),&t<\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon},\\
\dagger,&t\geq\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon},\end{cases}

each coordinate of
\widehat{f}_{\varepsilon}
converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to the corresponding coordinate of
\widehat{f}
in the Skorokhod metric. This result has a bearing on the issue at hand, because (a) we know from the case of
\gamma=0
that for almost all paths, each coordinate of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
, converges in the Skorokhod topology to the corresponding coordinate of

W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
, (b) we have established that, almost surely,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}=\mathpzc t
, and (c) points of discontinuity of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
are precisely points of jumps of
\mathcal{U}
, and since
\mathcal{U}
is independent of
\zeta
, a continuous random variable, the probability that
\mathpzc t
is a point of discontinuity of
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
is zero. Thus, paths of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}
converge, almost surely, to those given by
W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}(t)
for
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)<\zeta
and
\dagger
otherwise.

On the other hand, we know from the analysis of resolvents and the Trotter–Sova–Kurtz–Mackevičius Theorem that the processes
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}^{\dagger}
converge in distribution to
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}
. Hence,

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}^{\dagger}(t)=\begin{cases}W_{\alpha,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}(t),&L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)<\zeta,\\
\dagger,&L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}(t)\geq\zeta.\end{cases}

Since this establishes (
8.4
), our proof is completed, save for the fact that
L
is a local time of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
.

To reach our last goal, let
i\in{\mathpzc K}
be such that
\mathpzc m_{i}
, the restriction of
\mathpzc m
to the
i
th ray, is infinite, and let us take a look at the process

X\coloneqq B_{i}+U_{i}\circ U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}.

Similarly as the process in (
6.17
),
X
is a Brownian motion on the right half-line with jumps from
0
, but, since
\mathpzc m_{i}
is infinite, these jumps tend to be ‘infinitely small’ and occur ‘infinitely often’. Such processes are described in detail in
[
11
]
. In particular, it follows from
[
11
, p. 68]
that the local time of
X
is
U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}
, and that, almost surely with respect to any initial distribution,

U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\frac{\sum_{s\leq t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s-)=0,X(s)>\varepsilon\}}}{\mathpzc m_{i}([\varepsilon,\infty))},\qquad t\geq 0.

Hence

\displaystyle L(t)=U_{i}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{i}\circ T_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\frac{\sum_{s\leq T_{i}(t)}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(s-)=0,X(s)>\varepsilon\}}}{\mathpzc m_{i}([\varepsilon,\infty))}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\frac{\sum_{s\leq t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{X(T_{i}(s-))=0,\ X(T_{i}(s))>\varepsilon\}}}{\mathpzc m_{i}([\varepsilon,\infty))}\qquad\text{a.s.}

because function
T_{i}
is continuous and monotonous. Since
X\circ T_{i}
is the
i
th coordinate of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
, this formula shows that
L
is a continuous additive functional of
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
. Moreover,
L
does not grow except when
W_{0,\beta,0,\mathpzc m}
is at
\mathfrak{0}
. It follows that
L
is a local time for this processes and thus we are done.

8.5.
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
without assumption (
8.1
)

In this section we sketch a picture of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
in the case where (
8.1
) is violated. To begin with, since
\mathpzc m
is assumed to be infinite, we have a nonempty set of indices
\mathpzc J
such that

\text{ the $\mathpzc m$ measure of the $i$th ray is infinite whenever }\beta_{i}=0,\qquad i\in\mathpzc J.

(8.16)

Let
S_{\mathpzc J}
and
S_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}
be the subgraphs of
S
formed by edges with indices in
\mathpzc J
and
{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J
, respectively. Also, let
\mathpzc m_{\mathpzc J}
and
\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}
be the restrictions of
\mathpzc m
to
S_{\mathpzc J}
and
S_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}
, respectively.

This scenario naturally splits into two sub-cases: the first, a bit simpler one, where
\mathpzc m
is concentrated on
\mathpzc J,
and the one where it is not. In the first of these,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is in a sense identical with a process of the type described in Theorem
8.1
having values in the subgraph
SJ
of
S
. To wit,

W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}\overset{\text{ident.}}{=}W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},\gamma,\mathpzc mJ},

(8.17)

where
\beta^{\natural}
is obtained from
\beta
by throwing away all its zero coordinates with indexes in
\mathpzc{K\setminus J}
.
To elaborate: When started in
S_{\mathpzc J}
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is indistinguishable from
W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},\gamma,\mathpzc mJ}
; in particular, despite frequently going through
\mathfrak{0}
, it never enters the proper subgraph
S{K\setminus J}
. Also, when started in one of the edges of
S_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}
, the process behaves like a one-dimensional Brownian motion until the time when it reaches
\mathfrak{0}
; from that time on, it behaves like
W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},\gamma,\mathpzc mJ}
and ‘does not see’ the subgraph
S_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}
anymore.

When
\mathpzc m\not=\mathpzc m_{\mathpzc J}
, the situation changes: the fact that
\mathpzc m_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}>0
allows the process to return to the previously omitted subgraph
S_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}
(or at least its part; there may be edges of
S_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}
with
\mathpzc m
measure
0
). More precisely,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}

turns out to be a concatenation of an infinite number of copies of processes on
S
that can be identified with
W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},\gamma+|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|,\mathpzc mJ}
as in (
8.17
). This means that

(a)

when started in
S{K\setminus J}
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
initially behaves like a one-dimensional Brownian motion,

(b)

from the moment it reaches
\mathfrak{0}
(or if started at
SJ
), it behaves like
W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},\gamma+|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|,\mathpzc mJ}
,

(c)

when the local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta^{\natural},0,\mathpzc mJ}
(the process
L\circ A_{\alpha}^{-1}
in Theorem
8.1
) reaches the level of an independent exponential random variable with parameter
\gamma+|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is killed with probability
\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|}
or resurrected with probability
\frac{|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|}{\gamma+|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|}
; in the latter case, its new starting point lies in
S_{\mathpzc{K\setminus J}}
and is distributed according to
\frac{\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}}{|\mathpzc m_{{\mathpzc K}\setminus\mathpzc J}|}
; of course, the process forgets its past and starts all over again.

9.
\lambda
-potential of the local time (
8.5
)

This section is devoted to a further information on the local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
. This information will allow us to conclude in particular that
\overline{\beta}K
is the symmetric local time.

Notably, local times of a Markov process at a given point differ by multiplicative constants, but are fully determined by their
\lambda
-potentials, see
[
11
, §III.3]
. We will calculate the
\lambda
-potential
u_{\lambda}
of the local time
K
(the latter being spelled out in (
8.5
)); to recall, the
\lambda
-potential of
K
is defined, for
x\in S
and
\lambda>0
, by

u_{\lambda}(x)\coloneqq\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K(s).

Our result is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose that assumption (
8.1
) is satisfied. Then

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\,\overline{\beta}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\mathpzc l}_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0.

(9.1)

As a corollary we also obtain:

Theorem 9.2
.

Assume that
\overline{\beta}>0
. Then
the process
\overline{\beta}K
is the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m},
that is,

\overline{\beta}K(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s)|<\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s

in probability with respect to all underlying measures
\mathsf{P}_{x}
.

9.1.
Background material

For the proof of these two theorems we need a background material, including some facts from the general theory of
\lambda
-potentials (Lemmas
9.3
and
9.4
, and Theorem
9.5
) and a useful estimate (Lemma
9.6
). We start with the first of the lemmas.

Lemma 9.3
.

For
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, let

K_{\varepsilon}(t),t\geq 0
be a family of random variables defined on a probability space that is common for all these families. Suppose,
furthermore, that the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

For all
\omega
, the paths
[0,\infty)\ni s\mapsto K_{\varepsilon}(s,\omega)
are continuous and nondecreasing with
K_{\varepsilon}(0,\omega)=0,\varepsilon\in[0,1]
.

(b)

There is a function
\chi\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathsf{E}[K_{\varepsilon}(s)]^{2}\leq\chi(s),

s\geq 0,
whereas
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\sqrt{\chi(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty
for all
\lambda>0
.

(c)

For all
s\geq 0
,
K_{\varepsilon}(s)
converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
K_{0}(s)
in probability.

Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}(s)=\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{0}(s).

Proof.

The monotone convergence theorem, integration by parts formula and the Tonelli’s theorem, yield, for
\lambda>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}(s)

\displaystyle=\lim_{u\to\infty}\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{u}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}(s)=\lim_{u\to\infty}\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{u}\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}K_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}K_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\mathsf{E}K_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad

(9.2)

because the estimate
\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathsf{E}K_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\sqrt{\mathsf{E}[K_{\varepsilon}(u)]^{2}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\sqrt{\chi(u)}
shows that
\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathsf{E}K_{\varepsilon}(u)
converges to
0
as
u\to\infty
.

Next, for each
u\geq 0
, since
K_{\varepsilon}(u)
converges to
K_{0}(u)
in probability, there is a sequence
\left(\varepsilon_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
such that
K_{\varepsilon_{n}}(u)
converges to
K_{0}(u)
almost surely. The Fatou lemma implies thus that
\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathsf{E}K_{0}(u)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathsf{E}K_{\varepsilon}(u)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sqrt{\mathsf{E}[K_{\varepsilon}(u)]^{2}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\sqrt{\chi(u)}
, and this allows extending the calculation (
9.2
) to
\varepsilon=0
also.

Finally, for any
u\geq 0
, by (b), the second moments of
random variables
K_{\varepsilon}(u),\varepsilon\in(0,1]
are uniformly bounded, and so the variables are
uniformly integrable (see e.g.
[
33
]
*Theorem 4.2, p. 215). Hence, their expectations converge:
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathsf{E}K_{\varepsilon}(u)=\mathsf{E}K_{0}(u)
, see e.g.
[
33
]
*Theorem 5.4, p. 221. In the light of this information, our thesis is a consequence of the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem combined with (
9.2
) extended to include
\varepsilon=0
— here the estimate of point (b) is used again.
∎

Turning to the rudiments of the theory of
\lambda
-potentials, let
K
be a continuous additive functional of a Markov process taking values in a complete separable metric space
S
. The function
\varphi=\varphi_{t}
given by

\varphi_{t}(x)\coloneqq\mathsf{E}_{x}K(t),\qquad t\geq 0,x\in S

is said to be its
characteristic
, see
[
23
]
. The following proposition gathers basic estimates of the growth of the characteristic.

Lemma 9.4
.

Assume that there is a
t>0
such that
\kappa_{0}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in S}\varphi_{t}(x)<\infty
. Then

(a)

\sup_{x\in S}\varphi_{s}(x)\leq\kappa(1+s),s\geq 0,
where
\kappa\coloneqq\max(\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}/t),
and

(b)

\sup_{x\in S}\mathsf{E}_{x}[K(s)]^{n}\leq n!(\sup_{x\in S}\varphi_{s}(x))^{n}
for
n\geq 1,s\geq 0
(in what follows we will use this inequality only with
n=2
).

Proof.

The functional equation that characterizes continuous additive functionals (see e.g.
[
53
]
*p. 83) implies that

\mathsf{E}_{x}K(t+h)\leq\mathsf{E}_{x}K(t)+\sup_{y\in S}\mathsf{E}_{y}K(h),\qquad x\in S,t,h\geq 0,

that is,

\varphi_{t+h}(x)\leq\varphi_{t}(x)+\sup_{y\in S}\varphi_{h}(y),\qquad x\in S,t,h\geq 0.

Let now
t>0
be the real number described in the assumption. Given
s\geq 0
one can find the natural
n
such that
s\in[nt,(n+1)t)
. Then, by the relation established above and the fact that
[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto K(t)
is nondecreasing with
K(0)=0
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{(n+1)t}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}[\varphi_{(i+1)t}(x)-\varphi_{it}(x)]\leq(n+1)\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{0}(1+\tfrac{s}{t})\leq\kappa(1+s).

Since
s
is arbitrary, this shows (a).
Point (b) follows from
[
30
]
*Ch II, §6, Lemma 3. ∎

Lemma
9.4
shows that in the case of
\lambda
-potentials there is a natural and rather simple condition guaranteeing that assumption (b) of Lemma
9.3
is satisfied. In what follows we will combine these lemmas to deduce convergence of
\lambda
-potentials of local times of interest from the convergence of local times themselves (see the proofs of Theorems
9.1
and
9.2
given further down).
For the proof of Theorem
9.2
we will also need the following theorem showing that, vice versa, sometimes convergence of local times can be inferred from the convergence of their
\lambda
-potentials.

Theorem 9.5
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda},\varepsilon\in(0,1]
be
\lambda
-potentials of continuous additive functionals
K_{\varepsilon}
of Feller processes
X_{\varepsilon}
with values in a locally compact metric space
S
:

u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)=\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}(s),\qquad x\in S.

Assume that

(a)

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{x\in S}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)<\infty
,
\lambda>0
, and

(b)

u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}
converge, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly with respect to
x\in S
to a function
u_{\lambda},\lambda>0
.

Then
u_{\lambda},\lambda>0
is a
\lambda
-potential of a continuous additive functional
K
, and
for all
t\geq 0
,
K_{\varepsilon}(t)
converges in probability, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
K(t)
with respect to all measures
\mathsf{P}_{x},x\in S
.
