Unfortunately, a detailed proof of this theorem is quite long and requires discussing a number of notions we would rather avoid discussing in this paper. Neither could we find such a proof in the existing literature. Hence, we need to restrict ourselves to stating that the theorem can be established by following the arguments presented in the proof of Theorem 3.8 on page 162 in
[
10
]
;
we are grateful to Prof. Patrick J. Fitzsimmons for this observation. We note also that a similar result, stating that uniform convergence of characteristics entails convergence of continuous additive functionals, is contained in Theorem 6.4 Ch. VI, §3 of
[
23
]
.

9.2.
Proof of Theorem
9.1

To complete the preparations for the proof of Theorem
9.1
, as mentioned earlier, we need estimates that will allow us to use Lemma
9.3

and Theorem
9.5
in the particular case of additive continuous functionals of interest to us — these estimates are gathered in Lemma
9.6
, further down. To set the stage for the lemma, let
M_{t},t\geq 0
be the minimal Brownian motion on the star graph
S
with
{\mathpzc k}
edges. The relation

\displaystyle R_{\lambda}^{0}g(i,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{E}_{(i,x)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}g(M_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}|x-y|}-\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}(x+y)})g(i,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2C_{i}(g)\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{x}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}(x-y)g(i,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

(9.3)

an incarnation of (
3.10
), has been established for
g\in C(S)
, but a standard procedure extends it to all integrable
g
(for
x\geq 0
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
). Hence, it makes sense to speak of
R_{\lambda}^{0}g
for integrable functions
g
also; for such
g
,
R_{\lambda}^{0}g
is the continuous function defined by the right-hand side of (
9.3
). In the specific instance of
g_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\in(0,1]
defined by

g_{\varepsilon}(x)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|x|<\varepsilon\}},\qquad x\in S,

(9.4)

we can similarly extend (
7.9
). Indeed, each
g_{\varepsilon}
is a pointwise limit of a sequence of continuous functions vanishing at
\mathfrak{0}
and
having a common integrable bound. Hence, (
7.9
) together with the dominated convergence theorem yield

\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t=R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x),

(9.5)

for
\,x\in S,\lambda>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
where

\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})=\frac{2\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}C_{i}(g_{\varepsilon})+\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m},

whereas
C_{i}(g_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon\sqrt{2\lambda}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}y}\,\mathrm{d}y
,
i\in{\mathpzc K},\varepsilon\in(0,1],\lambda>0
.

Lemma 9.6
.

For each
\lambda>0
, there is a constant
\kappa_{\lambda}
such that

|R^{0}_{\lambda}g(i,x)|\leq\kappa_{\lambda}(1\wedge x)\|g\|_{L^{1}(S)},\qquad x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

(9.6)

for all integrable
g
on
S
, where, of course,
\|g\|_{L^{1}(S)}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(i,y)|\,\mathrm{d}y
.
Moreover, for each
t>0
,

\max_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\,\sup_{x\geq 0}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathsf{E}_{(i,x)}\int_{0}^{t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

Proof.

The expression in the second line of (
9.3
) makes it clear that
|R_{\lambda}^{0}g(i,x)|
does not exceed
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\|g\|_{L^{1}(S)}
for all
x\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\lambda>0.
However, for
x\in(0,1]
we can estimate differently: using the other expression, we see that

\displaystyle|R_{\lambda}^{0}g(i,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(2C_{i}(g)+\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{x}|g(i,y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\right)\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\|g\|_{L^{1}(S)}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x\leq 2x\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\sup_{y\in(0,1]}\tfrac{\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y}{y}\|g\|_{L^{1}(S)},

and the supremum is finite because so is
\lim_{y\to 0+}\tfrac{\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y}{y}
. These two inequalities render (
9.6
) with
\kappa_{\lambda}
being the larger of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}
and
2\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\lambda}}\sup_{y\in(0,1]}\tfrac{\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y}{y}
.
(The case of
x=0
is immediate since
R_{\lambda}^{0}g(i,0)=0
,
i\in{\mathpzc K},\lambda>0
.)

To establish the rest, we write

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{(i,x)}\int_{0}^{t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}\mathsf{E}_{(i,x)}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}(R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}(\tfrac{{\mathpzc k}\kappa_{\lambda}}{2}+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})),

(9.7)

where first (
9.5
) and then (
9.6
) was used (together with
\|g_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}(S)}=\frac{{\mathpzc k}}{2}
). This reduces our task to showing that
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})
is finite, and for this it suffices to show that the supremum over
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
of the numerators of
\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})
is finite. However, since
0\leq C_{i}(g_{\varepsilon})\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\lambda}}
, the latter supremum does not exceed
\overline{\beta}+\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
. This is finite by (
9.6
).
∎

We are finally ready to present the proof of Theorem
9.1
. Here is its plan.

(i)

First we consider the case where
\mathpzc m
is finite,
\beta_{i}>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
; parameters
\alpha\geq 0
and
\gamma\geq 0
are chosen arbitrarily. Here, we use the fact that
K
can be approximated by the continuous additive functionals
K_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\in(0,1]
defined by

K_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{0<|W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s)|\leq\varepsilon\}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s,

(9.8)

for
t\geq 0
, employ the previously obtained formula (
9.5
) for their
\lambda
-potentials, and justify passage to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(ii)

Next, we drop the assumption
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
by means of (
6.19
) and (
6.21
).

(iii)

To treat the general case, we approximate
K
by local times of the type considered in (i) and (ii).

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

(i)
Assume that
\mathpzc m
is finite (perhaps even zero),
\beta_{i}>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
; parameters
\alpha\geq 0
and
\gamma\geq 0
are chosen arbitrarily.
In particular,
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
where
\delta\geq 0
and
\mu
is a probability measure. Then, by the definition of Section
6.4
,
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}=W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\mu}
is
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma,\delta,\mu}W_{\alpha,\beta}
. Therefore, as expounded in Section
6.1
, the local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is constructed by means of a sequence of copies of the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta}
, that is, of the local time given by (
5.19
). It follows that for each
s\geq 0
the value
{K(s)}
of the local time is the almost sure limit, and hence also the limit in probability (for the all underlying probability measures
\mathsf{P}_{x}
) of
K_{\varepsilon}(s)
defined in (
9.8
).

This suggests that we could perhaps use Lemma
9.3
(with
K_{0}\coloneqq K
) to conclude that
\lambda
-potentials of
K_{\varepsilon}
s converge to that of
K
— we have just discovered that condition (c) of the lemma holds. To check the other conditions, we recall that, by the second part of Lemma
9.6
, the characteristics of the continuous additive functionals
K_{\varepsilon}
have a common finite bound: for each
t\geq 0
,

\sup_{x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathsf{E}_{x}K_{\varepsilon}(t)<\infty.

Lemma
9.4
then implies that, for a certain (finite) constant
\kappa>0
,

\sup_{x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathsf{E}_{x}[K_{\varepsilon}(s)]^{2}\leq 2\kappa^{2}(1+s)^{2},\qquad s\geq 0.

Hence, also condition (b) of Lemma
9.3
is satisfied. Since condition (a) is obvious, we infer that
the
\lambda
-potential
u_{\lambda}
of
{K}
is given by (see (
9.5
))

u_{\lambda}(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}(R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x)),

x\in S
. This limit further reduces to
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x)
, because, as can be seen from the third line in (
9.3
),
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=0
for each
x\in S
. (Indeed, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}C_{i}(g_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\lambda}}
, and so the first term in
R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)
converges to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x
; since the limit of the second term is also equal to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x
, the claim follows.)

Now, the denominator in
\overline{C}_{\lambda}(g_{\varepsilon})
does not depend on
\varepsilon
and equals
\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{\mathsf{}}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
because
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
by assumption. Also, the numerator involves two summands, of which the first, by
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}C_{i}(g_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\lambda}}
, converges to
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
. We have also proved that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=0
for each
x\in S
. This, however, when combined with (
9.6
), yields by the dominated convergence theorem that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m=0
.
Hence,

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\mathpzc l}_{\lambda}^{0},\qquad\lambda>0,

(9.9)

establishing (
9.1
) in the first case.

(ii)
Assume now that the assumption
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=1
is violated, but the remaining ones are satisfied. Formulae (
6.19
) and (
6.21
) tell us that in this case the
\lambda
-potential of the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
is the
\lambda
-potential of the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha/\overline{\beta},\beta/\overline{\beta},\gamma/\overline{\beta},\mathpzc m/\overline{\beta}}
divided by
\overline{\beta}
. This shows that (
9.1
) holds also in this case.

(iii)
We finally allow some of
\beta_{i}
s to vanish, but compensate this by assumption (
8.1
). In order to extend our formula to this case, we approximate
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
by processes
W_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}}
as in Sections
7
and

8
. More precisely, we take positive
\beta_{i}(\varepsilon),{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
that converge to
\beta_{i}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and define finite measures
\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\delta(\varepsilon)\mu_{\varepsilon}
as the restrictions of
\mathpzc m
to the sets
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
; as before,
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\subset S
is composed of points in
S
that lie at a distance
\geq\varepsilon
from the graph’s center. Then, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

•

L_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
8.11
) is the symmetric local time of
W_{0,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}
,

•

L_{\varepsilon}\circ A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}^{-1}
(see (
8.11
) again) is the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),0,\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}
,

•

and the symmetric local time of
W_{\alpha,\beta(\varepsilon),\gamma,\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}
, say,
K^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}
, (the superscript
\sharp
is to distinguish this local time from
K_{\varepsilon}
of (
9.8
)) defined by an analogue of (
6.25
), can in view of definition (
8.13
) be written as

K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}(t)=L_{\varepsilon}\circ A_{\varepsilon,\alpha}^{-1}(t\wedge\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}),\qquad t\geq 0.

(9.10)

Since the coordinates of
\beta(\varepsilon)
are positive and
\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}
is finite and formula (
9.1
) has been establish for such parameters, the
\lambda
-potential of
K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}(s)=\frac{1}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\;\overline{\beta(\varepsilon)}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}{\mathpzc l}_{\lambda}(x).

(9.11)

The right-hand side above converges to
\frac{1}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\overline{\beta}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}{\mathpzc l}_{\lambda}(x)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, because
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{S}1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m=\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
(by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem) and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\beta(\varepsilon)=\beta
. Hence, to establish (
9.1
) it suffices to show that the left-hand side converges to
\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K(s)
, the
\lambda
-potential of
K
evaluated at
x
.

To this end we will use Lemma
9.3
for
K_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}
and
K_{0}\coloneqq K
. Let us fix
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
. First of all, it is clear that condition (a) of the lemma is satisfied. Secondly,
we recall formula (
8.14
) speaking on convergence of
L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(s)
to
L\circ A_{\alpha}(s)
, convergence that is uniform with respect to
s
in finite intervals, and the fact established in Section
8.4.2
(part (B)) that
\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon}
converges to
\mathpzc t
of (
8.15
) almost surely with respect to
\mathsf{P}_{x}
(we recall that forumla (
8.14
) is established under standing assumption (
8.1
)). We thus infer that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}(s)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}L_{\varepsilon}\circ A^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(s\wedge\mathpzc t_{\varepsilon})=L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(s\wedge\mathpzc t),\qquad s\geq 0

(9.12)

in the mode that is stronger than convergence in probability (in
\mathsf{P}_{x}
); moreover, in view of (
8.15
), we recognize
L\circ A^{-1}_{\alpha}(s\wedge\mathpzc t)
as
K(s)
. This shows that condition (c) of the lemma is satisfied also. Finally, we estimate as in (
9.7
) to see that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}}\mathsf{E}_{x}K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}

\displaystyle(t)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}}\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}(s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\sup_{x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}}\ \frac{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\;\overline{\beta(\varepsilon)}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}{\mathpzc l}_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\;\overline{\beta(\varepsilon)}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}}

Since the last fraction converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0+
, to a finite limit and
\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}\mathsf{1}_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m_{\varepsilon}
increases when
\varepsilon
decreases, by redefining
\beta(\varepsilon)
if necessary for
\varepsilon
s that are not so close to
0
, we can make sure that the supremum in the last line is finite. This, however, as in the proof of part (i), allows inferring that condition (b) of the lemma is also satisfied.

Hence, the left-hand side of (
9.11
) converges to
\mathsf{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}K(s)
, as desired. This completes the proof. ∎

9.3.
Proof of Theorem
9.2

In terms of functions
g_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
9.4
), our task is to show that

\overline{\beta}K(t)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0

in probability with respect to all underlying measures
\mathsf{P}_{x}
. The idea is to use Theorem
9.5
which allows deducing such convergence from the convergence of the
\lambda
-potentials, say,
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}
, of
K_{\varepsilon}
, where
K_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}g_{\varepsilon}(W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s
,
t\geq 0
. We have

u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)=R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})\mathpzc l_{\lambda}^{0}(x),\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,1],\lambda>0,

see (
9.5
) and the definition of
\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})
following this formula. We need to find a common bound for
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)
and the limit
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)
.

(i)
Starting with the latter, we argue as in the last lines of part (i) of the proof of Theorem
9.1
that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})=\frac{\overline{\beta}}{\lambda\alpha+\sqrt{2\lambda}\overline{\beta}+\gamma+\lambda\int_{S}R_{\lambda}^{0}1_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m}
(note that now
\overline{\beta}
need not be
1
). In the same part of the proof just mentioned, but a bit earlier, we have also shown that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=0,x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
; this allows us to conclude, by (
9.1
),
that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)
exists and coincides with the
\lambda
-potential of
\overline{\beta}K
. However, assumption (a) of Theorem
9.5
speaks of convergence that is uniform with respect to
x
, and thus we still need to show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=0
uniformly in
x\in S_{\mathsf{can}}
.

To this end, we take a look at the second line in (
9.3
); this line reveals that
\sqrt{2\lambda}R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)
can be written as

\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y\,\mathrm{d}y

for
x\geq\varepsilon
, and as

\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}x}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{x}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y\,\mathrm{d}y+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x\int_{x}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2\lambda}y}\,\mathrm{d}y
for
x\in[0,\varepsilon]
. The first expression above does not exceed
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y\,\mathrm{d}y
and the second does not exceed
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y\,\mathrm{d}y+\max_{0\leq x\leq\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x
. Since
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}\varepsilon=0
implies
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}y\,\mathrm{d}y=0
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\max_{0\leq x\leq\varepsilon}\sinh\sqrt{2\lambda}x=0
, this completes the proof of uniform convergence of
R_{\lambda}^{0}g_{\varepsilon}(x)
to
0
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(ii)
To find a bound for
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)
we estimate as in (
9.7
):

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{{\mathpzc k}\kappa_{\lambda}}{2}+\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon}),

(9.13)

where again (
9.6
) was used together with
\|g_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}(S)}=\frac{{\mathpzc k}}{2}
.
Since we know from the end of the proof of Lemma
9.6
that
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\overline{C}(g_{\varepsilon})
is finite, this shows that assumption (a) of Theorem
9.5
is satisfied.

The theorem is thus applicable and says that
K_{\varepsilon}
s converge to a CAF whose
\lambda
-potential coincides with the
\lambda
-potential of
K
. But
\lambda
-potentials characterize CAFs uniquely;
K_{\varepsilon}
s must converge therefore to
K
. This completes the proof.

10.
Formulae for
T_{i}
s

Our goal in this section is to express explicitly the time-change processes
T_{i},1\leq i\leq{\mathpzc k}
featured in the representation of the process
W_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\mathpzc m}
given in Theorem
8.1
. Our argument hinges on Proposition
10.1
(presented below) that belongs to the realm of real analysis, and complements Lemma
6.2
. To recall, the problem at hand is whether, having given continuous nondecreasing functions
\ell_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\ell_{i}(0)=0
,
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, one can find a collection of functions
t_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(s)=s,s\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}\text{ does not depend on }i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(10.1)

Lemma
6.2
establishes that the solution to the problem is unique provided that
\ell_{i}
s do not have common levels of constancy. Proposition
10.1
, in turn, says that a solution always exists, and under assumption of no levels of constancy accompanied by
\lim_{s\to\infty}\ell_{i}(s)=\infty,i\in{\mathpzc K}
, provides a representation of
t_{i}
s in terms of functions

\mathcal{k}_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K},\mathcal{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{l}

defined as follows:

•

\mathcal{k}_{i}\coloneqq\ell_{i}^{-1}
is the generalized inverse of
\ell_{i}
, that is,

\mathcal{k}_{i}(s)\coloneqq\inf\{u\geq 0\colon\ell_{i}(u)>s\},\qquad s\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

•

\mathcal{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{i}
, and

•

\mathcal{l}\coloneqq\mathcal{k}^{-1}
is the generalized inverse of
\mathcal{k}
.

We note that
\mathcal{k}_{i}
s are well-defined provided that
\lim_{s\to\infty}\ell_{i}(s)=\infty,i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{k}_{i}
s are strictly increasing since
\ell_{i}
s are continuous. It follows that
\mathcal{k}
is strictly increasing as well, and thus
\mathcal{l}
is continuous.

Proposition 10.1
.

Assume that
\ell_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty),i\in{\mathpzc K}
are continuous non-decreasing functions such that
\ell_{i}(0)=0
and
\lim_{s\to\infty}\ell_{i}(s)=\infty,i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

(a)

There is a collection of nondecreasing continuous functions
t_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty),i\in{\mathpzc K}
such that (
10.1
) is satisfied.

(b)

If, additionally,
\ell_{i}
s do not have common levels of constancy,
t_{i}
s are determined uniquely.
Furthermore, the function
\mathcal{l}
introduced above coincides with
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and
t_{i}
s are represented as follows:

t_{i}(s)=\mathcal{k}_{i}\circ\mathcal{l}(s)+1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{(\vartriangle\mathcal{k}_{i})\circ\mathcal{l}(s)>0}(s-\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{l}(s)),\quad s\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

(10.2)

where
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}_{i}
is the jump of
\mathcal{k}_{i},
that is,
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)=\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)-\mathcal{k}_{i}(u-),u\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
.

Before presenting the proof, we remark that
(
10.2
) is a counterpart of a formula obtained originally in

[
6
]
. However, differences between these two are worth spelling out here. First of all, on the technical side, in
[
6
]
the role of
\mathcal{k}_{i}
s is played by the
càdlàg  inverses of
\ell_{i}
s defined as
\ell^{\leftarrow}(t)\coloneqq\inf\{s\geq 0\colon l_{i}(s)\geq t\},t\geq 0
. More importantly, the counterpart of (
10.2
), as obtained in
[
6
]
applies merely to the case of Brownian motion and has been proved by stochastic means. Formula (
10.2
), on the contrary, is of purely deterministic nature, and applies to paths of all processes that satisfy the assumptions of the proposition.

Proof of Proposition
10.1
.

Throughout the proof, we say that a sequence of functions converges locally uniformly if the convergence is uniform in compact subintervals of
[0,\infty)
.

(a)
Suppose first that
\ell_{i}
s are strictly increasing. Then the generalized inverses
\mathcal{k}_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
become the usual inverses, and as such are continuous; also, we obviously have
\mathcal{k}_{i}\circ\ell_{i}(s)=\ell_{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{i}(s)=s,s\geq 0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
. It follows that
\mathcal{k}
and
\mathcal{l}
are continuous and strictly increasing and that
\mathcal{l}\circ\mathcal{k}(s)=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{l}(s)=s,s\geq 0
. Hence, introducing

t_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{k}_{i}\circ\mathcal{l},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}

(this is a very particular case of (
10.2
)), we see that
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
does not depend on
i
, being equal to
\mathcal{l}
. Since, at the same same time
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(s)=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{i}\circ\mathcal{l}(s)=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{l}(s)=s,s\geq 0
, we see that these
t_{i}
s satisfy conditions (
10.1
).

In the general case we approximate each
\ell_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
by a sequence of strictly increasing functions, say,
\left(\ell_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}
. For example, we may take
\ell_{n,i}(t)

\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}t+\ell_{i}(t),t\geq 0,n\geq 1
, so that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\ell_{n,i}=\ell_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
locally uniformly. Then, for each
n\geq 1
, there is a collection
t_{n,i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
of nondecreasing continuous functions satisfying (
10.1
) with
\ell_{i}
replaced by
\ell_{n,i}
. Arguing as in Lemma
8.3
we check that, for each integer
m\geq 1
and
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, elements of the sequence
\left(t_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}
form a pre-compact set in
C[0,m]
. Hence, we can choose a subsequence of this sequence that converges in
C[0,1]
, and from that one, a subsequence that converges in
C[0,2]
, etc. Therefore, the diagonal argument shows that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, there is a subsequence of
\left(t_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}

that converges to a function
t_{i}
locally uniformly. Moreover, it is rather easy to see that this subsequence can be chosen in such a way that
\left(t_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}
converges locally uniformly for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, that is, that the subsequence is the same for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Then, since
\ell_{n,i}\circ t_{n,i}
does not depend on
i
, we can see immediately that neither does
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
, and similarly that
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}t_{i}(s)=s,s\geq 0
. This, however, means that we have found
t_{i}
s that satisfy (
10.1
), completing the proof of (a).

(b)
By assumption,
\ell_{i}
s do not have common levels of constancy; thus, Lemma
6.2
guarantees that the collection of functions
t_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
satisfying (
10.1
) obtained in (a) is unique, and thus we can restrict ourselves to proving the rest of (b).

Before continuing, let us recapitulate. We know from (a) that there are strictly increasing functions
\ell_{n,i},n\geq 1,i\in{\mathpzc K}
(for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
,
\left(\ell_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}
is a subsequence of the sequence denoted
\left(\ell_{n,i}\right)_{n\geq 1}
in point (a)),
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\ell_{n,i}=\ell_{i}
locally uniformly, and simultaneously
t_{i}=\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n,i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
in the same way, where
t_{n,i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
satisfy (
10.1
) with
\ell_{i}
replaced by
\ell_{n,i}
. In particular, introducing
\mathcal{l}_{n}
as a common value of
\ell_{n,i}\circ t_{n,i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, that is,

\mathcal{l}_{n}\coloneqq\ell_{n,i}\circ t_{n,i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},

we see that
\mathcal{l}_{n}
s converge locally uniformly to a function, say,
\mathcal{l}_{\infty}
. This function is a common value of
\ell_{i}\circ t_{i}
,

\mathcal{l}_{\infty}\coloneqq\ell_{i}\circ t_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},

but we would like to identify it with
\mathcal{l}
— the function introduced above as the generalized inverse of
\mathcal{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{i}
. This is the first of our two goals (the other one being formula (
10.2
)).

Goal no. 1.
It is the biggest obstacle on our way that the operation of taking the generalized inverse is not continuous in the metric of the Skorokhod space used in this paper so far, and neither is the operation of summation.
Fortunately, the metric used so far (the most popular one, see e.g.
[
9
]
, known also as
J_{1}
) is not the only one at our disposal: in fact, there are at least four natural, related but not identical, topologies in this space, introduced by Skorokhod himself. In particular, Theorem 13.6.3 in
[
66
]
says that a sequence of monotone functions in
D((0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
converges in the so-called
M_{1}
metric iff so does the sequence of their generalized inverses. This convergence also is implied by a pointwise convergence of the functions on a dense subset of
(0,\infty)
, and implies such pointwise convergence. As applied to our scenario, the theorem declares thus that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the generalized inverses
\mathcal{k}_{n,i}
of restrictions of
\ell_{n,i}
to
(0,\infty)
converge to the generalized inverse of
\ell_{i}
in the sense of
M_{1}
in
D((0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
; for,
\ell_{n,i}
converge locally uniformly to
\ell_{i}
and thus also in the sense of
M_{1}
. For future use we record this information as

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{k}_{n,i}\overset{M_{1}}{=}\mathcal{k}_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

(10.3)

To continue, we recall that whereas, as we have just seen,
M_{1}
topology has nice properties related to the operation of taking inverse, the so-called
SM_{1}
topology (i.e., strong
M_{1}
topology) has nice properties related to Cartesian products. Elaborating on this succinct statement, given functions
f\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{i}
and
g\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{j}
for some integers
i
and
j
, we will write
(f,g)
to denote the function
t\mapsto(f(t),g(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{i+j}
. In this notation, Theorem 12.6.1 in
[
66
]
says that if a sequence, say,
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{i})
converges to an
f
in the sense of
SM_{1}
, and a sequence
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{j})
converges to a
g
in the sense of
SM_{1}
, and, additionally,
f
and
g
do not have common points of discontinuity (i.e., there is no argument
t
such that both
f
and
g
are discontinuous at
t
), then
(f_{n},g_{n})
s converge to
(f,g)
in the sense of
SM_{1}
in
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{i+j})
.
(We note on the side that the Whitt book speaks of convergence of members of Skorokhod spaces of functions defined on a finite interval, not on
[0,\infty)
or
(0,\infty)
. However, the argument in the case of our interest is analogous, see §12.9 in
[
66
]
.)

Lucky for us,
SM_{1}
has further nice properties. First of all, as explained on p. 394 in
[
66
]
, even though in general topologies
M_{1}
and
SM_{1}
differ, in the case of
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
they coincide. Secondly, in
SM_{1}
the operation of summation is, in a limited sense, continuous. To be more explicit: Corollary 12.7.1 in
[
66
]
says that if, for some integer
i
, two sequences, say,
\left(f_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
and
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
, of elements of
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{i})
converge to
f
and
g
, respectively, in the metric of
SM_{1}
, and, additionally,
f
and
g
do not have common points of discontinuity, then the sequence
\left(f_{n}+g_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
converges to
f+g
in the same metric.

These three results allow us to argue as follows. First of all, we note that convergence in (
10.3
) can be replaced by that in the sense of
SM_{1}
. Then, using Theorem 12.6.1 in
[
66
]
a couple of times, we conclude that the functions
t\mapsto(\mathcal{k}_{n,i}(t))_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}
converge, as
n\to\infty
, to the function
t\mapsto(\mathcal{k}_{i}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathpzc k}
in the sense of
SM_{1}
metric; the theorem is applicable because, by assumption,
\ell_{i}
s do not have common points of constancy, and so
\mathcal{k}_{i}
s do not have common points of discontinuity. By the same token, Corollary 12.7.1 of
[
66
]
can be used to see that

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{n,i}
converge to
\mathcal{k}=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{i}
in the
SM_{1}
metric of
D({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(}0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
, or, which is the same, in the
M_{1}
metric of this space. But then, by Theorem 13.6.3 in
[
66
]
, the generalized inverses of
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{n,i}
converge to the generalized inverse of
\mathcal{k}
, that is, to
\mathcal{l}
. But
\mathcal{l}_{n}
s are defined as the usual inverses of
\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathcal{k}_{n,i}
(see part (a)), and we know that
\mathcal{l}_{\infty}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{l}_{n}
locally uniformly. Since all the functions involved here are
càdlàg, this completes the proof of the fact that (on the entire half-line)

\mathcal{l}=\mathcal{l}_{\infty}=\ell_{i}\circ t_{i},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K}.

Goal no. 2.
We are left with showing (
10.2
). All we know so far about
t_{i}
s is that they are locally uniform limits of
t_{n,i}
s, that the latter satisfy

t_{n,i}=\mathcal{k}_{n,i}\circ\mathcal{l}_{n},\qquad i\in{\mathpzc K},n\geq 1,

and that
\mathcal{l}_{n}
s converge locally uniformly to
\mathcal{l}_{\infty}=\mathcal{l}
.

To proceed, we need the following real analysis result concerning generalized inverses. Suppose that nondecreasing functions
f_{n}
converge locally uniformly to a function
f
and that a
u\geq 0
is not a level of constancy of
f
, that is, that
u
is a point of continuity of
f^{-1}
; suppose furthermore that nonnegative numbers
u_{n}
converge to
u
. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(u_{n})=f^{-1}(u)
.
Indeed, in such circumstances, since
f^{-1}
is continuous at
u
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that

f(f^{-1}(u)-\varepsilon)\leq u-\delta<u<u+\delta\leq f(f^{-1}(u)+\varepsilon).

Therefore

\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(f^{-1}(u)-\varepsilon)\leq u-\delta<u<u+\delta\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(f^{-1}(u)+\varepsilon),

and consequently

f^{-1}(u)-\varepsilon\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(u-\delta)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(u+\delta)\leq f^{-1}(u)+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, monotonicity of
f_{n}^{-1}
implies thus that we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(u_{n})=f^{-1}(u)
as long as
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=u.
.

Armed with this knowledge, fix an
i\in{\mathpzc K}
and take an
s\geq 0
such that
u\coloneqq\mathcal{l}(s)
is a point of continuity of
\mathcal{k}_{i}
, that is, not a level of constancy of
\ell_{i}
. Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{l}_{n}(s)=\mathcal{l}(s)
, the result proven above implies that
t_{i}(s)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{k}_{n,i}(\mathcal{l}_{n}(s))=\mathcal{k}_{i}(\mathcal{l}(s))
and thus establishes (
10.2
) in this case.

It remains therefore to treat the case where
u\coloneqq\mathcal{l}(s)
is not a point of continuity of
\mathcal{k}_{i}
, that is, we have
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)>0
(
i
is still fixed).

In such a scenario,
u
is a level of constancy of
\ell_{i}
and thus not a level of constancy of
\ell_{j},j\not=i
. It follows that
u
is a point of continuity of
\mathcal{k}_{j},j\not=i
and we conclude that
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}(u)=\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)
. This means that at
u
both
\mathcal{k}
and
\mathcal{k}_{i}
have a jump of value, and the size of the jump is in both cases the same.
For simplicity of notation, let us write simply
\vartriangle
instead of
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}(u)
and
\vartriangle\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)
.

Since
\mathcal{k}
has a jump at
u
of size
\vartriangle
and
\mathcal{l}
is the generalized inverse of
\mathcal{k}
,
\mathcal{l}
remains constant at the level
u
in a time interval of length
\vartriangle
; moreover to the left of this interval
\mathcal{l}(v)
is smaller than
s
, and larger than
s
to the right of this interval. We record this fact as follows

\mathcal{l}(v)\begin{cases}<u,&v<v^{\flat},\\
=u,&v\in[v^{\flat},v^{\flat}+\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle],\\
>u,&v>v^{\flat},\end{cases}

(10.4)

where
v^{\flat}\coloneqq\inf\{v\geq 0\colon\mathcal{l}(v)=u\}
.
Similarly, since
\mathcal{k}_{i}
is the inverse of
\ell_{i}
,

\ell_{i}(v)\begin{cases}<u,&v<v^{\sharp},\\
=u,&v\in[v^{\sharp},v^{\sharp}+\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle],\\
>u,&v>v^{\sharp},\end{cases}

(10.5)

where
v^{\sharp}\coloneqq\inf\{v\geq 0\colon\ell_{i}(v)=u\}
.

So prepared, let us consider a sequence
\left(v_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of reals smaller than but converging to
v^{\flat}
and chosen in such a way that each
v_{n}
is a point of continuity of
\mathcal{k}_{i}
. Then, by the already established part,

t_{i}(v_{n})=\mathcal{k}_{i}(\mathcal{l}(v_{n})),\qquad n\geq 1.

Since
t_{i}
is continuous, the left-hand side above converges to
t_{i}(v^{\flat})
. At the same time,
since
\mathcal{l}
is continuous,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{l}(v_{n})=\mathcal{l}(v^{\flat})=u
and so, since
\mathcal{k}_{i}
has left limits, the right-hand side converges to
\mathcal{k}_{i}(u-)
, which, by (
10.5
) equals
v^{\sharp}
. This shows that
t_{i}(v^{\flat})=v^{\sharp}
, that is, that
v^{\flat}
is the time when
t_{i}
reaches the level of constancy of
\ell_{i}
visible in (
10.5
).

Lemma
8.5
(a) says thus that, in the interval
[v^{\flat},v^{\flat}+\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle]
,
t_{i}
grows linearly with slope
1
:

t_{i}(v)=v^{\sharp}+v-v^{\flat}

(whereas the other
t_{j}
s stay constant).
Next, since
\mathcal{l}(s)=u
, (
10.4
) shows that
s\in[v^{\flat},v^{\flat}+\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle]
, and so the above formula applies to
v=s
. Formula (
10.4
) shows also that
\mathcal{k}(u)=v^{\flat}+\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle
. Thus, recalling that
v^{\sharp}=\mathcal{k}_{i}(u-)=\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)-\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle
, we finally see that

\displaystyle t_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)-\hskip-2.84544pt\vartriangle+s-v^{\flat}=\mathcal{k}_{i}(u)+s-\mathcal{k}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}_{i}\circ\ell_{i}(s)+s-\mathcal{k}\circ\ell_{i}(s).

This completes the proof. ∎

As a corollary to this proposition, we see that the time-change processes
T_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
spoken of in Theorem
8.1
are given by a counterpart of our formula (
10.2
) (almost surely). This is simply because, as discussed in the proof of the theorem, paths of processes

U_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{\circ}\mathcal{L}_{i}
do not have common levels of constancy (almost surely).

11.
Synopsis

11.1.
The analytical part

The analytical part of the paper can be summarized as follows. Let
\mathfrak{C}(S)
be the space of continuous functions on the natural
{\mathpzc k}
-point compactification
S
of the infinite star graph with
{\mathpzc k}
edges and center at
\mathfrak{0}
(see Section
3
for details). Also, let
\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
be the space of twice continuously differentiable functions on
S
, and
\Delta
be the Laplace operator given by
\Delta f=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
on
\mathfrak{C}^{2}(S)
. Our point of departure is the fact that for any Feller generator, say,
\mathfrak{G}
, in
\mathfrak{C}(S)
that is a restriction of
\Delta
, there are non-negative constants
\alpha,\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, and
\gamma
, and a (possibly infinite) positive Borel measure
\mathpzc m
on
S_{\mathfrak{0}}\coloneqq S\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}\}
such that
\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}(1\wedge x)\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
, at least one of the conditions
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
and
\mathpzc m(S)=\infty
holds, and

{\tfrac{\alpha}{2}}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})-\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}).

(11.1)

In our search for a converse, we exclude the possibility for
\mathpzc m
to have a mass at any of the points at infinity, because it allows the related process to jump from
\mathfrak{0}
to one of those points never to come back to the regular state-space, and such a behavior is uninteresting. Under this additional assumption we prove that for any set of parameters
\alpha,\beta=(\beta_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
,
\gamma
and
\mathpzc m
described above, the operator
\Delta
as restricted to the space of
f
such that (
11.1
) holds, is a Feller generator.

As in the seminal paper of L.C.G. Rogers
[
60
]
, the resolvent of this generator can be expressed by means of the resolvent of the minimal Brownian motion and the parameters described above — see (
7.9
). Formula (
7.9
) reduces to (
3.9
) when
\mathpzc m=0
and has an alternative form (
6.7
) when
\mathpzc m
is nonzero but finite. Moreover, it informs of continuous dependence of the underlying processes on the quadruple of parameters. In the degenerate case where
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
and yet
\mathpzc m(S)<\infty
, (
7.9
) is not a Feller resolvent, but when restricted to a subspace of
\mathfrak{C}(S)
gives rise to a Feller-like semigroup; in the related process, however, the graph center does not belong to the state-space — see Section
7.5
.

11.2.
The stochastic part

Stochastic analysis part commences with the description of the all-important Walsh’s spider process, corresponding to the case where, in the boundary condition above,
\alpha=\gamma=0
,
\mathpzc m
is zero measure, and all
\beta_{i}
s are positive and add up to
1
. In Section
4
we recall two of this process’ characterizations, due to Barlow, Pitman and Yor
[
4
]
. Also, following the recent work by Bayraktar et al.
[
6
]
, we describe the process by means of a multi-dimensional time-change.

In Section
5
we show that by slowing down the Walsh’s process at the point
\mathfrak{0}
, see (
5.15
), we obtain a new Feller process, the process generated by the operator with boundary condition (
11.1
) featuring nonzero
\alpha
(and still with
\gamma=0
and
\mathpzc m=0
). Moreover,
the so-obtained process can be further modified by requiring that it is killed when its local time at
\mathfrak{0}
reaches the level of an independent exponential random variable, see (
5.16
). Such a modified process still enjoys the Feller property; in fact, it is generated by the Laplace operator with boundary condition (
7.9
) where both
\alpha
and
\gamma
positive (
\mathpzc m
is still zero). Interestingly, if proper care is exercised, the order in which we slow down and kill can be reversed without changing the end result.

In Section
6
we turn to the case where
\mathpzc m
is nonzero but finite, so that it is a scalar multiple of a probability measure
\mu
:
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
. We show that the related process can be constructed as a concatenation of the processes of the type considered in Section
5
: when the process of the latter type is killed, the new process with certain probability restarts at a random point distributed according to the measure
\mu
. Theorem
6.1
provides a description of the process corresponding to
\alpha=\gamma=0
and
\mathpzc m=\delta\mu
(with the standing assumption that
\beta_{i}
s are positive and add up to
1
) by means of (a) a single
{\mathpzc k}
-dimensional Brownian motion, (b) a family of subordinators build of
\beta_{i}
s and a compound Poisson process, and (c) a multi-parameter time-change. Notably, the compound Poisson process is a function of
\mathpzc m
, for
\delta
is its intensity and
\mu
is the distribution of its jumps. In the limit case where
\mathpzc m
vanishes, Theorem
6.1
reduces to the result of Bayraktar et al. cited above. The full case of nonzero but finite
\mathpzc m
is obtained by scaling parameters if necessary (see (
6.19
)) and then slowing down and/or killing the process of Theorem
6.1
. Degenerate scenarios are described in Section
6.9
.

The main result of Section
8
, Theorem
8.1
, extends Theorem
6.1
to the situation where
\mathpzc m
is infinite. The crucial difference is that in these new circumstances jumps of the underlying process are not modeled by means of a compound Poisson process but rather by a Poisson point process with mean measure
\,\mathrm{d}t\times\mathpzc m
. Nevertheless, the theorem remains in nature the same as Theorem
6.1
: it builds the searched-for process of a standard Brownian motion, a family of subordinators and a multi-dimensional time-change. Theorem
8.1
, with formulae (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), can be thought of as the climax of the paper and of its stochastic part in particular. Interestingly, this multi-dimensional time-change can be given rather explicitly — see formula (
10.2
).

In this context, Theorem
9.1
that provides an explicit form of the
\lambda
-potential of the local time of the process with boundary condition (
11.1
), is rightfully viewed as a corollary to the main results of the analytic and stochastic parts of the paper.

12.
Appendix

In this appendix we prove two results taken for granted in the main body of the paper.

12.1.
Existence of constants
\alpha,\beta_{i},\gamma
and measure
\mathpzc m
such that (
3.2
) holds

This section is devoted to the proof of the proposition stated in Section
3.1
, saying that for each Feller generator
\mathfrak{G}
that is a restriction of
\Delta
there are constants
\alpha,\beta_{i},i\in{\mathpzc K}
and
\gamma
and a measure
\mathpzc m
such that the domain of
\mathfrak{G}
is characterized by relation (
3.2
).

To begin the proof we note that, by assumption,

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\lim_{t\to 0+}t^{-1}[T(t)f(\mathfrak{0})-f(\mathfrak{0})],\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}).

(12.1)

Moreover,
f\mapsto T(t)f(\mathfrak{0})
being a positive functional on
\mathfrak{C}(S)
of norm not exceeding one, there is a Borel sub-probability measure
\mathsf{n}_{t}
such that
T(t)f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathsf{n}_{t}
and thus we have

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\lim_{t\to 0+}\left[t^{-1}\int_{S}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\mathsf{n}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)-a_{t}\,f(\mathfrak{0})\right],

where

a_{t}\coloneqq t^{-1}(1-\mathsf{n}_{t}(S))\geq 0,\qquad t>0.

(We note that the measures
\mathsf{n}_{t},t>0
cannot be all equal to zero, because by assumption
\lim_{t\to 0+}T(t)f(\mathfrak{0})=f(\mathfrak{0}).
) Introducing

g=(g_{i})_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}
with
g_{i}(x)\coloneqq\frac{f_{i}(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}},x>0
and recalling that
\mathfrak{G}
is a restriction of
\Delta
,
we see that this function extends to a member of
\mathfrak{C}(\widetilde{S})
(see (
3.1
)) with
g_{i}(0)=f_{i}^{\prime}(0),i\in{\mathpzc K}
. In terms of
g
, our relation becomes

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\lim_{t\to 0+}\left[t^{-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\int_{[0,\infty]}g_{i}(x)(1-\mathrm{e}^{-x})\mathsf{n}_{t,i}(\mathrm{d}x)-\alpha_{t}\,f(\mathfrak{0})\right],

where
\mathsf{n}_{t,i}
is the restriction of
\mathsf{n}_{t}
to the
i
th edge, as identified with
[0,\infty]
; note that all integrands in the display above vanish at
x=0
.

Next, we turn our attention to the (non-zero) positive measures on
[0,\infty]
, defined by

\nu_{t,i}(\mathrm{d}x)\coloneqq t^{-1}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-x})\mathsf{n}_{t,i}(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad t>0,i\in{\mathpzc K},

and write

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\lim_{t\to 0+}\left[\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\|\nu_{t,i}\|\int_{[0,\infty]}g(x)\|\nu_{t,i}\|^{-1}\nu_{t,i}(\mathrm{d}x)-a_{t}f(\mathfrak{0})\right],

(12.2)

where
\|\nu_{t,i}\|=\nu_{t,i}([0,\infty]),t>0,i\in{\mathpzc K}
.
By Helly’s principle, there is a sequence
\left(t_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of positive numbers converging to
0
such that, for each
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the associated probability measures

\|\nu_{t_{n},i}\|^{-1}\nu_{t_{n},i}

converge weakly to a probability measure on
[0,\infty]
, say,
\nu_{i}
.

By (
12.2
), it is amply clear that further analysis depends on convergence of sequences
\left(a_{t_{n}}\right)_{n\geq 1}
and
\left(\|\nu_{t_{n},i}\|\right)_{n\geq 1},i\in{\mathpzc K}
. However, they are composed of non-negative numbers, and thus passing to subsequences if necessary, we obtain the existence of the possibly infinite limits

a\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{t_{n}},\quad b_{i}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\nu_{t_{n},i}\|,i\in{\mathpzc K}\quad\text{and}\quad c_{i}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{t_{n}}}{\|\nu_{t_{n},i}\|},i\in{\mathpzc K},

and are naturally led to the following two cases.

Case 1.
a
and
b_{i}
s are finite.
In this scenario, (
12.2
) yields

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}b_{i}\int_{[0,\infty]}g_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{i}-af(\mathfrak{0}),

that is

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}b_{i}\nu_{i}(\{0\})f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\int_{(0,\infty]}[f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(0)]\mathpzc m_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)-af(\mathfrak{0}),

where
\mathpzc m_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)\coloneqq\frac{b_{i}\nu_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}},i\in{\mathpzc K}
, so that
\int_{(0,\infty]}[1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}]\mathpzc m_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)\leq b_{i}<\infty
. This is (
3.2
) with

\alpha\coloneqq 1,\quad\beta_{i}\coloneqq b_{i}\nu_{i}(\{0\}),i\in{\mathpzc K},\quad\gamma\coloneqq a,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathpzc m\coloneqq\textstyle\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathpzc m_{i},

where
\mathpzc m_{i}
s are now seen as measures on
S
, that is, each
\mathpzc m_{i}
, by definition a measure on
[0,\infty]
, is identified with its image via the map
x\mapsto(i,x)\in S
; the latter is a measure on
S
supported on the
i
th edge.

Case 2. At least one of
a
and
b_{i}
s is infinite.
In this case, by passing to further subsequences if necessary, we can find an index
i_{0}\in{\mathpzc K}
such that
d_{i}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|\nu_{t_{n},i}\|}{\|\nu_{t_{n},i_{0}}\|}
exists and is finite for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. Dividing (
12.2
) (with
t
replaced by
t_{n}
) by
a_{t_{n}}+\|\nu_{t_{n},i_{0}}\|
, and letting
n\to\infty
, yields

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\frac{d_{i}}{1+c_{i_{0}}}\int_{[0,\infty]}g_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{i}=\frac{c_{i_{0}}}{1+c_{i_{0}}}f(\mathfrak{0}),\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G}),

(12.3)

provided that
c_{i_{0}}\not=\infty
. This again can be rewritten as

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\frac{d_{i}\nu_{i}(\{0\})}{1+c_{i_{0}}}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\int_{(0,\infty]}[f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(0)]\mathpzc m_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{c_{i_{0}}}{1+c_{i_{0}}}f(\mathfrak{0}),

where this time
\mathpzc m_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)\coloneqq\frac{d_{i}}{1+c_{i_{0}}}\frac{\nu_{i}(\mathrm{d}x)}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}},i\in{\mathpzc K}
.
In other words, we obtain (
3.2
) with

\alpha\coloneqq 0,\quad\beta_{i}\coloneqq\frac{d_{i}\nu_{i}(\{0\})}{1+c_{i_{0}}},i\in{\mathpzc K},\quad\gamma\coloneqq\frac{c_{i_{0}}}{1+c_{i_{0}}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathpzc m\coloneqq\textstyle\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\mathpzc m_{i}.

Finally, we note that
c_{i_{0}}
cannot be infinite. Indeed, otherwise (
12.3
) would turn into

f(\mathfrak{0})=0,f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
.
This is clearly impossible, because
\mathfrak{G}
, being a generator, is densely defined.

We are hence left with explaining why at least one of the conditions
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}>0
and
\mathpzc m(S)=\infty
must hold. To this end, assume that
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
and yet
\mathpzc m(S)<\infty
. Then (
3.2
) turns into

(\gamma+\delta)f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S_{0}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m,\qquad f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})

where
\delta\coloneqq\mathpzc m(S_{0})
. Now, if
\gamma+\delta>0
, this relation shows that
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
is contained in the kernel of the non-zero bounded linear functional
f\mapsto(\gamma+\delta)f(\mathfrak{0})-\int_{S_{0}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m
, and thus
\mathfrak{G}
cannot be a generator, since it is not densely defined; a contradiction. Moreover, if
\gamma+\delta=0
, (
3.2
) becomes
0=0
. This means that
\mathfrak{G}
coincides with
\Delta
, and here also we have the desired contradiction because
\Delta
cannot be a generator, its resolvent equation having too many solutions.

12.2.
The positive-maximum principle for
\Delta
with boundary condition (
3.2
)

In this section, we prove that for any constants
\alpha,\beta_{i},\gamma
and a measure
\mathpzc m
that satisfy the properties listed in Section
3.1
, the operator
\Delta
, as restricted to the domain where (
3.2
) holds, satisfies the positive-maximum principle.

First of all, if a positive maximum of an
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{G})
is attained at an
x\not=\mathfrak{0}
, the claim is obvious by the classical theorem of analysis. To treat the case where the maximum is attained at
\mathfrak{0}
, in turn, we consider three sub-cases.

(a)
Assume first that
\alpha\not=0
. Then

\mathfrak{G}f(\mathfrak{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{0})=\tfrac{1}{\alpha}(\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)-\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})+\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\mathpzc m)\leq 0

because
f^{\prime}_{i}(0)\leq 0
for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
, the coefficients are nonnegative, and
f(\mathfrak{0})
is both nonnegative and no smaller than any other value of
f
.

(b)
Next, assume that
\alpha=0
, but at least one of
\beta_{i}
s is positive. Then, on the one hand,

\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}f^{\prime}_{i}(0)=\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})+\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(\mathfrak{0})-f(x)]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)\geq 0,

and, on the other, as before,
f^{\prime}_{i}(0)\leq 0
for all
i\in{\mathpzc K}
. It follows that for all
i
such that
\beta_{i}>0
we have
f_{i}^{\prime}(0)=0
, and by assumption at least one such
i
exists.
But then the even extension of
f_{i}
to the whole of
\mathbb{R}
is twice continuously differentiable and, since its maximum is attained at
x=0
, this yields
f^{\prime\prime}_{i}(0)\leq 0
. Moreover, by the definition of
\Delta
,

f^{\prime\prime}_{i}(0)
does not depend on
i
and coincides with
2\mathfrak{G}f(\mathfrak{0})
. This completes the proof in this case.

(c)
Finally, assume that
\alpha+\sum_{i\in{\mathpzc K}}\beta_{i}=0
so that
\mathpzc m
is necessarily infinite. In such a scenario, (
3.2
) reduces to

\gamma f(\mathfrak{0})=\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}[f(x)-f(\mathfrak{0})]\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x).

Since by assumption the left-hand side is nonnegative whereas the right-hand side is nonpositive, this relation shows that both expressions are zero, and in particular
f(x)
equals
f(\mathfrak{0})
for all
x
except on a set of
\mathpzc m
measure
0
.

Next, let
\mathcal{C}
be the support of
\mathpzc m
, that is, let

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\{x\in S_{\mathfrak{0}}\colon\text{for any ball $B$ containing $x$, $\mathpzc m(B)>0$}\}.

We claim that

\updelta\coloneqq\inf_{x\in\mathcal{C}}d(x,\mathfrak{0}),

where
d
is the metric in
S
discussed in Section
7.4
, is zero. Indeed, otherwise the open ball, say,
B_{\updelta}
, with center at
\mathfrak{0}
and radius
\updelta
, is of
\mathpzc m
measure
0
, and, moreover, since
1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{B_{\updelta}^{\complement}}\coloneqq 1\!\!\,{\rm I}_{\{d(x,\mathfrak{0})\geq\updelta\}}\leq\frac{1}{\min(\updelta,1)}(1\wedge d(x,\mathfrak{0}))
,

\mathpzc m(B_{\updelta}^{\complement})\leq\tfrac{1}{\min(\updelta,1)}\int_{S_{\mathfrak{0}}}1\wedge d(x,\mathfrak{0})\,\mathpzc m(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty.

Since this contradicts our assumption that
\mathpzc m
is infinite, the claim is established.

As a result, there is a sequence
\left(x_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}d(x_{n},\mathfrak{0})=0
. Choosing a subsequence if necessary, we can also assume that all
x_{n}
s belong to one edge, say the
i
th. But, for each
x\in\mathcal{C}
,
f(x)=f(\mathfrak{0})
as established above, and this implies that
f_{i}^{\prime}(0)=0
. As in the case (b) we can thus argue that
f^{\prime\prime}_{i}(0)\leq 0
and so
\mathfrak{G}f(\mathfrak{0})\leq 0
, completing the proof.
